# Classical arithmetic, sequences, polynomials and reciprocity

Blueprint for the roadmap `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion`, job `BP-ClassicalArithmeticCompletion` (issue #1025).
Packet: `research/blueprint/packets/ClassicalArithmeticCompletion.json`. Suggested Lean file:
`research/blueprint/suggested/ClassicalArithmeticCompletion.lean`. Handoff:
`research/blueprint/handoff/BP-ClassicalArithmeticCompletion.md`.

This is the second pass. The first (#2837) planned ten nodes and left CA.5 without mathematics, CA.6 and CA.7 empty
and most of CA.1 to CA.4 as remaining work, for want of sources. This pass keeps those ten nodes, each re-checked
and corrected where needed, and plans the rest from free sources, under the accepted restructuring RS-03 and with
the two maintainer-added sources covered: Bennett–Siksek, *A conjecture of Erdős, supersingular primes and short
character sums* (items 72, 152 and 81), and Martin, *A new proof of Chen's theorem for Markoff graphs* (items 1–13).

**Status: partial.** CA.0 is closed; CA.4 and CA.5 are source decomposed; CA.1, CA.2, CA.3, CA.6 and CA.7 are
partial, each with a precise `remaining` list in the packet's coverage record. The packet has 312 nodes,
501 API items and 279 unit tests, cites 579 declarations of the pinned libraries,
records 50 mistakes in its sources, 8 gaps and 19 requests to other roadmaps.

Pinned baseline: Mathlib `082e2d37e8b0463410cdb532e111cd43d5a66174`, Tau Ceti `f790474821cf4256814db967cb154e7af3d0c369`.

| Layer | Status | Nodes | Planets |
|---|---|---|---|
| CA.0 | closed | 1 | — |
| CA.1 | partial | 34 | Units modulo a power of two; Quadratic character of a square class; Power residue symbol; Power reciprocity law; Cubic reciprocity; Eisenstein reciprocity |
| CA.2 | partial | 48 | Minimal polynomial of a sequence; Rational generating functions; Lucas sequences; Euler numbers; Christol's theorem; Farey sequences |
| CA.3 | partial | 37 | Smith normal form; Hermite normal form; Rational canonical form; Pólya's theorem on integer-valued polynomials; Capelli's theorem; Dumas's theorem on Newton polygons |
| CA.4 | source decomposed | 74 | Integer linear systems; Markoff tree; Legendre's three-square theorem; Jacobi's four-square theorem; Ramanujan–Nagell equation; Numerical semigroup |
| CA.5 | source decomposed | 18 | Class group generated by small primes; Class group from a relation matrix; Class number and regulator certificate; Hermite matrix of an ideal; Discriminant and index of a sublattice; Pell solutions as norm-one units |
| CA.6 | partial | 45 | Pisot number; Salem number; Lehmer's number; Smyth's theorem on nonreciprocal algebraic integers; Dobrowolski's lower bound; Schinzel–Zassenhaus conjecture (Dimitrov) |
| CA.7 | partial | 55 | Ring of integers as a Galois module; Normal integral basis; Noether's theorem; Hilbert–Speiser theorem; Locally free class group; Taylor's theorem (Fröhlich's conjecture) |

## RS-03 and what this roadmap owns

The accepted restructuring RS-03 is binding here. It keeps CA.0, CA.2, CA.4 and CA.7 as they stand and narrows four
layers:

- **CA.1** to higher reciprocity and the power-residue and Hilbert-symbol extensions, with the place 2, the infinite
  places and ramification conventions. Quadratic reciprocity and Gauss and Jacobi sums are imported from Mathlib and
  compared explicitly. `FiniteFieldsAndCharacterSums:FF.1` owns the finite-field character normalisations.
- **CA.3** to the general Smith, Hermite and rational canonical normal forms with certificates, and the
  integer-valued-polynomial and irreducibility refinements the audit does not find. Resultants and discriminants stay
  imported.
- **CA.5** to comparisons between presentations and intrinsic invariants, and the reading of certified outputs.
  `ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.2` owns the certified algorithms. The first pass proposed removing CA.5 as a process
  layer; that proposal contradicted RS-03 and is withdrawn.
- **CA.6** to Pisot and Salem numbers, the scoped lower bounds and the height comparisons. The Mahler measure of an
  algebraic number, the house and the height comparisons are `DiophantineApproximationAndTranscendence:DT.0`'s nodes,
  cited here, and Lehmer's problem stays a conjecture.

Everything the reviewed library audit (AUDIT-18) finds in the pinned libraries is a baseline citation, listed in
each layer's section under "already provided", never a node.

Every source is freely available and was opened and read; the sections read, and the SHA-256 of each file, are in
the packet.

## Layers

## CA.0 Divisibility and multiplicative arithmetic

This layer is the elementary arithmetic of ℤ and ℕ on which the rest of the roadmap stands. It is
built: the pinned Mathlib contains every object and theorem the layer names, and they are cited, not
restated. The layer contributes one statement that the library leaves unstated, the existence half
of the Chinese remainder theorem at non-coprime moduli over the integers.

### What is already built

- **Greatest common divisors and Bézout.** `Nat.xgcd` is the computable extended Euclidean algorithm;
  Bézout's identity is `Nat.gcd_eq_gcd_ab` over ℕ and `Int.gcd_eq_gcd_ab` over ℤ; greatest common
  divisors in a Euclidean domain are `EuclideanDomain.gcd`.
- **Unique factorisation.** `UniqueFactorizationMonoid`, with every principal ideal domain an
  instance; for ℕ, `Nat.primeFactorsList_unique` and the finitely supported factorisation
  `Nat.factorization`.
- **Congruences and residue rings.** `Nat.ModEq`, `Int.ModEq`, the rings `ZMod n`, and Euler's theorem
  `ZMod.pow_totient`.
- **The Chinese remainder theorem.** For coprime moduli, `Nat.chineseRemainder` (computable) and the
  ring isomorphism `ZMod.chineseRemainder : ZMod (m n) ≃+* ZMod m × ZMod n`. At non-coprime moduli over
  ℕ, `Nat.chineseRemainder'` takes exactly the compatibility hypothesis `a ≡ b [MOD gcd n m]` and
  returns a solution below `lcm n m` (`Nat.chineseRemainder'_lt_lcm`). Uniqueness modulo the least
  common multiple holds over ℤ for all moduli: `Int.modEq_and_modEq_iff_modEq_lcm`.
- **Valuations.** `padicValNat`, the `p`-part of `Nat.factorization`, and Legendre's formula in
  digit-sum form, `sub_one_mul_padicValNat_factorial`: `(p − 1) · v_p(n!) = n − s_p(n)`.
- **Arithmetic functions.** `ArithmeticFunction`, a semiring under Dirichlet convolution, with
  `ArithmeticFunction.IsMultiplicative`, the Möbius function `ArithmeticFunction.moebius`, Euler's
  function `Nat.totient` with `Nat.sum_totient` (`∑_{d ∣ n} φ(d) = n`), and Möbius inversion
  instantiated at divisor sums, `ArithmeticFunction.sum_eq_iff_sum_mul_moebius_eq`, with Mathlib's
  convention that statements are made for `n > 0`.

The unit and zero cases the stage asks to be explicit are those of the library: congruence modulo
`0` is equality, `f 0 = 0` for arithmetic functions, and Möbius inversion is stated for positive
arguments.

### The Chinese remainder theorem at non-coprime moduli over ℤ

`crt-at-non-coprime-moduli-over-the-integers` (theorem). For all integers `m, n, a, b`, the
congruences `x ≡ a (mod m)` and `x ≡ b (mod n)` have a common integer solution if and only if
`a ≡ b (mod gcd(m, n))`, where `gcd(m, n)` is the nonnegative gcd of `|m|` and `|n|`. When a
solution `x` exists, the solutions are exactly the `y ≡ x (mod lcm(m, n))`; that uniqueness half is
the pinned `Int.modEq_and_modEq_iff_modEq_lcm` and is not a new declaration.

No positivity or coprimality hypothesis is imposed. Shoup states the result for positive moduli; the
statement holds verbatim for every pair of integers, because congruence depends only on `|m|` and
`|n|` and congruence modulo `0` is equality (for `m = 0` the first congruence forces `x = a`, and the
criterion reads `a ≡ b (mod n)`).

Proof. Necessity: `gcd(m, n)` divides `m` and `n`, hence `(x − b) − (x − a) = a − b`. Sufficiency:
write `g = s m + t n` (Bézout); if `a − b = g k` then `x = a − s m k = b + t n k` solves both
congruences.

⚠ The solution set is one class modulo the least common multiple, never modulo the product: for
`m = 4`, `n = 6` the solutions of `x ≡ 1 (mod 4)`, `x ≡ 3 (mod 6)` are `x ≡ 9 (mod 12)`, not a class
modulo `24`.

Suggested name: `int_exists_modEq_and_modEq_iff`, in a module on the Chinese remainder theorem at
non-coprime moduli.

Acceptance tests:

- `x ≡ 1 (mod 4)` and `x ≡ 3 (mod 6)` are compatible (`x = 9`), since `1 ≡ 3 (mod 2)`;
  `x ≡ 0 (mod 4)` and `x ≡ 1 (mod 6)` are not, since `0` and `1` differ modulo `gcd(4, 6) = 2`.
- For coprime moduli the criterion is vacuous and the statement is the classical theorem, whose ring
  form is `ZMod.chineseRemainder`.
- For `m = n` the criterion is `a ≡ b (mod m)` and the solution set is one class modulo `m`.
- On nonnegative data with positive moduli the existence half agrees with `Nat.chineseRemainder'`.

Source: Shoup, *A Computational Introduction to Number Theory and Algebra*, version 2, Exercise 2.18
(p. 24).

### Dependencies

CA.0 has no prerequisite layer inside this roadmap. It is a prerequisite of CA.1 and CA.2. Its only
external supplier is the pinned Mathlib.

### Status

Closed: every target of the stage is a baseline citation, and the one unstated statement is the node
above.

---

## CA.1 Residues and reciprocity

This layer owns the residue symbols and reciprocity laws beyond what the libraries and the Tau Ceti
roadmaps already supply: the structure of the units modulo `2^e` and the `d`-th power criteria; the
primitive quadratic Dirichlet character of a rational square class, with its conductor; the reading
of the Hilbert symbol of `ℚ` at every place, including `2` and `∞`, in Mathlib's residue symbols; the
`n`-th power residue symbol in a number field containing the `n`-th roots of unity; the degree-`n`
Hilbert symbol at places not dividing `n`, Hilbert's product formula of degree `n` and the general
power reciprocity law; and the classical laws of cubic and Eisenstein reciprocity. Quadratic
reciprocity and the Gauss and Jacobi sum identities are built and are imported, not re-proved; the
nodes here compare with them explicitly.

The boundary with neighbouring roadmaps is fixed as follows.

- `FiniteFieldsAndCharacterSums:FF.1` owns the normalisations of characters of finite fields and of
  Gauss and Jacobi sums (trivial-character conventions, trace characters, transport of values). This
  layer imports them for the cubic and Eisenstein laws.
- The `{±1}`-valued Hilbert symbol over a nonarchimedean local field, with its closed formulas at odd
  residue characteristic and over `ℚ_2`, is `tauceti:TauCetiRoadmap/QuadraticFormInvariants`, 6C; its
  archimedean value and the product formula over the places of a number field are
  `tauceti:TauCetiRoadmap/GlobalQuadraticForms`, Layer 4 (4.4); the cohomological product formula
  and quadratic reciprocity derived from it are `tauceti:TauCetiRoadmap/ClassFieldTheory`, Layer 14.
  This layer defines no second quadratic symbol: it reads that symbol over the completions of `ℚ`.
- Local and global Artin reciprocity are `tauceti:TauCetiRoadmap/ClassFieldTheory`, Layers 6 and 11;
  unramified extensions of local fields are `tauceti:TauCetiRoadmap/LocalFieldsRamification`,
  Layer 2. Class Field Theory places explicit power reciprocity laws beyond quadratic reciprocity
  outside its scope; they are this layer's.
- The degree-`n` norm-residue symbol of a local field containing `μ_n` is `K2SymbolsBrauer:T.7`'s
  `normResidueSymbol`; this layer pins its orientation and proves the tame formula, the product
  formula of degree `n` and the power reciprocity law.

### Conventions

- Characters of `ℤ/N` are Mathlib's `DirichletCharacter ℤ N` (that is `MulChar (ZMod N) ℤ`): values in
  `{0, ±1}` for quadratic characters, `0` exactly at the non-units. Complex values are obtained by
  `MulChar.ringHomComp`.
- The Legendre symbol of a rational `u` with `v_p(u) = 0` at an odd prime `p` is
  `(u / p) := legendreSym p (num u · den u)`, which equals `(num u / p)(den u / p)^(-1)`.
- The Kronecker character of a fundamental discriminant `D` takes at `2` the value `0` if `D` is even,
  `1` if `D ≡ 1 (mod 8)` and `-1` if `D ≡ 5 (mod 8)`, and at `-1` the sign of `D`.
- The quadratic Hilbert symbol `(a, b)_v` is the norm-equation symbol of Quadratic Form Invariants
  6C: `1` exactly when `b = x^2 − a y^2` has a solution in the completion. It is symmetric.
- The degree-`n` Hilbert symbol is `(a, b)_v = Art_v(a)(ⁿ√b) / ⁿ√b`, with `Art_v` the local Artin map
  (Shastri's orientation). Milne writes the opposite orientation, `φ_K(b)(a^{1/n}) = (a, b) a^{1/n}`;
  the two differ by exchanging the arguments, that is by inversion. Every statement below uses the
  pinned orientation.
- The `n`-th power residue symbol at a prime `𝔭` is defined only for `𝔭 ∤ n`, read as `N𝔭` coprime to
  `n`. At primes dividing `n` the declaration carries a fixed junk value (the trivial character) and
  every theorem carries the coprimality hypothesis: a prime above `n` is never passed through the
  residue-symbol formulas, and in the reciprocity laws such primes enter only through Hilbert symbols.
- Primary elements: in `ℤ[ω]`, `π ≡ 2 (mod 3)`; in `ℤ[ζ_l]`, prime to `l` and congruent to a rational
  integer modulo `(1 − ζ)^2`.

### What is already built

Cited from the pinned Mathlib: `legendreSym`, `jacobiSym`, `legendreSym.quadratic_reciprocity`,

`jacobiSym.at_neg_one`, `jacobiSym.at_two`; Euler's criterion `ZMod.euler_criterion` and
`FiniteField.isSquare_iff`; the characters `ZMod.χ₄`, `ZMod.χ₈`, `ZMod.χ₈'` and `quadraticChar`;
`gaussSum`, `gaussSum_mul_gaussSum_eq_card`, `gaussSum_sq`, `gaussSum_frob`, `jacobiSum`,
`jacobiSum_eq_gaussSum_mul_gaussSum_div_gaussSum`, `gaussSum_pow_eq_prod_jacobiSum`; the cyclicity
criterion `ZMod.isCyclic_units_iff` with `ZMod.isCyclic_units_two_pow_iff` and `ZMod.orderOf_five`;
the power-map cardinalities `IsCyclic.card_powMonoidHom_range` and `IsCyclic.card_powMonoidHom_ker`;
Dirichlet characters with `DirichletCharacter.conductor`, `DirichletCharacter.IsPrimitive`,

`Nat.forall_exists_prime_gt_and_eq_mod`; the reduction of roots of unity
`Ideal.rootsOfUnityMapQuot_injective`; and the arithmetic Frobenius `IsArithFrobAt`.

Cited from the pinned Tau Ceti: the prime-discriminant characters
`TauCeti.Multiquadratic.primeDiscriminantCharFun` and the genus characters

action of a Frobenius on a square root by the Legendre symbol.

### 1. The units modulo a power of two, and power residue criteria

**Construction `unit-group-of-a-power-of-two`** (planet: *Units modulo a power of two*). For every
`e`, with `5_e` the unit `5` of `ℤ/2^(e+3)`, there is an isomorphism
`unitsTwoPowEquiv e : (ℤ/2^(e+3))ˣ ≃* Multiplicative (ZMod 2 × ZMod 2^(e+1))` sending `-1` to
`(1, 0)` and `5_e` to `(0, 1)`: every unit is uniquely `(-1)^s 5^k`. The indexing by `e + 3` encodes
that the modulus is at least `8`; modulo `2` and `4` the group is cyclic
(`ZMod.isCyclic_units_two_pow_iff`).

API: `unitsTwoPowFive` (the unit `5`); `unitsTwoPowEquiv`; `unitsTwoPowEquiv_symm_apply`
(`(s, k) ↦ (-1)^s 5^k`); `unitsTwoPowEquiv_neg_one` and `unitsTwoPowEquiv_five` (the two
evaluations); `neg_one_notMem_zpowers_unitsTwoPowFive`; `orderOf_unitsTwoPowFive` (order
`2^(e+1)`, the unit-group reading of `ZMod.orderOf_five`).

Unit tests: every unit modulo `8` squares to `1` (`unitsTwoPow_test_eight_klein`); `5` has order
`4` modulo `16` (`unitsTwoPow_test_sixteen_order_five`); the group modulo `2^(e+3)` is not cyclic
(`unitsTwoPow_test_not_cyclic`); modulo `4` it is (`unitsTwoPow_test_four_cyclic`); `7` has order `2`
modulo `16`, so it cannot replace `5` (`unitsTwoPow_test_seven_not_generator`).

Proof: the order of `5` is `ZMod.orderOf_five`; `-1` is not a power of `5` because powers of `5` are
`1` or `5` modulo `8`; the product map `(s, k) ↦ (-1)^s 5^k` is injective between groups of order
`2^(e+2) = φ(2^(e+3))`. Source: Shoup, Theorem 7.28 and its proof for `p = 2` (pp. 203–205).

**Lemma `squares-modulo-a-power-of-two`.** A unit modulo `2^(e+3)` is a square exactly when it is
`1` modulo `8` (Conrad, *Hensel's lemma*, Theorem 4.5, at finite level). Proof from the construction:
squares are the `5^(2k)`.

**Lemma `principal-units-modulo-an-odd-prime-power-are-squares`.** For an odd prime `p`, a unit
modulo `p^(k+1)` that is `1` modulo `p` is a square: the kernel of reduction has odd order `p^k`, so
squaring is bijective on it (`powCoprime`). Source: Conrad, Example 2.8. ⚠ False for `p = 2`: `5` is
not a square modulo `8`.

**Theorem `power-residue-criterion-in-a-cyclic-group`.** In a finite cyclic group of order `N`, the
image of the `d`-th power map is the kernel of the `N / gcd(N, d)`-th power map; so `a` is a `d`-th
power exactly when `a^(N / gcd(N, d)) = 1`. No divisibility of `N` by `d` is assumed. Proof: the image
is inside the kernel, and both have `N / gcd(N, d)` elements (`IsCyclic.card_powMonoidHom_range`,
`IsCyclic.card_powMonoidHom_ker`, `Subgroup.eq_of_le_of_card_ge`). Source: Shoup, Theorem 6.32 (iii)
and (vi), and Example 7.61. Acceptance: for `(ℤ/p)ˣ` and `d = 2` this is `ZMod.euler_criterion`.

**Theorem `power-residue-criterion-in-a-finite-field`.** In a finite field with `q` elements, a
nonzero `a` is an `n`-th power exactly when `a^((q − 1)/gcd(q − 1, n)) = 1`; the nonzero `n`-th powers
have index `gcd(q − 1, n)`, and each has exactly `gcd(q − 1, n)` roots. Source: Relyea, Theorem 1.7.
Acceptance: for `n = 2` this is `FiniteField.isSquare_iff`; every nonzero element of `𝔽_5` is a cube,
while the nonzero cubes of `𝔽_7` are `±1`.

### 2. The primitive quadratic character of a rational square class

Bennett and Siksek (§§6–8) attach to each rational `λ` and `ω ∈ {±1, ±2}` "the unique primitive
quadratic character of conductor `N`" with `χ(p) = (ω λ / p)` at odd primes `p` with `v_p(λ) = 0`, and
use that its conductor has squarefree odd part and `2`-part at most `8`. Mathlib has conductors and
primitivity but neither the classification by square classes nor the conductor bound; Tau Ceti has
the prime-discriminant and genus characters as unbundled functions. This section bundles them.

**Definition `squarefree-part-of-a-rational`.** For `u ∈ ℚˣ`, `ratSquarefreePart u` is the unique
squarefree integer `d` with `u = d c^2` for some nonzero rational `c`; `ratSquarefreePart 0 = 0`. Its
sign is that of `u`, and a prime `p` divides it exactly when `v_p(u)` is odd.

API: `ratSquarefreePart`; `squarefree_ratSquarefreePart`; `exists_eq_ratSquarefreePart_mul_sq`;
`ratSquarefreePart_eq_iff` (uniqueness); `ratSquarefreePart_mul_sq` (square-class invariance);
`ratSquarefreePart_intCast_of_squarefree`; `prime_dvd_ratSquarefreePart_iff` (odd valuation);
`ratSquarefreePart_pos_iff`; `ratSquarefreePart_zero`.

Unit tests: `ratSquarefreePart (8/9) = 2`; `ratSquarefreePart (-12) = -3`; `ratSquarefreePart 1 = 1`;
`ratSquarefreePart (-1) = -1`, not `1` (a definition through `|u|` fails).

Sources: Shoup, Exercise 1.30 (the factorisation `x = ± ∏ p^{ν_p(x)}` of a rational); existence is
Tau Ceti's `Rat.exists_squarefree_int_mul_sq`.

**Lemmas on primitivity.** `dyadic-characters-are-primitive`: `χ₄`, `χ₈` and `χ₈'` are primitive
(`χ₄(3) = -1` with `3 ≡ 1 (mod 2)`; `χ₈(5) = χ₈'(5) = -1` with `5 ≡ 1 (mod 4)`).
`legendre-character-is-primitive`: `quadraticChar (ZMod p)` is primitive for odd `p`.
`primitivity-of-a-product-at-coprime-levels`: the product of primitive characters of coprime levels
`m, n`, lifted to level `m n`, is primitive (Chinese remainder theorem).

**Construction `kronecker-character`.** For a fundamental discriminant `D`, `kroneckerCharacter D` is
the Dirichlet character of level `|D|` with values `(D / n)`: completely multiplicative, `0` exactly off
the integers prime to `D`, `legendreSym q D` at odd primes `q`, the dyadic value above at `2` and the
sign of `D` at `-1`. It is Tau Ceti's `genusCharFun` on the prime-discriminant factorisation of `D`,
bundled.

API: `kroneckerCharacter`; `kroneckerCharacter_apply_prime`; `kroneckerCharacter_apply_two`;
`kroneckerCharacter_apply_neg_one`; `kroneckerCharacter_isQuadratic`;
`kroneckerCharacter_apply_eq_zero_iff`; `kroneckerCharacter_natCast_eq_jacobiSym` (agreement with the
Jacobi symbol at odd arguments); `kroneckerCharacter_mul` (coprime discriminants);
`kroneckerCharacter_one`; the prime-discriminant cases `kroneckerCharacter_neg_four` (`χ₄`),
`kroneckerCharacter_eight` (`χ₈`), `kroneckerCharacter_neg_eight` (`χ₈'`) and
`kroneckerCharacter_oddPrimeDiscr` (the Legendre character at `p* = (-1)^((p−1)/2) p`).

Unit tests: `(5 / 2) = -1`; `(-3 / 2) = -1` while `jacobiSym (-3) 2 = 1` (the Kronecker character is
not Mathlib's Jacobi symbol at even arguments); `(12 / n) = -1, -1, 1` at `n = 5, 7, 11`;
`kroneckerCharacter 1` is the trivial character of level `1`.

Sources: Bennett–Siksek §6 (the characters `µ_i`, p. 369); Voight §24.3.3 (the Kronecker symbol as a
splitting type, including `p = 2`).

**Theorem `kronecker-character-is-primitive`.** The conductor of `kroneckerCharacter D` is `|D|`:
induction on the prime-discriminant factorisation with the three primitivity lemmas.

**Construction `quadratic-character-of-a-squareclass`** (planet: *Quadratic character of a square
class*). For `u ∈ ℚˣ` with `d = ratSquarefreePart u`, `squareclassDiscr u` is `d` if `d ≡ 1 (mod 4)`
and `4d` otherwise, a fundamental discriminant, and `squareclassCharacter u := kroneckerCharacter
(squareclassDiscr u)`. It is primitive of conductor `|squareclassDiscr u|`; it agrees with `(u / p)`
at every odd `p` with `v_p(u) = 0`; it depends only on the square class; the odd part of its conductor
is the squarefree product of the odd primes where `v_p(u)` is odd, and equals `1` exactly when
`u ∈ {±1, ±2} · (ℚˣ)^2`; its `2`-adic valuation is `0`, `2` or `3` according as `d ≡ 1`, `d ≡ 3
(mod 4)` or `d` even; its value at `-1` is the sign of `u`; and it is the only primitive character
agreeing with `(u / p)` at almost all primes.

API: `squareclassDiscr`; `squareclassCharacter`; `isFundamentalDiscr_squareclassDiscr`;
`squareclassCharacter_isPrimitive`; `squareclassCharacter_apply_prime`; `squareclassDiscr_mul_sq`;
`squareclassDiscr_one`; `odd_prime_dvd_squareclassDiscr_iff`;
`ordCompl_two_squareclassDiscr_eq_one_iff`; `factorization_two_squareclassDiscr`;
`squareclassCharacter_apply_neg_one`; `squareclassCharacter_mul` (at integers prime to `2` and to the
numerators and denominators).

Unit tests: the class of `-1` has discriminant `-4` and character `χ₄`; the classes of `2` and of
`8/9` have discriminant `8`; the class of `3` has conductor `12`, not `3`; the class of `5` has
conductor `5`; the class of `1` has conductor `1`.

Uses: Bennett–Siksek Lemma 6.2 (ii), Proposition 6.1 (the characters `χ_a` and `µ_i` attached to
`λ, −λ, 2λ, −2λ` and `ω t v`), Lemma 7.3 and Proposition 7.2; the proposed Part II of Elliptic curves
on Legendre models, and the proposed roadmap on perfect powers in arithmetic progressions, both from
the routing of that paper, import this character.

**Lemma `primitive-characters-agreeing-at-almost-all-primes-are-equal`.** Two primitive characters of
nonzero levels agreeing at all primes outside a finite set have the same level and are equal
(Dirichlet's theorem gives, in each unit class modulo the product of the levels, a prime outside the
set). This is the word "unique" in Bennett–Siksek, Lemma 6.2 (ii).

**Lemmas and theorem on quadratic conductors.** Let `χ` be a primitive quadratic character of nonzero
level `N`, with values in a commutative ring.

- `quadratic-conductor-is-not-twice-odd`: `v₂(N) ≠ 1` (for any primitive character: modulo `2m`, `m`
  odd, reduction to `m` is injective on units).
- `odd-part-of-a-quadratic-conductor-is-squarefree`: `N / 2^(v₂(N))` is squarefree (a unit that is `1`
  modulo `N/p` with `p^2 ∣ N`, `p` odd, is a square, so `χ` factors through `N/p`).
- **Theorem `two-adic-conductor-bound`**: `v₂(N) ≤ 3`, that is `N ≤ 8 N^odd` (a unit that is `1`
  modulo `N/2` with `16 ∣ N` is `1` modulo `8`, hence a square modulo the `2`-part, so `χ` factors
  through `N/2`). Together: `v₂(N) ∈ {0, 2, 3}` and `N^odd` squarefree, the shape `N = 2^κ N₁` with
  `N₁` squarefree and `κ ∈ {0, 1, 2}` used in Bennett–Siksek, Lemma 7.3.

⚠ The quadratic hypothesis is needed in both conductor statements: the characters of order `3`
modulo `9` and of order `4` modulo `16` are primitive.

### 3. The Hilbert symbol of ℚ at every place

**Comparison `hilbert-symbol`.** For `a, b ∈ ℚˣ` and a place `v` of `ℚ`, `(a, b)_v` is Quadratic
Form Invariants' norm-equation symbol over `ℚ_v` (`ℚ_[p]` or `ℝ`). At an odd prime `p`, writing
`a = p^α u`, `b = p^β w` with `u, w` rational `p`-adic units,

```text
(a, b)_p = (−1)^(α β (p − 1)/2) · (u / p)^β · (w / p)^α,      (u / p) = legendreSym p (num u · den u).
```

This is QFI 6C's closed formula at `K = ℚ_p` read on rational inputs (Voight (12.4.10)). The value at
`∞` (`−1` exactly when `a < 0` and `b < 0`) and the product formula `∏_v (a, b)_v = 1` are imported
from Global Quadratic Forms 4.4 and Class Field Theory 14.

**Comparison `dyadic-hilbert-symbol-via-chi4-chi8`.** For odd integers `u, w`,

```text
(2^α u, 2^β w)_2 = s(u, w) · χ₈(w)^α · χ₈(u)^β,      s(u, w) = −1 exactly when χ₄(u) = χ₄(w) = −1;
```

that is, Serre's `ε` and `ω` of QFI 6C item 10 are read off Mathlib's characters: `(−1)^ε = χ₄` and
`(−1)^ω = χ₈` on odd integers.

Acceptance tests: `(−1, −1)_2 = −1`, `(−1, −1)_∞ = −1` and `(−1, −1)_p = 1` for odd `p`; `(2, 5)_2 = −1`,
`(2, 2)_2 = (−1, 2)_2 = (5, 5)_2 = 1`; for distinct odd primes `p, q`, `(p, q)_q = legendreSym q p` and
`(p, q)_2 = (−1)^((p−1)(q−1)/4)`, so the imported product formula at `(p, q)`, `(−1, p)` and `(2, p)`
is equivalent to `legendreSym.quadratic_reciprocity`, `legendreSym.at_neg_one` and
`legendreSym.at_two` (Voight, Proposition 14.2.1). This is the explicit comparison of Hilbert
reciprocity over `ℚ` with the pinned quadratic reciprocity; the product formula itself is owned by
Global Quadratic Forms 4.4.

### 4. The n-th power residue symbol

Throughout, `K` is a number field, `n ≥ 1`, and `𝓞_K` contains a primitive `n`-th root of unity
(`HasEnoughRootsOfUnity (𝓞_K) n`).

**Lemma `norm-of-a-prime-is-one-modulo-n`.** For a maximal ideal `𝔭` with `N𝔭` coprime to `n`,
`N𝔭 ≡ 1 (mod n)`, and `μ_n(𝓞_K)` reduces bijectively onto the `n`-th roots of unity of `𝓞_K/𝔭`
(Adhikari §4, p. 68).

**Definition `power-residue-symbol`** (planet: *Power residue symbol*). For `𝔭` maximal with `N𝔭`
coprime to `n`, `(· / 𝔭)_n` is the multiplicative character of the residue field `𝓞_K/𝔭` with values in
`𝓞_K`: `0` at `0`, and for `x ≠ 0` the unique `μ ∈ μ_n(𝓞_K)` with `μ ≡ x^((N𝔭 − 1)/n) (mod 𝔭)`. For
`α ∈ 𝓞_K`, `(α / 𝔭)_n` is the symbol at the residue of `α`. At primes dividing `n` it is the trivial
character by convention.

API: `powerResidueSymbol`; `powerResidueSymbol_spec` (the defining congruence);
`powerResidueSymbol_mem_rootsOfUnity`; `powerResidueSymbol_eq_iff` (uniqueness);
`powerResidueSymbol_zeta` (`(ζ / 𝔭)_n = ζ^((N𝔭 − 1)/n)`, the supplement for roots of unity);
`powerResidueSymbol_pow_div` (`((x / 𝔭)_n)^(n/d) = (x / 𝔭)_d` for `d ∣ n`);
`orderOf_powerResidueSymbol` (order `n`); `powerResidueSymbol_of_not_coprime` (the convention).

Unit tests: for `n = 1` the symbol is trivial; for a primitive cube root of unity `ζ`, `(ζ / 𝔭)_3 = ζ`
at a prime of norm `4` and `ζ^2` at a prime of norm `7`; at a prime of norm `4`, `(ζ / 𝔭)_3` is neither
`1` nor `−1` (a sign-valued "is a cube" indicator is not this object); for `n = 2` the symbol is
`quadraticChar` of the residue field, which at a prime of `ℤ` is `legendreSym p`.

Sources: Adhikari §4 (p. 68, the definition as a character of the residue field); Milne V.3 (p. 166,
the definition through the Artin symbol); Shastri §6 (p. 180, the local definition through the
Hilbert symbol). The three agree by the Frobenius comparison and the tame formula below.

**Theorem `power-residue-euler-criterion`.** For `N𝔭` coprime to `n` and `x ≠ 0`, `(x / 𝔭)_n = 1`
exactly when `x` is an `n`-th power in `𝓞_K/𝔭` (from the finite-field criterion, since `n ∣ N𝔭 − 1`).

**Definition `power-residue-symbol-of-an-ideal`.** For an ideal `𝔞` with `N𝔞` coprime to `n`,
`(α / 𝔞)_n = ∏ (α / 𝔭ᵢ)_n` over the prime factors of `𝔞` with multiplicity; `(α / β)_n` is the symbol at
`(β)`; the unit ideal gives `1`.

API: `powerResidueSymbolIdeal`; `powerResidueSymbolIdeal_of_isMaximal`;
`powerResidueSymbolIdeal_mul_right`; `powerResidueSymbolIdeal_mul_left`; `powerResidueSymbolIdeal_top`;
`powerResidueSymbolIdeal_eq_zero_iff`; `powerResidueSymbolIdeal_congr`.

Unit tests: `(α / 𝓞_K)_n = 1`; `(α / 𝔭^2)_n = ((α / 𝔭)_n)^2`; for `n = 2` a nonresidue modulo `𝔭` has
symbol `1` at `𝔭^2` but is not a square modulo `𝔭^2`.

**Lemma `power-residue-symbol-galois-equivariance`.** For every ring automorphism `σ` of `𝓞_K`,
`σ((α / 𝔞)_n) = (σα / σ𝔞)_n` (Adhikari, Proposition 4.1).

**Comparison `power-residue-symbol-and-frobenius`.** If `σ` is an arithmetic Frobenius at a prime `𝔓`
of `𝓞_L` above `𝔭` (Mathlib's `IsArithFrobAt`), `α ∉ 𝔭`, `N𝔭` coprime to `n` and `β^n = α`, then
`σ(β) = (α / 𝔭)_n · β`. For `n = 2` this is Tau Ceti's `IsArithFrobAt.smul_sqrt`. It identifies the
congruence definition with Milne's definition through the Artin symbol of `K(ⁿ√α)/K`.

### 5. Degree-n Hilbert symbols and the power reciprocity law

`K` contains `μ_n`; `(a, b)_v` is the degree-`n` norm-residue symbol in the pinned orientation.

**Theorem `tame-hilbert-symbol-formula`.** At a finite place `v` whose prime `𝔭` does not divide `n`,

```text
(a, b)_v = ( (−1)^(v(a) v(b)) · b^(v(a)) / a^(v(b))  mod 𝔭  /  𝔭 )_n;
```

in particular `(u, w)_v = 1` for units and `(π, u)_v = (u / 𝔭)_n`. Proof: units are norms from the
unramified extension `K_v(ⁿ√w)` (LocalFieldsRamification 2, Class Field Theory 6); a uniformiser acts as
Frobenius, which moves `ⁿ√w` by `w^((N𝔭 − 1)/n)`; skew-symmetry and `(π, −π)_v = 1` give the rest.
Source: Shastri §6. ⚠ Not valid at places above `n`: `(−1, −1)_2 = −1` for `n = 2`.

**Theorem `hilbert-product-formula-of-degree-n`.** For `a, b ∈ Kˣ`, `(a, b)_v = 1` at almost all places
and `∏_v (a, b)_v = 1` over all places. For `n ≥ 3` the archimedean places are complex and contribute
`1`; for `n = 2` a real place contributes `−1` exactly when both arguments are negative there. Proof:
global Artin reciprocity (Class Field Theory 11) applied to `ⁿ√b`; finite support from the tame
formula. Sources: Shastri, Theorem 4; Milne, Example V.5.4. For `n = 2` this is the imported
`ClassFieldTheory.hilbertProductFormula`.

**Theorem `power-reciprocity-law`** (planet: *Power reciprocity law*). For nonzero `a, b ∈ 𝓞_K` with
`(a)`, `(b)`, `(n)` pairwise coprime,

```text
(a / b)_n · ((b / a)_n)^(−1) = ∏_{v ∣ n∞} (a, b)_v.
```

Proof: split the product formula over `v ∣ a`, `v ∣ b`, `v ∣ n∞` and the rest, and evaluate the first
two by the tame formula (Shastri, Theorem 5, with the index misprint of its proof corrected). The
places above `n` enter only through Hilbert symbols. Acceptance: for `K = ℚ`, `n = 2` and odd positive
coprime `a, b` the right side is `(a, b)_2 (a, b)_∞ = (−1)^((a−1)(b−1)/4)`, which is
`jacobiSym.quadratic_reciprocity`.

### 6. Cubic reciprocity

`K = ℚ(ζ₃)`, `𝓞_K = ℤ[ω]`, and `χ_π = (· / π)_3`.

**Definition `primary-eisenstein-integer`.** `π` is primary if `π ≡ 2 (mod 3)`; for `π = a + bω`,
`a ≡ 2`, `b ≡ 0 (mod 3)`. API: `IsPrimaryEisenstein`; `isPrimaryEisenstein_add_mul_iff`;
`exists_unique_associated_isPrimaryEisenstein` (every prime of norm `≠ 3` has exactly one primary
associate); `IsPrimaryEisenstein.neg_mul`; `isPrimaryEisenstein_intCast_iff`. Unit tests: `2` and
`−1 − 3ω` (norm `7`) are primary; `3 + ω` (norm `7`) is not; `1 − ω` (norm `3`) is not.

**Lemma `cubic-jacobi-sum-of-a-primary-prime`.** For a primary prime `π` of norm `p ≡ 1 (mod 3)`,
`J(χ_π, χ_π) = π` (Adhikari, Lemma 3.2).

**Lemma `cubic-gauss-sum-cube`.** For such `π`, an embedding `φ : 𝓞_K → ℂ` and a primitive additive
character `ψ` of `𝔽_p`, `g(χ_π, ψ)^3 = p · φ(π)` (Adhikari, Proposition 3.3 and (11); the identity
`g^n = χ(−1) q ∏ J(χ, χ^i)` is `gaussSum_pow_eq_prod_jacobiSum`).

**Theorem `cubic-reciprocity`** (planet: *Cubic reciprocity*). For primary primes `π₁, π₂` of norms
different from `3` and from each other, `(π₂ / π₁)_3 = (π₁ / π₂)_3`. Proof in three cases (both
rational; one rational; both complex) by the Gauss sum congruence modulo the rational prime
(`gaussSum_frob`) and Galois equivariance (Adhikari, Theorem 3.1). Acceptance: for `π = −1 − 3ω`,
`(2 / π)_3 = (π / 2)_3 = ω^2`.

**Theorem `cubic-supplement-for-one-minus-zeta`.** For a primary prime `π = a + bω` of norm `≠ 3` with
`a = 3m − 1`, `((1 − ω) / π)_3 = ω^(2m)` (Eisenstein's supplement, Williams' proof, Adhikari
pp. 66–67). With `(−1 / π)_3 = 1` and `(ω / π)_3 = ω^((Nπ − 1)/3)` from the power residue symbol API
these are the supplementary laws.

### 7. Eisenstein reciprocity

**Definition `primary-element-of-a-cyclotomic-ring`.** For an odd prime `l` and `ζ` a primitive `l`-th
root of unity, `α ∈ ℤ[ζ]` is primary if it is prime to `l` and congruent to a rational integer modulo
`(1 − ζ)^2`. API: `IsEisensteinPrimary`; `exists_unique_zeta_pow_mul_isEisensteinPrimary`;
`isEisensteinPrimary_intCast`; `IsEisensteinPrimary.mul`. Unit tests: `1` and `2` are primary; `ζ` is
not; `1 − ζ` is not.

**Theorem `eisenstein-reciprocity`** (planet: *Eisenstein reciprocity*). For a primary nonunit `α` and
a rational integer `a ≠ ±1` prime to `l` and to `α`, `(α / a)_l = (a / α)_l` (Adhikari, Theorem 4.1).
The proof goes through the Gauss sums `g(P)` of the characters `χ_P = (· / P)^(−1)`, their `l`-th
powers `Φ(P) ∈ ℚ(ζ)`, Stickelberger's theorem `(Φ(P)) = P^γ`, the unit `ε(α) = ±1` for primary `α`, and
the relation `(Φ(P) / P')_l = (N P' / P)_l`. Stickelberger's theorem is FiniteFieldsAndCharacterSums FF.1's
`FF.1/stickelberger-relation`; the other two inputs are recorded as a gap.

### Dependencies

Inside the roadmap: CA.1 rests on CA.0 (congruences, the Chinese remainder theorem) and is a
prerequisite of CA.4 (elementary descent uses square classes and residue symbols). Other roadmaps:
`FiniteFieldsAndCharacterSums:FF.1` (character normalisations) and `K2SymbolsBrauer:T.7` (the
degree-`n` norm-residue symbol), both by request; the Tau Ceti layers
`tauceti:TauCetiRoadmap/QuadraticFormInvariants#6c-the-hilbert-symbol-and-the-local-hasse-invariant`,
`tauceti:TauCetiRoadmap/GlobalQuadraticForms#layer-4-global-square-norm-and-approximation-lemmas`,
`tauceti:TauCetiRoadmap/ClassFieldTheory#layer-6-the-local-class-formation-and-finite-local-reciprocity`,
`tauceti:TauCetiRoadmap/ClassFieldTheory#layer-11-the-global-class-formation-and-global-artin-reciprocity`,
`tauceti:TauCetiRoadmap/ClassFieldTheory#layer-14-hilbert-reciprocity-and-quadratic-reciprocity` and
`tauceti:TauCetiRoadmap/LocalFieldsRamification#layer-2-unramified-extensions-and-frobenius`, as
upstream prerequisites. Consumers outside the roadmap: the Bennett–Siksek routes (the Legendre-model
Part II of Elliptic curves and the roadmap on perfect powers in arithmetic progressions) import the
square-class character and the conductor bound.

### Acceptance tests of the layer

- Reciprocity normalisations agree with the pinned ones: the power residue symbol for `n = 2` is
  `quadraticChar` and at a prime of `ℤ` is `legendreSym`; the power reciprocity law at `K = ℚ`, `n = 2`
  is `jacobiSym.quadratic_reciprocity`; the Hilbert symbol of `ℚ` at odd `p`, at `2` and at `∞` is
  read in `legendreSym`, `χ₄`, `χ₈` and signs, and its product formula is equivalent to quadratic
  reciprocity with both supplements.
- Finite-field character conventions are imported from FF.1, including the trivial-character case,
  where Mathlib's `jacobiSum 1 1 = q − 2` differs from the classical `J(ε, ε) = q`.
- Ramified primes are never passed through unramified formulas: the power residue symbol and the
  tame formula carry `𝔭 ∤ n`; cubic reciprocity excludes the prime of norm `3`; Eisenstein reciprocity
  excludes `l`; the power reciprocity law keeps the places above `n` as Hilbert symbols.
- The `2`-adic conductor of a primitive quadratic character is `1`, `4` or `8`, attained by the classes
  of `5`, `−1` and `2`.

### Remaining in this layer

- Biquadratic reciprocity in `ℤ[i]`: the one public statement read (Relyea, Theorem 4.4) is misstated
  and leaves "primary" undefined; a public source with the definition (`α ≡ 1 mod (1 + i)^3`) and a
  proof is needed, and the law is then planned on the power residue symbol with `n = 4`.
- The two inputs of Eisenstein reciprocity besides Stickelberger's relation (which FF.1 plans), with the
  decomposition of Adhikari §4 into lemmas once an owner is fixed (Katre's article in the same
  proceedings is the public source).
- Explicit formulas for the degree-`n` Hilbert symbol at places above `n`; the power reciprocity law
  keeps those factors as Hilbert symbols.

---

## CA.2 Sequences and generating functions

This layer builds the classical theory of sequences that the rest of the roadmap and several
consumers read coefficients from: linear recurrences and their minimal polynomials, rational
generating functions and the closed form of recurrence sequences (including the polynomial
coefficients forced by repeated roots), companion matrices in every rank, Lucas sequences and
their strong divisibility, the Euler (zigzag) numbers, the passage from formal identities to
analytic ones on an explicit disc (with the Bernoulli and sec + tan generating functions as the
worked cases), radix expansions of p-adic integers and of reals, automatic sequences with
Christol's theorem over finite fields, and Farey sequences with the Stern–Brocot tree. Every
formal identity is proved formally first, and each analytic specialisation is a separate
convergence theorem with its radius stated.

Sources: V. Shoup, *A Computational Introduction to Number Theory and Algebra*, v2, §18.1
(linearly generated sequences); R. P. Stanley, *Enumerative Combinatorics* 1, 2nd ed. (author's
PDF), §4.1 (Theorem 4.1.1) and §1.6.1 (Euler numbers), with the author's errata of 17 August
2025; J.-H. Evertse, *Diophantine Approximation*, Leiden notes (2019), §8.4 (Theorem 8.17);
J. Hefferon, *Linear Algebra*, 4th ed., Topic: Linear Recurrences (companion matrices);
R. D. Carmichael, *On the numerical factors of the arithmetic forms αⁿ ± βⁿ*, Ann. of Math. 15
(1913) (JSTOR Early Journal Content), and P. Bala's note on strong divisibility sequences (OEIS
A238600), for Lucas sequences; the NIST DLMF §§4.19, 24.2 (radii of the Bernoulli, tan and sec
series); J. S. Milne, *Algebraic Number Theory* v3.08, Proposition 7.26 (p-adic expansions); the
PlanetMath entry on existence and uniqueness of decimal expansions; G. Christol, T. Kamae,
M. Mendès France, G. Rauzy, *Suites algébriques, automates et substitutions*, Bull. SMF 108
(1980) (Numdam); A. Bridy, *Automatic sequences and curves over finite fields*
(arXiv:1604.08241); B. Adamczewski and R. Yassawi, *A note on Christol's theorem*;
J. Byszewski and J. Konieczny, *Automatic sequences and generalised polynomials*
(arXiv:1705.08979); E. Rowland, M. Stipulanti and R. Yassawi, *An elementary proof of Bridy's
theorem* (arXiv:2308.10977); A. Hatcher, *Topology of Numbers* (revised 2024), §§1.1–1.2
(mediants, Farey series, the Farey diagram).

### What Mathlib and Tau Ceti already provide (consume; never restate)

- **Linear recurrences.** Mathlib's `LinearRecurrence R` (order and coefficients), `IsSolution`,
  the solution space `solSpace` with `toInit` (solutions ≃ initial values) and
  `solSpace_rank` (rank = order), `charPoly` (monic, `charPoly_monic`), the geometric solutions
  `geom_sol_iff_root_charPoly`, and the state shift `tupleSucc`. Fibonacci: `Nat.fib`,
  `Nat.fib_add`, Binet `Real.coe_fib_eq`, strong divisibility `Nat.fib_gcd` /
  `Nat.isStrongDvdSequence_fib`; the predicates `IsDvdSequence` and `Nat.IsStrongDvdSequence`.
  Dickson polynomials `Polynomial.dickson`; the Lucas–Lehmer sequence `LucasLehmer.s`.
- **Elliptic divisibility sequences.** Mathlib's `normEDS` and `IsEllipticDvdSequence`; Tau Ceti
  proves `isEllipticNet_normEDS` and `isDvdSequence_normEDS` (a normalised EDS is an elliptic
  net and a divisibility sequence over any commutative ring).
- **Bernoulli numbers.** `bernoulli'` and `bernoulli` (B₁ = +1/2 and −1/2), their formal
  generating functions `bernoulli'PowerSeries_mul_exp_sub_one` and
  `bernoulliPowerSeries_mul_exp_sub_one`, Faulhaber (`sum_range_pow`), von Staudt–Clausen
  (`Bernoulli.vonStaudt_clausen`), Bernoulli polynomials with `Polynomial.bernoulli_generating_function`,
  and the analytic values `riemannZeta_two_mul_nat`, `riemannZeta_neg_nat_eq_bernoulli`.
- **Binomial identities and number families.** `add_pow`, Vandermonde `Nat.add_choose_eq`,
  Lucas's theorem `Choose.choose_modEq_prod_range_choose`, Kummer `padicValNat_choose`;
  `catalan` with `catalan_eq_centralBinom_div`, `Nat.stirlingFirst`, `Nat.stirlingSecond`,
  `Nat.bell`, `Nat.Partition.genFun`, `numDerangements` with `numDerangements_tendsto_inv_e`.
- **Power series.** `PowerSeries`, `PowerSeries.mk`, `coeff_mul`, the inverse `PowerSeries.inv`
  (with `inv_eq_zero`), `invOneSubPow` ((1 − X)^{−d}), the formal `exp`, `sin`, `cos`, the
  derivative `PowerSeries.derivative`; `RatFunc`, `LaurentSeries` and the compatible embeddings
  (`RatFunc.coe_coe`); the K[X]-algebra structure `PowerSeries.algebraPolynomial` used for
  `IsAlgebraic K[X] f`.
- **Radix expansions.** `Nat.digits` (least significant first) with `Nat.ofDigits_digits` and
  `Nat.digits_ofDigits`; `Real.ofDigits`, `Real.digits`, `Real.ofDigits_digits`,
  `Real.ofDigits_const_last_eq_one'` (existence of real expansions, and 0.(b−1)(b−1)… = 1);
  `PadicInt.appr` with `appr_lt`, `appr_spec`, `dvd_appr_sub_appr`, `toZModPow`,
  `ext_of_toZModPow`.
- **Automata.** Acceptor automata `DFA` with `eval`, `evalFrom`, `accepts`, `Language.IsRegular`
  (states a Fintype in `Type`).
- **Analysis.** `iteratedDeriv` with the Leibniz rule `iteratedDeriv_mul`,
  `Complex.hasSum_taylorSeries_on_ball` (a function holomorphic on a disc is the sum of its
  Taylor series there), `DifferentiableOn.analyticAt`, `Complex.differentiableOn_dslope`,
  `Complex.exp_eq_one_iff`, `Complex.cos_eq_zero_iff`, `Complex.hasSum_cos`, `Complex.hasSum_sin`.
- **Companion matrices.** Tau Ceti has rank 2 only: `TauCeti.companionFinTwo t d = [[0, −d], [1, t]]`
  with `det_companionFinTwo`, `trace_companionFinTwo`. Mathlib has `Algebra.leftMulMatrix`,
  `AdjoinRoot.powerBasis'`, `charpoly_leftMulMatrix`, `Matrix.charpoly`,
  `Module.End.hasEigenvalue_iff_isRoot_charpoly`.

Mathlib and Tau Ceti have no minimal polynomial of a sequence, no rationality predicate, no
closed form with repeated roots, no companion matrix beyond rank 2, no Lucas sequences, no Euler
numbers, no convergent Bernoulli generating function, no p-adic digit equivalence, no uniqueness
statement for real expansions, no automata with output, no automatic sequences, no Christol
theorem, and nothing on Farey sequences, mediants or the Stern–Brocot tree.

### Conventions

- **Sequences** are functions ℕ → V, indexed from 0. A recurrence is Mathlib's
  `LinearRecurrence`; the recurrence of a monic polynomial p is `LinearRecurrence.ofMonic p`
  (order `natDegree p`, coefficients −`p.coeff i`), so that its characteristic polynomial is p.
  Evertse's u_h = c₁u_{h−1} + ⋯ + c_ku_{h−k} with c_k ≠ 0 is `ofMonic (X^k − c₁X^{k−1} − ⋯ − c_k)`;
  "nonzero constant term" is `E.charPoly.coeff 0 ≠ 0`.
- **Generating functions** are formal (`PowerSeries`); a statement about values carries its disc
  of convergence and is a separate theorem.
- **Companion matrix** `Polynomial.companion p`: ones on the subdiagonal and −`p.coeff i` in the
  last column (the matrix of multiplication by X on R[X]/(p) in the basis 1, X, …, X^{d−1}, as
  Tau Ceti's rank-two `companionFinTwo`). State vectors are oldest first,
  (u(n), …, u(n + d − 1)), as Mathlib's `tupleSucc`, and the recurrence acts by the transpose.
- **Lucas sequences** start at U₀ = 0, U₁ = 1 and V₀ = 2, V₁ = P over any commutative ring; gcd of
  integers is `Int.gcd` (a natural number) and is compared with `Int.natAbs`.
- **Euler numbers**: `zigzag n` = Stanley's E_n (1, 1, 1, 2, 5, 16, 61, …, all positive); the
  signed classical numbers of DLMF (sech t = ∑ 𝐄ₙtⁿ/n!) are `eulerNumber n` = (−1)^{n/2}E_n for
  even n and 0 for odd n.
- **Bernoulli numbers** are Mathlib's `bernoulli` (B₁ = −1/2), so ∑ Bₙzⁿ/n! = z/(e^z − 1).
- **Digits.** Real expansions are Mathlib's `Real.ofDigits d = ∑ dᵢ/b^{i+1}` (d₀ the first digit
  after the point); p-adic digits are the standard representatives 0, …, p − 1.
- **Automata** read the base-k digits of n *least significant digit first*, in Mathlib's
  `Nat.digits` order and without padding zeros (Christol–Kamae–Mendès France–Rauzy's "lecture
  inverse", Bridy's "reverse reading"); the other reading direction is a theorem, not a variant
  definition. States of an automatic sequence form a `Fintype` in `Type`. The k-kernel is
  {n ↦ a(k^e n + r) : e ≥ 0, r < k^e}.
- **Cartier operators** Λ_r (modulus q) act on Laurent series: Λ_r(∑ aₙXⁿ) = ∑ a_{qn+r}Xⁿ.
  Algebraic means `IsAlgebraic K[X] f` for f ∈ K⟦X⟧ (equivalently over K(X)).
- **Farey sequences** F_n are sets of rationals in [0, 1] with denominator ≤ n (both endpoints
  included, F₀ = ∅); fractions are compared through `Rat.num` and `Rat.den`; the mediant of x and
  y is (x.num + y.num)/(x.den + y.den) as a rational number. Stern–Brocot paths are `List Bool`
  (false = left) read from the root, with bounds starting at (0/1, 1/0).

### 2A. Linearly generated sequences, rational series and closed forms

- **`Sequence.minPoly`** (construction, `generating-polynomials-and-the-minimal-polynomial`;
  Shoup §18.1). For a field F, an F-vector space V and s : ℕ → V, `Sequence.star g s` is
  g ⋆ s = ∑ aⱼsⱼ; g is a generating polynomial (`Sequence.IsGeneratingPoly`) when (Xⁱg) ⋆ s = 0
  for all i; s is linearly generated (`Sequence.IsLinearlyGenerated F s`) when s_{k+i} =
  ∑_{j<k} cⱼs_{j+i} for some k ≥ 0. The generating polynomials form an ideal
  `Sequence.generatingIdeal F s` (closure under multiplication by X is the shift invariance), and
  `Sequence.minPoly F s` is its monic generator, and 0 when s is not linearly generated: the
  unique monic generating polynomial dividing every generating polynomial (not "the monic
  polynomial dividing every generating polynomial", which 1 satisfies). API: `star_add`,
  `mem_generatingIdeal`, `minPoly_dvd` (g generating iff minPoly ∣ g), `minPoly_monic`,
  `isLinearlyGenerated_iff` (iff minPoly ≠ 0), `minPoly_of_powers` (for α in an F-algebra,
  minPoly of (αⁱ) is Mathlib's `minpoly F α`, including the non-integral case where both are 0),
  `isSolution_iff_charPoly_mem` (E.IsSolution u iff E.charPoly ∈ G(u)). Unit tests:
  `Sequence.minPoly_zero` (the zero sequence has minimal polynomial 1),
  `Sequence.isGeneratingPoly_one_iff` (1 is generating iff s = 0; Shoup Exercise 18.1),
  `Sequence.minPoly_fib` (Fibonacci over ℚ: X² − X − 1), `Sequence.minPoly_geom` ((aⁿ) has
  X − a, which is `minpoly F a`), `Sequence.not_isLinearlyGenerated_two_pow_sq` (2^{n²} over ℚ is
  not linearly generated).
- **`PowerSeries.IsRational`** (definition, `rational-power-series`; Stanley §4.1). f ∈ K⟦X⟧ is
  rational when f·Q = P for polynomials with Q(0) ≠ 0; no degree normalisation. API:
  `IsRational.polynomial`, `IsRational.inv` (also when constantCoeff f = 0, where Mathlib's
  f⁻¹ = 0), `IsRational.subring`, `isRational_iff_mem_range` (f is rational iff its image in
  K⸨X⸩ comes from K(X), Stanley's K[[x]] ∩ K(x)), `isRational_mk_iff` (∑ aₙXⁿ is rational iff
  (aₙ) is linearly generated: a generating polynomial X^m h with h(0) ≠ 0 gives the denominator
  `reverse h`). Unit tests: `PowerSeries.isRational_mk_one` ((1 − X)∑Xⁿ = 1),
  `PowerSeries.mk_fib_mul` ((∑FₙXⁿ)(1 − X − X²) = X), `PowerSeries.not_isRational_exp`.
- **`LinearRecurrence.ofMonic`** (construction, `linear-recurrence-of-a-monic-polynomial`).
  API: `order_ofMonic`, `charPoly_ofMonic` (= p for monic p), `ofMonic_charPoly`
  (ofMonic E.charPoly = E over a nontrivial ring), `isSolution_ofMonic_iff` (over a field, u
  solves ofMonic p iff p is a generating polynomial of u). Unit tests:
  `LinearRecurrence.isSolution_ofMonic_fib`, `LinearRecurrence.isSolution_ofMonic_one` (only
  u = 0 solves the order-zero recurrence), `LinearRecurrence.isSolution_ofMonic_X` (u(n + 1) = 0
  for all n: a zero constant term admits δ₀, which is no exponential polynomial).
- **Lemma** `exponential-polynomial-solutions` (`LinearRecurrence.isSolution_pow_mul_geom`): over a
  field of characteristic 0, if (X − θ)^{j+1} divides E.charPoly then n ↦ nʲθⁿ solves E. Proof by
  Hasse derivatives: n ↦ C(n, k)θ^{n−k} gives (D^{(k)}(Xⁿ·charPoly))(θ) = 0 for k ≤ j, and
  nʲ = ∑ k!S(j, k)C(n, k).
- **Lemma** `exponential-polynomials-are-linearly-independent` (`exponentialPolynomial_eq_zero`):
  in characteristic 0, for a finite set S of nonzero θ, ∑_{θ∈S} g_θ(n)θⁿ = 0 for all n forces
  every g_θ = 0. Proof by the difference operators Δ_μu(n) = u(n + 1) − μu(n) (Δ_θ lowers the
  degree of g_θ, Δ_μ preserves the others); Stanley's partial-fraction argument is the same
  statement for generating functions. Both hypotheses are needed (0ⁿ and n^p − n over 𝔽_p).
- **Theorem (Evertse 8.17)** `closed-form-of-a-complex-linear-recurrence`
  (`LinearRecurrence.existsUnique_closedForm`): for E : LinearRecurrence ℂ with
  E.charPoly.coeff 0 ≠ 0 and a solution u, there is a unique g : ℂ → ℂ[X] with
  deg g_θ < rootMultiplicity θ E.charPoly for all θ and u(h) = ∑_{θ root} g_θ(h)θʰ for all h ≥ 0.
  In particular, for simple roots u(h) = ∑ g_θθʰ with constants, and u = 0 iff all g_θ = 0.
  Proof: ℂ is algebraically closed, so charPoly = ∏(X − θ)^{mult}; the order-many functions
  nʲθⁿ (j < mult) are independent solutions, hence a basis of the solution space of dimension
  `order` (`solSpace_rank`). The recurrence need not be minimal. This is the statement
  `DiophantineApproximationAndTranscendence:DT.2` imports for Skolem–Mahler–Lech.
- **Theorem** `closed-forms-satisfy-a-linear-recurrence` (`LinearRecurrence.isSolution_closedForm`):
  for finite S ⊂ ℂ and deg g_θ < e_θ, h ↦ ∑ g_θ(h)θʰ solves ofMonic (∏ (X − θ)^{e_θ}); the
  constant term is nonzero when 0 ∉ S (Evertse's "conversely").
- **Theorem (Stanley 4.1.1)** `rational-iff-linearly-generated` (`generatingFunction_tfae`): for
  Q = 1 + α₁x + ⋯ + α_dx^d ∈ ℂ[x], d ≥ 1, α_d ≠ 0, and f : ℕ → ℂ the following are equivalent:
  (i) ∑ f(n)xⁿ = P/Q with deg P < d; (ii) f(n + d) + α₁f(n + d − 1) + ⋯ + α_df(n) = 0 for all n;
  (iii) f(n) = ∑ᵢ Pᵢ(n)γᵢⁿ where Q = ∏(1 − γᵢx)^{dᵢ} with distinct nonzero γᵢ and
  deg Pᵢ < dᵢ. (i) ⇔ (ii) by comparing coefficients; (ii) is the recurrence of `reverse Q`
  (monic since Q(0) = 1, constant term α_d ≠ 0, roots the γᵢ), so (ii) ⇔ (iii) is the two
  closed-form theorems. With Q = (1 − x)² the closed form is a + bn: constant coefficients are
  wrong for repeated roots.

### 2B. Companion matrices

- **`Polynomial.companion p`** (construction, `companion-matrix`), indexed by Fin (natDegree p),
  over any commutative ring. API: `companion_apply`; `leftMulMatrix_root_eq_companion` (for
  monic p, the matrix of multiplication by the root of `AdjoinRoot p` in
  `AdjoinRoot.powerBasis'`); `toLin'_companion_transpose` (`Matrix.toLin'` of the transpose is
  `(ofMonic p).tupleSucc`, Mathlib's state shift; no cast because (ofMonic p).order is
  natDegree p by definition); `det_companion` (det = (−1)^{natDegree p}·p.coeff 0 for monic p);
  `minpoly_companion` (over a field, minpoly = p: companion matrices are non-derogatory);
  `hasEigenvalue_companion_iff` (over a field, q is an eigenvalue iff p(q) = 0). Unit tests:
  `Polynomial.companion_fib` (C(X² − X − 1) = [[0, 1], [1, 1]] over ℤ),
  `Polynomial.companion_quadratic` (C(X² − tX + d) = [[0, −d], [1, t]], the body of
  `TauCeti.companionFinTwo t d`), `Polynomial.charpoly_companion_one` (C(1) is empty with
  charpoly 1), `Polynomial.companion_ne_transpose` (C(X² + 1) is not symmetric: the orientation
  is part of the definition).
- **Theorem** `characteristic-polynomial-of-the-companion-matrix` (`Polynomial.charpoly_companion`):
  for every commutative ring and monic p, charpoly C(p) = p. Induction on the degree, expanding
  det(X·I − C(p)) along the first row (Hefferon's Exercise 5 expands down the last column of the
  transposed matrix); over a field also `charpoly_leftMulMatrix`.
- **Theorem** `linear-recurrence-as-a-matrix-power`
  (`LinearRecurrence.isSolution_iff_state_eq_pow_mulVec`): for natDegree p ≥ 1, u solves
  ofMonic p iff (u(n), …, u(n + d − 1)) = (C(p)ᵀ)ⁿ(u(0), …, u(d − 1)) for all n; u(n) is the
  first coordinate. For d = 0 the equivalence fails (only u = 0 solves ofMonic 1).
- **Lemma** `roots-are-eigenvalues-of-the-companion-matrix`
  (`Polynomial.isRoot_iff_companion_transpose_mulVec`): for monic p of degree ≥ 1 and q ∈ R,
  p(q) = 0 iff C(p)ᵀ(1, q, …, q^{d−1}) = q(1, q, …, q^{d−1}): eigenvalues are taken in the base
  ring, never after extending scalars (over ℚ, C(X² − 2) has none).

### 2C. Lucas sequences

- **`lucasU P Q`** (definition, `lucas-sequence-of-the-first-kind`): U₀ = 0, U₁ = 1,
  U_{n+2} = PU_{n+1} − QUₙ over any commutative ring (Carmichael's Dₙ = (αⁿ − βⁿ)/(α − β)).
  API: `lucasU_zero`, `lucasU_one`, `lucasU_add_two`, `isSolution_lucasU` (solves ⟨2, ![−Q, P]⟩ =
  ofMonic (X² − PX + Q)), `lucasU_mul_sub` ((α − β)Uₙ = αⁿ − βⁿ when α + β = P, αβ = Q),
  `map_lucasU`, `lucasU_succ_eq_eval_dickson` (U_{n+1} = D_n^{(2)}(P, Q), Mathlib's
  `dickson 2 Q n` at P), `lucasU_two_mul` (U_{2n} = UₙVₙ), `lucasU_one_neg_one` (= `Nat.fib`).
  Unit tests: `lucasU_three_two` (Uₙ(3, 2) = 2ⁿ − 1), `lucasU_two_one` (Uₙ(2, 1) = n, the
  repeated root), `lucasU_fib`, `lucasU_zero_one_two` (U₂(0, 1) = 0, U₃(0, 1) = −1: terms can
  vanish).
- **`lucasV P Q`** (definition, `lucas-sequence-of-the-second-kind`): V₀ = 2, V₁ = P, same
  recursion (Carmichael's Sₙ = αⁿ + βⁿ). API: `lucasV_zero`, `lucasV_one`, `lucasV_add_two`,
  `lucasV_eq_add_pow`, `lucasV_eq_eval_dickson` (Vₙ = `dickson 1 Q n` at P), `lucasV_sq_sub`
  (Vₙ² − (P² − 4Q)Uₙ² = 4Qⁿ), `map_lucasV`. Unit tests: `lucasV_three_two` (2ⁿ + 1),
  `lucasV_two_one` (constantly 2), `lucasV_lucas_numbers` (2, 1, 3, 4, 7, 11),
  `lucasV_dickson`, `lucasV_lucasLehmer` (V_{2^i}(4, 1) = `LucasLehmer.s i`).
- **Lemma** `lucas-addition-formula` (`lucasU_add_add_one`): U_{m+n+1} = U_{m+1}U_{n+1} − QU_mUₙ
  (for Fibonacci, `Nat.fib_add`).
- **Theorem** `lucas-sequences-are-divisibility-sequences` (`lucasU_mul`): U_{mn}(P, Q) =
  U_m(P, Q)·Uₙ(V_m(P, Q), Q^m) over every commutative ring, proved in the universal ring
  ℤ[P, Q][α]/(α² − Pα + Q) and specialised; hence n ↦ Uₙ is an `IsDvdSequence`.
- **Lemma** `coprimality-of-lucas-sequences` (`isCoprime_lucasU`): for coprime P, Q ∈ ℤ, U_{n+1}
  is coprime to Q and to Uₙ.
- **Theorem** `strong-divisibility-of-lucas-sequences` (`gcd_lucasU`): for coprime P, Q ∈ ℤ and
  all m, n, gcd(U_m, Uₙ) = |U_{gcd(m, n)}|, that is n ↦ |Uₙ| is `Nat.IsStrongDvdSequence`.
  Degenerate cases (α/β a root of unity, P = 0 or Q = 0) are included; coprimality is needed
  (P = Q = 2: gcd(U₂, U₃) = 2).

### 2D. Euler numbers and analytic specialisations

- **`zigzag`** (definition, `euler-zigzag-numbers`): E₀ = E₁ = 1 and
  E_{n+1} = ∑_{1≤j≤n, j odd} C(n, j)E_jE_{n−j} (Stanley's Note to Proposition 1.6.1), the
  secant (even index) and tangent (odd index) numbers. API: `zigzagPowerSeries A`
  (∑ E_nXⁿ/n! over a ℚ-algebra), `eulerNumber` (DLMF's signed numbers), `zigzag_odd_eq_bernoulli`
  (E_{2n+1} = (−1)ⁿ2^{2n+2}(2^{2n+2} − 1)B_{2n+2}/(2n + 2)). Unit tests: `zigzag_values`
  (1, 1, 1, 2, 5, 16, 61, 272), `zigzag_three_bernoulli` (E₃ = 2 against B₄ = −1/30),
  `eulerNumber_two` (𝐄₂ = −1 while E₂ = 1).
- **Theorem (André)** `andre-alternating-permutations` (`card_alternating_perm`): E_n is the
  number of permutations w of n letters with w(1) > w(2) < w(3) > ⋯ (descent set
  {1, 3, 5, …} ∩ [n − 1]); by the position of the letter 1.
- **Theorem (Stanley 1.6.1, formal)** `secant-plus-tangent-formal-identity`
  (`zigzagPowerSeries_mul_cos`): (∑ E_nXⁿ/n!)·cos X = 1 + sin X in A⟦X⟧, i.e. the series is
  sec X + tan X, its even part sec X and odd part tan X; equivalently 2y′ = y² + 1, whose
  coefficients are 2E_{n+1} = ∑ C(n, k)E_kE_{n−k} (n ≥ 1). Proof by the parity system
  O′ = 1 + O², E′ = OE and uniqueness of formal solutions.
- **`taylorPowerSeries f`** (construction, `taylor-series-of-a-complex-function`):
  ∑ f⁽ⁿ⁾(0)/n!·Xⁿ ∈ ℂ⟦X⟧. API: `coeff_taylorPowerSeries`, `taylorPowerSeries_add`,
  `taylorPowerSeries_mul` (for f, g analytic at 0, by `iteratedDeriv_mul`),
  `taylorPowerSeries_const`, `taylorPowerSeries_id` (= X), `taylorPowerSeries_exp`,
  `taylorPowerSeries_sin`, `taylorPowerSeries_cos` (the formal `exp`, `sin`, `cos`),
  `eventuallyEq_zero_of_taylorPowerSeries_eq_zero` (identity theorem),
  `hasSum_taylorPowerSeries` (convergence on a disc of holomorphy). Unit tests:
  `taylorPowerSeries_geometric` (1/(1 − z) ↦ ∑ Xⁿ), `taylorPowerSeries_one`,
  `taylorPowerSeries_sq` (z² ↦ X², not 2X²).
- **Theorem (transfer)** `formal-identities-specialise-on-a-disc`
  (`hasSum_of_mul_taylorPowerSeries_eq`): if g, h are holomorphic on |z| < r with g ≠ 0 there
  and F·T(g) = T(h) in ℂ⟦X⟧, then ∑ Fₙzⁿ = h(z)/g(z) for |z| < r. This is the general
  formal-to-analytic transfer; the hypothesis g ≠ 0 on the disc cannot be dropped (∑ zⁿ at
  |z| = 1).
- **Theorem** `bernoulli-generating-function-on-its-disc` (`hasSum_bernoulli_mul_pow`): for
  |z| < 2π, ∑ Bₙzⁿ/n! = z/(e^z − 1) (1 at z = 0), from Mathlib's
  `bernoulliPowerSeries_mul_exp_sub_one`, g = dslope (exp − 1) 0 (zeros at 2πiℤ∖{0}) and the
  transfer theorem; the radius 2π is exact (DLMF 24.2.1).
- **Theorem** `secant-plus-tangent-on-its-disc` (`hasSum_zigzag_mul_pow`): for |z| < π/2,
  ∑ E_nzⁿ/n! = 1/cos z + tan z (DLMF 4.19.3, 4.19.5); the radius is exact (pole at π/2).

### 2E. Radix expansions

- **`PadicInt.digitsEquiv`** (construction, `p-adic-digit-expansion`): ℤ_p ≃ (ℕ → Fin p),
  x = ∑ dᵢpⁱ (Milne, Proposition 7.26). API: `hasSum_digitsEquiv`, `sum_digitsEquiv_eq_appr`
  (∑_{i<n} dᵢpⁱ = `appr x n`), `digitsEquiv_apply` (dᵢ = (`toZModPow (i+1) x`).val / pⁱ),
  `digitsEquiv_symm_apply`, `continuous_digitsEquiv` (a homeomorphism for the product
  topology). Unit tests: `PadicInt.digitsEquiv_neg_one` (all digits p − 1),
  `PadicInt.digitsEquiv_natCast` (the digits of n are `Nat.digits p n` padded by zeros),
  `PadicInt.digitsEquiv_zero`, `PadicInt.digitsEquiv_not_additive` (in ℤ₂, 1 + 1 has digit 1
  in position 1).
- **Lemma** `canonical-real-expansion-is-recovered` (`Real.digits_ofDigits`): if b ≥ 2 and d is
  not eventually b − 1, then `Real.digits (Real.ofDigits d) b = d`.
- **Theorem** `uniqueness-of-base-b-expansions` (`Real.ofDigits_eq_ofDigits_iff`): two digit
  sequences have equal value iff they are equal, or they agree before a position n where one
  digit exceeds the other by 1 and the tails are all 0 in the first and all b − 1 in the second
  (0.1 = 0.0111… in base 2).

### 2F. Automatic sequences

- **`DFAO`** (definition, `automata-with-output`): transition `step`, `start`, `output`, run
  from the head of a list (`evalFrom`, `eval`). API: `evalFrom_append`, `toDFA` (the Mathlib
  `DFA` accepting output⁻¹(S)), `eval_toDFA`, `mem_accepts_toDFA`, `mapOutput`, `prod`. Unit
  tests: `DFAO.parity_eval`, `DFAO.eval_nil`, `DFAO.toDFA_accepts`.
- **`IsAutomatic k a`** (definition, `automatic-sequences`): some DFAO with a `Fintype` of states
  over the alphabet Fin k computes a(n) from `Nat.digits k n` read least significant first. API:
  `IsAutomatic.map`, `IsAutomatic.prod`, `IsAutomatic.finite_range`, `isAutomatic_const`,
  `isAutomatic_of_eventually_periodic`. Unit tests: `isAutomatic_const_two`,
  `not_isAutomatic_id` (n ↦ n has infinite range), `isAutomatic_parity`.
- **`kernel k a`** (definition, `k-kernel`). API: `self_mem_kernel`, `kernel_subset`. Unit tests:
  `kernel_const`, `kernel_parity` ({n mod 2, 0, 1}), `kernel_thueMorse_eq_pair`,
  `kernel_two_mul_add_one_mem`.
- **Theorem (Eilenberg)** `automatic-iff-finite-kernel` (`isAutomatic_iff_finite_kernel`): for
  k ≥ 2, a is k-automatic iff its k-kernel is finite; the kernel automaton (states the kernel,
  output b ↦ b(0)) reads least significant digit first and ignores padding zeros.
- **Theorem** `automaticity-is-independent-of-reading-direction` (`isAutomatic_iff_reverse`):
  the same class arises when the most significant digit is read first (states: functions from
  the old states to the outputs).
- **Theorem** `automatic-in-base-a-power` (`isAutomatic_pow_iff`): k^m-automatic iff
  k-automatic, for k ≥ 2, m ≥ 1.
- **Theorem (Cobham)** `automatic-iff-uniform-morphism` (`isAutomatic_iff_uniformMorphism`): a is
  k-automatic iff a = τ ∘ s for a fixed point s of a k-uniform morphism φ of a finite alphabet,
  encoded as s(kn + r) = φ(s n)_r.
- **`thueMorse`** (definition, `thue-morse-sequence`): t(n) = s₂(n) mod 2 in ZMod 2. API:
  `thueMorse_two_mul`, `thueMorse_two_mul_add_one`, `isAutomatic_thueMorse`, `kernel_thueMorse`
  ({t, 1 + t}), `thueMorse_algebraic` ((1 + X)³T² + (1 + X)²T + X = 0 in 𝔽₂⟦X⟧). Unit tests:
  `thueMorse_values` (0, 1, 1, 0, 1, 0, 0, 1), `thueMorse_two_pow`,
  `thueMorse_not_eventually_periodic`.

### 2G. Christol's theorem

- **`LaurentSeries.cartier q r`** (definition, `cartier-operators`). API: `coeff_cartier`,
  `cartier_add`, `cartier_smul`, `cartier_ofPowerSeries` (power series go to the decimated power
  series), `cartier_pow_card_mul` (Λ_r(gᵠh) = gΛ_r(h) for q = |K|),
  `eq_sum_single_mul_cartier_pow` (f = ∑_{r<q} X^r(Λ_r f)^q), `natDegree_cartier_le`
  (deg Λ_r(P) ≤ deg P / q). Unit tests: `LaurentSeries.cartier_single`,
  `LaurentSeries.cartier_one_zero` (modulus 1 is the identity), `LaurentSeries.cartier_pow_card`
  (Λ₀(gᵠ) = g).
- **Lemma (Ore)** `ore-relation` (`exists_ore_relation`): an algebraic f ∈ 𝔽_q⟦X⟧ satisfies
  ∑_{i≤m} aᵢf^{qⁱ} = 0 with aᵢ ∈ 𝔽_q[X] and a₀ ≠ 0 (a nontrivial relation among f, f^q, f^{q²}, …,
  then Λ-reduction of the lowest index; CKMFR §7). For Thue–Morse:
  X(1 + X)²T + (1 + X)³T² + (1 + X)⁶T⁴ = 0.
- **Theorem** `algebraic-series-are-automatic` (`isAutomatic_of_isAlgebraic`): with g = f/a₀ ∈
  K⸨X⸩, g = ∑_{i≥1} bᵢg^{qⁱ} (bᵢ = −aᵢa₀^{qⁱ−2}), the finite set
  {∑_{i≤m} cᵢg^{qⁱ} : deg cᵢ ≤ N} contains f and is Λ-stable, so the q-kernel is finite.
- **Theorem** `automatic-series-are-algebraic` (`isAlgebraic_of_isAutomatic`): the kernel series
  y₁, …, y_m satisfy yᵢ ∈ ⟨y₁^q, …, y_m^q⟩ over K(X), so yᵢ, yᵢ^q, …, yᵢ^{q^m} are dependent
  (Bridy, Proposition 2.13); deg f ≤ q^m − 1.
- **Theorem (Christol)** `christol-theorem` (`christol`): for a finite field K of characteristic
  p (|K| = q = p^r) and a : ℕ → K, ∑ aₙXⁿ is algebraic over K(X) iff (aₙ) is p-automatic, iff it
  is q-automatic. Characteristic 0 is excluded: ∑ C(2n, n)Xⁿ is algebraic over ℚ(X) with
  unbounded coefficients.

### 2H. Farey sequences and the Stern–Brocot tree

- **`mediant`** (definition): (x.num + y.num)/(x.den + y.den). API: `mediant_comm`,
  `mediant_mem_Ioo`, `mediant_self`, `den_mediant_of_det` (if y.num·x.den − x.num·y.den = 1 the
  mediant is already reduced). Unit tests: `mediant_zero_one` (1/2), `mediant_third_twothirds`
  (3/6 = 1/2, denominator 2), `mediant_ne_average` (mediant(0, 1/2) = 1/3).
- **`fareySeq n`** (definition, `farey-sequence`). API: `mem_fareySeq` (0 ≤ x ≤ 1, n ≥ 1,
  x.den ≤ n), `card_fareySeq` (1 + ∑_{k≤n} φ(k)), `fareySeq_mono`, `one_sub_mem_fareySeq`. Unit
  tests: `fareySeq_three`, `card_fareySeq_seven` (19), `fareySeq_zero`, `fareySeq_one`.
- **Lemma** `denominators-between-farey-neighbours` (`den_ge_of_mem_Ioo`): if bc − ad = 1, every
  rational strictly between a/b and c/d has denominator ≥ b + d, with equality only for the
  mediant (y = d(bx − ay) + b(cy − dx)).
- **Theorem** `farey-neighbour-property` (`fareySeq_neighbours`): consecutive a/b < c/d in F_n
  satisfy bc − ad = 1 and b + d > n.
- **Theorem** `farey-mediant-property` (`fareySeq_mediant`): the middle of three consecutive terms
  is the reduced mediant of its neighbours (in F₇, 1/5, 1/4, 2/7 with 3/12 = 1/4).
- **`sternBrocot`** (construction, `stern-brocot-tree`) with `sternBrocotBounds`: the node along a
  path is the mediant of its bounds, starting from (0/1, 1/0). API: `sternBrocotBounds_det`
  (cb − ad = 1), `den_sternBrocot` (lowest terms, denominator b + d),
  `sternBrocot_lt_append_true`, `sternBrocot_append_false_lt` (in-order). Unit tests:
  `sternBrocot_nil` (1), `sternBrocot_small` (1/2, 2, 2/3), `sternBrocot_ne_three_sixths`.
- **Theorem** `stern-brocot-enumerates-positive-rationals` (`sternBrocot_bijective`): every
  positive rational occurs exactly once (Hatcher, Proposition 1.3 and the order of the labels);
  3/8 is the node left, left, right, left.

CA.2 further owns two targets, recorded here by their exact statements:
(a) **strong divisibility of elliptic divisibility sequences** — the statement (due to Ward, 1948) that a
nondegenerate integral EDS (W₀ = 0, W₁ = 1, W₂W₃ ≠ 0, W₂ ∣ W₄) with gcd(W₃, W₄) = 1 satisfies
gcd(W_m, Wₙ) = |W_{gcd(m,n)}|, extending Tau Ceti's `isDvdSequence_normEDS` (the coprimality
hypothesis is needed: 0, 1, 1, −6, −6, 210, … has gcd(W₃, W₄) = 6); (b) **Cobham's theorem** — a
sequence that is both k- and l-automatic for multiplicatively independent k, l ≥ 2 is eventually
periodic (public proof: T. Krebs, *A more reasonable proof of Cobham's theorem*,
arXiv:1801.06704), resting on `automatic-sequences` and `automatic-iff-finite-kernel`. The
packet's coverage record names the sources from which each is decomposed.

### Dependencies

- Inside the roadmap: `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.0` (the stage edge: gcd, coprimality,
  digits of naturals are Mathlib's). Consumers: `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3` (the
  rational canonical form is the block sum of `Polynomial.companion` of the invariant factors;
  CA.3 imports `companion-matrix` and `characteristic-polynomial-of-the-companion-matrix`),
  `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4` (Pell solutions yₙ = y₁Uₙ(2x₁, 1), xₙ = Vₙ(2x₁, 1)/2).
- Other roadmaps (consumers): `DiophantineApproximationAndTranscendence:DT.2` imports
  `closed-form-of-a-complex-linear-recurrence` and
  `exponential-polynomials-are-linearly-independent` for Skolem–Mahler–Lech;
  `DiophantineApproximationAndTranscendence:DT.5` uses rationality (the lacunary series) and
  automatic series as the model Mahler functions; `QSeriesPartitionsAndMockModularForms:QM.0`
  and `WeilConjectures:WC.5:power-sum-converse` use rational generating functions and the
  formal-to-analytic transfer; `DirichletPadicLFunctions:L0` applies the Mellin argument to the
  Bernoulli generating function whose Taylor expansion on |z| < 2π is proved here;
  `AdditiveCombinatorics:AC.0` is a recorded stage consumer.
- No input from another roadmap is needed; there are no requests.

### Acceptance

- Fibonacci: minimal polynomial X² − X − 1, series x/(1 − x − x²), companion matrix [[0, 1], [1, 1]],
  Uₙ(1, −1) = Fₙ, gcd(F_m, Fₙ) = F_{gcd(m,n)} agreeing with Mathlib.
- Repeated roots: Q = (1 − x)² gives f(n) = a + bn; a closed form with constant coefficients
  fails.
- Evertse's example uₕ = 10u_{h−1} − 31u_{h−2} + 30u_{h−3}, (1, 0, −12) is 2ʰ + 3ʰ − 5ʰ.
- Rank-two companion matrices agree with `TauCeti.companionFinTwo`; charpoly C(p) = p over ℤ.
- Uₙ(3, 2) = 2ⁿ − 1 and gcd(2^m − 1, 2ⁿ − 1) = 2^{gcd(m,n)} − 1; V_{2^i}(4, 1) = `LucasLehmer.s i`.
- E₀…E₇ = 1, 1, 1, 2, 5, 16, 61, 272; E_n counts alternating permutations (5 for n = 4);
  E₃ = 2 = −2⁴(2⁴ − 1)B₄/4.
- ∑ Bₙzⁿ/n! = z/(e^z − 1) on |z| < 2π and ∑ E_nzⁿ/n! = sec z + tan z on |z| < π/2, both radii
  exact; each is a separate theorem from its formal identity.
- The digits of −1 ∈ ℤ_p are all p − 1; 1/2 = 0.5 = 0.4999… is the only kind of non-uniqueness
  of real expansions.
- Thue–Morse: 2-automatic, kernel {t, 1 + t}, (1 + X)³T² + (1 + X)²T + X = 0 over 𝔽₂, not
  eventually periodic.
- |F₇| = 19; consecutive Farey fractions have determinant 1; every positive rational occurs once
  in the Stern–Brocot tree.

### Planets

*Minimal polynomial of a sequence* (`generating-polynomials-and-the-minimal-polynomial`),
*Rational generating functions* (`rational-iff-linearly-generated`), *Lucas sequences*
(`lucas-sequence-of-the-first-kind`), *Euler numbers* (`euler-zigzag-numbers`), *Christol's
theorem* (`christol-theorem`), *Farey sequences* (`farey-sequence`).

### Mistakes in the sources

- Evertse, p. 173 (E301): the constants of the example are printed as "c₁ = 1, c₂ = 0, c₂ = −12"
  (the initial values); they are c₁ = 1, c₂ = 1, c₃ = −1. Evertse, p. 172 (E302): "|u_h| ⩽ C^h
  for all h ⩾ 0" fails at h = 0; read C^{h+1}.
- Stanley, proof of Theorem 4.1.1, p. 536 (E303, corrected in the author's errata): "polynomial in
  n of degree j" should be "degree j − 1"; (E304) "for j ≥ 0" should be "for j ≥ 1".
- Bala, Appendix, p. 8 (E305): the restated initial values "S(0) = 1" should be S(0) = 0.
- PlanetMath, §2.2 (E306): "(b − 1)b²/(1 − b^{−1})" should be "(b − 1)b^{−2}/(1 − b^{−1})".
- Hefferon, Topic: Linear Recurrences (E307, E308): Exercise 6's "f_{r_i}(n) = r_kⁿ" should read
  r_iⁿ, and the Fibonacci system on p. 478 should have coefficients (1 ± √5)/2.

---

## CA.3 Polynomial and matrix arithmetic

**Coverage: partial.** The general matrix normal forms, the invariant-factor theory they rest on, the ring of integer-valued polynomials and four irreducibility criteria are planned from public sources; two items remain, listed below.

**Remaining in this layer:**

- Int(𝓞_K) beyond its definition and the regular-basis criterion: the local formula for the characteristic ideals, the theorem that Int(𝓞_K) has a regular basis exactly when every factorial ideal n!_{𝓞_K} is principal, and the Pólya group. The public survey read states these without proof.
- The equality case of Perron's criterion (|a_{n−1}| ≥ 1 + |a_{n−2}| + ⋯ + |a_0| together with f(±1) ≠ 0). The public sources read state and prove only the strict inequality.

### Scope

This layer builds the general normal forms of integer matrices and of matrices over a principal ideal domain, each with a certificate that can be checked by matrix multiplication: the **Smith normal form** (invertible P, Q with explicit inverses, the reconstruction equation P A Q = D and the divisibility chain d_0 | d_1 | ⋯), the **Hermite normal form** (a unimodular U with explicit inverse, A U = [H | 0] with H triangular and reduced) and the **rational canonical form** of an endomorphism (a basis in which the matrix is block diagonal with companion blocks of the invariant factors). Over ℤ the Smith and Hermite forms are given by terminating algorithms whose decreasing measures are part of the specification. Around them sit the invariant-factor structure theorem over a principal ideal domain with uniqueness, the cokernel of an integer matrix, and the classification of matrices up to similarity.

The layer also builds the ring **Int(D)** of integer-valued polynomials with Pólya's theorem, the consecutive-values criterion, the failure of the Noetherian property, the irreducibility of the binomial polynomials and the characterisation of regular bases; and the irreducibility criteria that the pinned libraries lack: **Capelli's theorem** for Xⁿ − a in every characteristic and every n (including 4 | n and n a power of 2), **Perron's criterion**, **Cohn's criterion** in every base, and **Newton polygons** over a valued field with Dumas's theorem, the root-valuation theorem and the Eisenstein–Dumas criterion.

Resultants and discriminants of polynomials, Eisenstein's criterion, Gauss's lemma, the irreducibility of cyclotomic and Selmer polynomials, the elementary-divisor form of the structure theorem and the special normal forms already in the libraries are used, not rebuilt; trinomial discriminants and resultants of binary forms are not targets of this layer. Algorithms with complexity bounds and effective ideal arithmetic belong to `ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.0` to `ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.2`, which import the normal-form theorems from here. The comparison between the ideals of a number field and their Hermite matrices is in `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5`. Companion matrices of recurrences are in `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.2`; the companion block used here is the same matrix.

### Conventions

- An integer matrix A with m rows and n columns acts on column vectors. Its **lattice** is the column span A ℤⁿ ⊆ ℤᵐ (Mathlib `Matrix.mulVecLin`). Transformations that preserve the lattice act on the right. A unimodular matrix is an invertible integer matrix, and every certificate records the inverse explicitly, so unimodularity is checked by multiplying, not by computing a determinant.
- **Smith normal form.** P A Q = D_{m,n}(d), where D_{m,n}(d) has d_i in position (i, i) for i < r and zeros elsewhere; the d_i are positive integers with d_i | d_j for i ≤ j. The list (d_0, …, d_{r−1}) is `smithInvariantFactors A`; r is the rank of A over ℚ.
- **Hermite normal form.** Column-style and upper: the pivot of column j is its lowest nonzero entry, in row p(j); the pivot rows strictly increase from left to right; pivots are positive; every entry to the right of a pivot in its row lies in [0, pivot). A Hermite basis has no zero columns; in A U = [H | 0] the zero columns come last. For a square nonsingular matrix this is an upper triangular matrix with positive diagonal and each row reduced modulo its diagonal entry to the right of it. Stein's exercise uses rows (the transpose); Micciancio's lecture 4 and Damer's survey use the mirror image obtained by reversing rows and columns.
- **Invariant factors** over a principal ideal domain R are nonzero nonunits a_1 | a_2 | ⋯ | a_k, unique up to units; over ℤ they are taken positive, over K[X] monic. The invariant factors of an endomorphism T of a finite-dimensional K-vector space are those of the K[X]-module V_T (Mathlib `Module.AEval'`).
- **Companion block** of a monic f of degree d: the matrix of multiplication by X on K[X]/(f) in the basis 1, X, …, X^{d−1}, that is ones on the subdiagonal and −coeff_i(f) in row i of the last column (Mathlib `Algebra.leftMulMatrix` of the power basis `AdjoinRoot.powerBasis'` at the root). The rational canonical form lists the blocks in increasing divisibility.
- **Int(D)** for an integral domain D with fraction field K is {f ∈ K[X] : f(D) ⊆ D}, a D-subalgebra of K[X]. The binomial polynomials are (X choose n) = X(X − 1)⋯(X − n + 1)/n! (Mathlib `Ring.choose X n`). The n-th characteristic ideal I_n(D) is the set of leading coefficients of the degree-n elements of Int(D), with 0.
- **Newton polygons** over a field with an additive valuation v to ℝ ∪ {∞}. The sloped valuation is v_r(f) = min_i (v(a_i) + r i). The multiplicity m_r(f) of the slope r is the distance between the least and the greatest index attaining v_r(f). The orientation is pinned so that slopes are valuations of roots: X − c has the single slope v(c). In the usual drawing with the points (i, v(a_i)) this is the negative of the geometric slope.

### What the pinned libraries already contain

- Tau Ceti: the Smith normal form of a square integer matrix with nonzero determinant (`Matrix.exists_smith_normal_form_of_det_ne_zero`), the uniqueness of nonnegative chained diagonals in a GL_n(ℤ)-equivalence class (`Matrix.smith_normal_form_unique`), the first invariant factor as the gcd of the entries (`Matrix.invariant_factor_zero_eq_gcd`), and the Smith form of the Gram matrix of an integral lattice (`TauCeti.IntegralLattice.exists_gramSmithInvariantFactors_smith_normal_form`).
- Mathlib: aligned bases of a submodule of a free module over a PID without a divisibility chain (`Submodule.exists_smith_normal_form_of_le`, `Ideal.smithCoeffs`), and the structure theorem in elementary-divisor form (`Module.equiv_free_prod_directSum`).
- Tau Ceti: the rational canonical form of a non-scalar 2 × 2 matrix (`TauCeti.exists_det_ne_zero_mul_eq_mul_companionFinTwo`).
- Tau Ceti: the binomial polynomials as a basis of K[X] (`TauCeti.binomialPolynomialBasis`) and as a ℤ-basis of their span (`TauCeti.ringChooseSpanBasisX`), closure of that span under multiplication (`TauCeti.mul_mem_ringChooseSpan`), and integral interpolation (`TauCeti.exists_forall_sum_mul_choose_eq`). The ring Int(ℤ) itself and Pólya's theorem are not stated.
- Mathlib: Capelli's theorem for odd n (`X_pow_sub_C_irreducible_of_odd`) and for a prime exponent (`X_pow_sub_C_irreducible_of_prime`), with the tower step `X_pow_mul_sub_C_irreducible`; Eisenstein's criterion, Gauss's lemma, the irreducibility of cyclotomic polynomials and of Xⁿ − X − 1.
- Mathlib and Tau Ceti: resultants and discriminants with their product formulas (`Polynomial.resultant`, `Polynomial.discr`), used and not rebuilt; Tau Ceti's Rouché theorem (`TauCeti.rouche_add`), used for Perron's criterion.

### Dependencies

Inside the roadmap: `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.2` (companion matrices; recurrences). Consumers: `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4` (integer linear systems through the Smith form), `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5` (class groups through the Smith form of a relation matrix; ideals through Hermite matrices), `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.6`, `ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.0` and through it `ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.1`, `ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.4` and `EffectiveDiophantineMethods:ED.0`, `FiniteFieldsAndCharacterSums:FF.0`, `GeometryOfNumbersAndQuadraticArithmetic:GN.5` and `ArithmeticStatistics:ST.1`.

### Acceptance tests of the layer

- A normal-form algorithm returns invertible change-of-basis matrices with their inverses and the reconstruction equation, with a termination measure that decreases at every step, and with the divisibility chain (Smith) or the reducedness conditions (Hermite).
- The Smith invariant factors of diag(2, 3) are (1, 6); of the matrix with rows (2, 4, 6), (3, 6, 9) they are (1); of the zero matrix there are none. Any two certificates agree.
- The Hermite basis of the column lattice of the matrix with columns (1, 2, 3), (4, 5, 6), (7, 8, 9) has columns (2, 1, 0) and (−3, 0, 3); the lattice with basis (3, 0), (5, 1) has Hermite basis (3, 0), (2, 1); the index of a full lattice is the product of the pivots.
- The identity of ℚ² has invariant factors X − 1, X − 1 and is not similar to the matrix with rows (1, 1), (0, 1), whose invariant factor is (X − 1)², although the characteristic polynomials agree.
- (X³ − X)/3 = 2 (X choose 3) + 2 (X choose 2) lies in Int(ℤ); X(X − 1)/4 is integer-valued at 0 and 1 but not at 2; Int(ℤ) is not Noetherian.
- X⁴ + 4 = (X² + 2X + 2)(X² − 2X + 2) while X⁴ + 1 and X⁸ − 2 are irreducible over ℚ; X³ + 5X² + X + 1 is irreducible by Perron's criterion; the digits of 397 give the irreducible 3X² + 9X + 7; X⁴ − p² is reducible and X² − p irreducible by the Eisenstein–Dumas criterion.

**Planets of this layer** (6): *Smith normal form*, *Hermite normal form*, *Rational canonical form*, *Pólya's theorem on integer-valued polynomials*, *Capelli's theorem*, *Dumas's theorem on Newton polygons*.

### Nodes (37)

#### `smith-normal-form-certificate` — The Smith normal form certificate of an integer matrix

*definition.*

**Statement.** For an integer matrix A with m rows and n columns, a Smith normal form certificate consists of integer matrices P, P′ (m × m) and Q, Q′ (n × n) with P P′ = P′ P = 1 and Q Q′ = Q′ Q = 1, a natural number r ≤ min(m, n) and positive integers d_0, …, d_{r−1} with d_i | d_j for i ≤ j, such that the reconstruction equation P A Q = D_{m,n}(d) holds, where D_{m,n}(d) is the m × n matrix with d_i in position (i, i) for i < r and zeros elsewhere.
