#### `positive-markoff-triples` — The positive Markoff triples M

*definition.*

**Statement.** M = {x ∈ X(ℤ) : x₀ > 0, x₁ > 0, x₂ > 0}, the triples of strictly positive integers with x₀² + x₁² + x₂² = 3x₀x₁x₂, kept as ordered triples (not up to permutation).

**Hypotheses.**

- Strict positivity of every coordinate; the signed integer solutions (such as (−1, −1, 1)) are not in M.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Define M as a subset of X(ℤ) cut out by positivity of the three coordinates.
2. The natural-number form: x ∈ M iff x = (a, b, c) with a, b, c ∈ ℕ positive and a² + b² + c² = 3abc in ℕ, since the equation has no subtraction.

**Acceptance.**

- (1, 1, 1), (1, 1, 2), (1, 2, 1), (2, 1, 1), (1, 2, 5), (2, 5, 29) ∈ M.
- (−1, −1, 1) ∈ X(ℤ) ∖ M and (0, 0, 0) ∈ X(ℤ) ∖ M.

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `positiveMarkoffTriples` | data | M ⊆ ℤ³ (as Fin 3 → ℤ). |
| `positiveMarkoffTriples.mem_iff` | characterisation | x ∈ M ↔ x ∈ X(ℤ) ∧ ∀ i, 0 < x i. |
| `positiveMarkoffTriples.subset_markoffSet` | coercion | M ⊆ X(ℤ), and in fact M ⊆ X*(ℤ). |
| `positiveMarkoffTriples.comp_perm_mem_iff` | relation | For σ ∈ S₃: x ∘ σ⁻¹ ∈ M ↔ x ∈ M. |
| `positiveMarkoffTriples.mem_iff_nat` | compatibility | x ∈ M ↔ ∃ a b c : ℕ, 0 < a ∧ 0 < b ∧ 0 < c ∧ a² + b² + c² = 3abc ∧ x = (a, b, c). |

**Uses.**

- Martin, §1: the integer Markoff graph whose tree property is recalled; Baragar's conjecture is surjectivity of reduction from M
- Bourgain–Gamburd–Sarnak, §1: M = Γ·(1, 1, 1) and strong approximation is surjectivity of M → X*(p)
- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/markoff-tree: the vertex set of the tree
- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/markoff-strong-approximation: the source of the reduction map

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `positiveMarkoffTriples_examples` (computation): (1, 1, 1), (1, 1, 2), (1, 2, 5), (2, 5, 29) and (5, 13, 194) lie in M.
- `neg_neg_one_not_mem_positiveMarkoffTriples` (non-example): (−1, −1, 1) ∈ X(ℤ) but (−1, −1, 1) ∉ M.
- `zero_not_mem_positiveMarkoffTriples` (degenerate): (0, 0, 0) ∉ M.
- `one_one_three_not_mem_positiveMarkoffTriples` (non-example): (1, 1, 3) ∉ M: 11 ≠ 9.

**Prerequisites.** `markoff-triples`

**Sources.**

- bgs-markoff-2016 — Jean Bourgain, Alexander Gamburd and Peter Sarnak, *Markoff surfaces and strong approximation: 1*, §1, p. 1.

  > The Markoff triples M are the positive integer solutions to (1). Let Γ be a group of affine integral morphisms of A³ generated by the permutations of the coordiantes and the Vieta involutions R₁, R₂, R₃, where R₃(x₁, x₂, x₃) = (x₁, x₂, 3x₁x₂ − x₃)

  The set M, the moves R_i and the coordinate permutations.

- martin-markoff-2025 — Daniel E. Martin, *A new proof of Chen's theorem for Markoff graphs*, §1, equation (1.1), arXiv v1 p. 1 (published version p. 623).

  > Integer solutions to the equation x₁² + x₂² + x₃² = 3x₁x₂x₃ (1.1) are known as Markoff triples.

  The coefficient-three equation defining X(R); the source works over ℤ and 𝔽_p and says any ring works.

#### `markoff-vieta-involution` — The Vieta moves R₀, R₁, R₂ and the integer Markoff graph

*construction.*

**Statement.** For a commutative ring R and i ∈ Fin 3, R_i : R³ → R³ replaces the i-th coordinate by 3x_{i+1}x_{i+2} − x_i and keeps the other two (indices mod 3). The integer Markoff graph is the simple graph on M in which x and y are adjacent iff x ≠ y and y = R_i x for some i.

**Hypotheses.**

- R commutative; the moves are defined on all of R³, not only on X(R).
- Indices are taken in Fin 3 (the source's R₁, R₂, R₃ are R₀, R₁, R₂ here).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Define R_i x = Function.update x i (3 · x(i + 1) · x(i + 2) − x i).
2. Evaluation: (R_i x)_i = 3x_{i+1}x_{i+2} − x_i and (R_i x)_j = x_j for j ≠ i; hence (R_i x)_i + x_i = 3x_{i+1}x_{i+2}, the sum of the two roots of the quadratic in the i-th variable.
3. Naturality: for a ring homomorphism f, f ∘ R_i x = R_i (f ∘ x), because R_i is given by an integer polynomial.
4. The graph: adjacency is the symmetrisation of y = R_i x on M, which is symmetric already by involutivity (markoff-vieta-involutive); M is preserved by markoff-vieta-preserves-positivity.

**Acceptance.**

- R₂(1, 1, 1) = (1, 1, 2), R₀(2, 1, 1) = (1, 1, 1), R₂(1, 2, 5) = (1, 2, 1).
- R_i(0) = 0 in every ring.
- The graph has no loops, since adjacency requires x ≠ y; that no move fixes a vertex of M is shown in markoff-tree.

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `markoffVieta` | data | R_i : R³ → R³. |
| `markoffVieta_apply_self` | simp | (R_i x)_i = 3 x_{i+1} x_{i+2} − x_i. |
| `markoffVieta_apply_of_ne` | simp | (R_i x)_j = x_j for j ≠ i. |
| `markoffVieta_add_self` | relation | (R_i x)_i + x_i = 3 x_{i+1} x_{i+2}. |
| `markoffVieta_zero` | simp | R_i 0 = 0. |
| `markoffVieta_map` | functoriality | For a ring homomorphism f : R → S, f ∘ (R_i x) = R_i (f ∘ x). |
| `markoffGraph` | data | The simple graph on M with x ~ y iff x ≠ y and y = R_i x for some i. |
| `markoffGraph_adj_iff` | characterisation | x ~ y in the Markoff graph ↔ x ≠ y ∧ ∃ i, R_i x = y. |

**Uses.**

- Martin, §2, equations (2.3)–(2.4): each edge of G_p comes from one coordinate move, and the Penner weights of the two endpoints of an R_i-edge sum to 1 in coordinate i
- Bourgain–Gamburd–Sarnak, §1: the generators of Γ together with the coordinate permutations
- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/markoff-root-generation: every element of M is a word in the moves applied to (1, 1, 1)
- ArithmeticDynamics Part II (PAPER-MARTIN-25 items 14–16): the Vieta group V_R and the labelled moves of the Markoff graph modulo p

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `markoffVieta_two_ones` (computation): R₂(1, 1, 1) = (1, 1, 2) and R₀(1, 1, 1) = (2, 1, 1) in ℤ³.
- `markoffVieta_two_one_two_five` (non-example): R₂(1, 2, 5) = (1, 2, 1), not the sign change (1, 2, −5).
- `markoffVieta_zero_eq` (degenerate): R_i(0, 0, 0) = (0, 0, 0) for each i.
- `markoffVieta_zmod_three` (computation): Over 𝔽₃, R₂(1, 1, 1) = (1, 1, −1): the move is a sign change of one coordinate there.
- `markoffVieta_cast_compat` (compatibility): R₂ commutes with reduction modulo 7 on (2, 5, 29): R₂(2, 5, 29) = (2, 5, 1) in ℤ³, and R₂ applied to (2, 5, 29) reduced modulo 7 is (2, 5, 1) in 𝔽₇³.

**Prerequisites.** `markoff-triples`, `mathlib:Function.update`

**Sources.**

- martin-markoff-2025 — Daniel E. Martin, *A new proof of Chen's theorem for Markoff graphs*, §1, equation (1.2), arXiv v1 p. 1 (published version p. 623).

  > Fixing x₂ and x₃ makes (1.1) a quadratic equation in x₁. The sum of the two solutions to this quadratic equation is 3x₂x₃. In other words, the map (x₁, x₂, x₃) ↦ (3x₂x₃ − x₁, x₂, x₃), (1.2) which is called a Vieta involution, preserves Markoff triples.

  The move R₁ and the reason it preserves the equation; R₂, R₃ are 'defined analogously'.

- bgs-markoff-2016 — Jean Bourgain, Alexander Gamburd and Peter Sarnak, *Markoff surfaces and strong approximation: 1*, §1, p. 1.

  > The Markoff triples M are the positive integer solutions to (1). Let Γ be a group of affine integral morphisms of A³ generated by the permutations of the coordiantes and the Vieta involutions R₁, R₂, R₃, where R₃(x₁, x₂, x₃) = (x₁, x₂, 3x₁x₂ − x₃)

  The set M, the moves R_i and the coordinate permutations.

#### `markoff-vieta-involutive` — The Vieta moves are involutions

*lemma.*

**Statement.** For every commutative ring R, every i ∈ Fin 3 and every x ∈ R³, R_i(R_i x) = x.

**Hypotheses.**

- R commutative; no hypothesis on x.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Only the i-th coordinate changes, and the other two coordinates of R_i x are those of x; so (R_i(R_i x))_i = 3x_{i+1}x_{i+2} − (3x_{i+1}x_{i+2} − x_i) = x_i.

**Acceptance.**

- In particular R_i is a bijection of R³ with inverse itself, with no field assumption.
- R₂R₂(1, 2, 5) = R₂(1, 2, 1) = (1, 2, 5).

**Prerequisites.** `markoff-vieta-involution`

**Sources.**

- martin-markoff-2025 — Daniel E. Martin, *A new proof of Chen's theorem for Markoff graphs*, §1, equation (1.2), arXiv v1 p. 1 (published version p. 623).

  > Fixing x₂ and x₃ makes (1.1) a quadratic equation in x₁. The sum of the two solutions to this quadratic equation is 3x₂x₃. In other words, the map (x₁, x₂, x₃) ↦ (3x₂x₃ − x₁, x₂, x₃), (1.2) which is called a Vieta involution, preserves Markoff triples.

  The move R₁ and the reason it preserves the equation; R₂, R₃ are 'defined analogously'.

---

#### `markoff-vieta-preserves-equation` — The Vieta moves preserve the Markoff equation

*lemma.*

**Statement.** For every commutative ring R, i ∈ Fin 3 and x ∈ R³: Φ(R_i x) = Φ(x), where Φ(x) = x₀² + x₁² + x₂² − 3x₀x₁x₂. In particular x ∈ X(R) ↔ R_i x ∈ X(R).

**Hypotheses.**

- R commutative.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Write u = x_i and c = x_{i+1}x_{i+2}; then Φ(x) = u² − 3cu + (x_{i+1}² + x_{i+2}²) and Φ(R_i x) is the same expression at u′ = 3c − u; the quadratic t² − 3ct + e takes equal values at u and 3c − u (a polynomial identity).

**Acceptance.**

- The polynomial identity holds before imposing Φ = 0, which is why it is valid over every ring.
- R₁ maps (1, 1, 2) ∈ X(ℤ) to (1, 5, 2) ∈ X(ℤ).

**Prerequisites.** `markoff-vieta-involution`, `markoff-triples`

**Sources.**

- martin-markoff-2025 — Daniel E. Martin, *A new proof of Chen's theorem for Markoff graphs*, §1, equation (1.2), arXiv v1 p. 1 (published version p. 623).

  > Fixing x₂ and x₃ makes (1.1) a quadratic equation in x₁. The sum of the two solutions to this quadratic equation is 3x₂x₃. In other words, the map (x₁, x₂, x₃) ↦ (3x₂x₃ − x₁, x₂, x₃), (1.2) which is called a Vieta involution, preserves Markoff triples.

  The move R₁ and the reason it preserves the equation; R₂, R₃ are 'defined analogously'.

---

#### `markoff-vieta-permutes-nonzero-locus` — The Vieta moves permute X*(R)

*lemma.*

**Statement.** For every commutative ring R and i ∈ Fin 3, R_i restricts to a bijection X*(R) → X*(R), equal to its own inverse.

**Hypotheses.**

- R commutative; no assumption that the coordinates of x are nonzero.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. R_i maps X(R) into X(R) (markoff-vieta-preserves-equation).
2. R_i(0) = 0 and R_i is injective (markoff-vieta-involutive), so R_i x = 0 only for x = 0; hence R_i maps X*(R) into X*(R).
3. Involutivity gives the inverse.

**Acceptance.**

- Over 𝔽₅, R₀ maps (0, 1, 2) ∈ X*(𝔽₅) to (1, 1, 2), which is again in X*(𝔽₅).

**Prerequisites.** `markoff-vieta-involutive`, `markoff-vieta-preserves-equation`

**Sources.**

- martin-markoff-2025 — Daniel E. Martin, *A new proof of Chen's theorem for Markoff graphs*, §1, arXiv v1 p. 1 (published version p. 623), the paragraph defining the Markoff graph.

  > The Markoff graph is defined to have one vertex for each nonzero triple (in 𝔽_p³ for our purpose, though any ring works) that solves (1.1), with an edge connecting two vertices if one triple is obtained from the other via a Vieta involution.

  The nonzero locus X*(R) and the edges given by the moves.

---

#### `markoff-permutation-equivariance` — Coordinate permutations normalise the Vieta moves

*lemma.*

**Statement.** For σ ∈ S₃ let P_σ x = x ∘ σ⁻¹, so that (P_σ x)_{σ(i)} = x_i. Then P_σ preserves X(R), X*(R) and M, and P_σ ∘ R_i = R_{σ(i)} ∘ P_σ for every i.

**Hypotheses.**

- R commutative; the convention (P_σ x)_{σ(i)} = x_i is pinned.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Φ is symmetric, so P_σ preserves X(R); P_σ fixes 0 and is bijective, so it preserves X*(R); it preserves positivity of all coordinates, so it preserves M.
2. Both sides of P_σ R_i x = R_{σ(i)} P_σ x agree at σ(i): the left gives (R_i x)_i = 3x_{i+1}x_{i+2} − x_i, and the right gives 3·x_{σ⁻¹(σ(i)+1)}x_{σ⁻¹(σ(i)+2)} − x_i, where {σ⁻¹(σ(i)+1), σ⁻¹(σ(i)+2)} = {i+1, i+2}.
3. At the other two coordinates both sides agree with P_σ x.

**Acceptance.**

- For the transposition σ = (0 2): P_σ R₀ = R₂ P_σ, and P_σ(1, 1, 2) = (2, 1, 1).
- The relation is the one needed to compare the Vieta group with the Bourgain–Gamburd–Sarnak group Γ generated by the moves and the permutations.

**Prerequisites.** `markoff-vieta-involution`, `positive-markoff-triples`, `mathlib:Equiv.Perm`

**Sources.**

- bgs-markoff-2016 — Jean Bourgain, Alexander Gamburd and Peter Sarnak, *Markoff surfaces and strong approximation: 1*, §1, p. 1.

  > The Markoff triples M are the positive integer solutions to (1). Let Γ be a group of affine integral morphisms of A³ generated by the permutations of the coordiantes and the Vieta involutions R₁, R₂, R₃, where R₃(x₁, x₂, x₃) = (x₁, x₂, 3x₁x₂ − x₃)

  The set M, the moves R_i and the coordinate permutations.

- martin-markoff-2025 — Daniel E. Martin, *A new proof of Chen's theorem for Markoff graphs*, §1, arXiv v1 p. 1 (published version p. 623).

  > The second- and third-coordinate Vieta involutions are defined analogously.

  The three moves are conjugate under the coordinate permutations, which is what the lemma makes precise (item 11 of the paper extraction).

---

#### `markoff-vieta-preserves-positivity` — The other root is positive: the moves preserve M

*lemma.*

**Statement.** If x ∈ M and i ∈ Fin 3, then R_i x ∈ M; moreover (R_i x)_i · x_i = x_{i+1}² + x_{i+2}² > 0.

**Hypotheses.**

- x ∈ M.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. The i-th coordinate of R_i x and x_i are the two roots of t² − 3x_{i+1}x_{i+2}t + (x_{i+1}² + x_{i+2}²), so their product is x_{i+1}² + x_{i+2}² by Vieta's formulas (equivalently, by expanding with the equation).
2. Since x_i > 0 and the product is positive, (R_i x)_i > 0; the other coordinates are unchanged, and R_i x ∈ X(ℤ) by markoff-vieta-preserves-equation.

**Acceptance.**

- R₂(1, 2, 5) = (1, 2, 1): 5 · 1 = 1² + 2².
- The statement fails for signed solutions only through the sign pattern: R_i preserves the sign pattern of x.

**Prerequisites.** `markoff-vieta-preserves-equation`, `positive-markoff-triples`

**Sources.**

- zhang-markoff-congruence-2006 — Ying Zhang, *Congruence and uniqueness of certain Markoff numbers*, §1, p. 1.

  > we have c + c′ = 3ab and cc′ = a² + b². Thus c′ = 3ab − c is a positive integer and (a, b, c′) is another solution triple to (1) in positive integers, that is, a Markoff triple.

  Positivity of the other root.

---

#### `markoff-descent-inequality` — The descent inequality for positive Markoff triples

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let 1 ≤ a ≤ b ≤ c be integers with a² + b² + c² = 3abc and (a, b, c) ≠ (1, 1, 1). Then c′ = 3ab − c satisfies 0 < c′ ≤ b and c′ < c, with c′ = b only for (a, b, c) = (1, 1, 2). Moreover b < c, so c is the unique largest coordinate, and the other two moves strictly increase the largest coordinate: 3bc − a > c and 3ac − b > c.

**Hypotheses.**

- (a, b, c) ordered increasingly, positive, not (1, 1, 1).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Let q(t) = t² − 3abt + a² + b², whose roots are c and c′ = 3ab − c; c′ > 0 by markoff-vieta-preserves-positivity.
2. q(b) = a² + (2 − 3a)b² ≤ a² − b² ≤ 0, with equality iff a = b = 1; in that case c² − 3c + 2 = 0 gives c ∈ {1, 2}, so (a, b, c) = (1, 1, 2), c′ = 1 = b.
3. Otherwise q(b) < 0, so b lies strictly between the roots; as c ≥ b, c is the larger root and c′ < b < c.
4. If b = c then q(b) = 0, which forces (1, 1, 1) or c = 2 ≠ b; hence b < c in all cases considered.
5. For the other moves: 3bc − a ≥ 3c − a > c and 3ac − b ≥ 3c − b > c, since a, b < 2c.

**Acceptance.**

- For (1, 2, 5): c′ = 1 < 2 = b; for (2, 5, 29): c′ = 1 < 5; for (1, 1, 2): c′ = 1 = b.
- Every nonroot ordered positive triple therefore has exactly one descending move, in its unique largest coordinate (item 9 of the paper extraction).

**Prerequisites.** `markoff-vieta-preserves-positivity`

**Sources.**

- zhang-markoff-congruence-2006 — Ying Zhang, *Congruence and uniqueness of certain Markoff numbers*, §1, p. 2, the paragraph after Theorem A.

  > Indeed, if a < b < c then (c − b)(c′ − b) = cc′ − (c + c′)b + b² = a² + 2b² − 3ab² < 0, and hence c′ < b.

  The descent inequality in the nonsingular case.

- zhang-markoff-uniqueness-2006 — Ying Zhang, *An elementary proof of Markoff conjecture for prime powers*, §3, proof of the Markoff Theorem, p. 2.

  > The operation (x, y, z) ↦ (x, y, z′) reduces the length x + y + z of Markoff triples exactly when z′ < z.

  The size measure used for the descent; the lemma records the inequality in the form the tree argument needs.

---

#### `markoff-root-generation` — Markoff's theorem: every positive Markoff triple comes from (1, 1, 1)

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Every x ∈ M is obtained from (1, 1, 1) by a finite word in the moves R₀, R₁, R₂: there are i₁, …, i_k ∈ Fin 3 with x = R_{i₁} ⋯ R_{i_k}(1, 1, 1). No coordinate permutation is needed.

**Hypotheses.**

- x ∈ M.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Strong induction on the largest coordinate max(x).
2. If x = (1, 1, 1) take the empty word.
3. Otherwise let i be the position of the unique largest coordinate (markoff-descent-inequality applied to the increasing rearrangement of x); R_i x ∈ M has smaller largest coordinate, so R_i x is a word applied to (1, 1, 1), and x = R_i(R_i x) by markoff-vieta-involutive.

**Acceptance.**

- (2, 5, 29) = R₂ R₁ R₀ (1, 1, 1): R₀(1, 1, 1) = (2, 1, 1), R₁(2, 1, 1) = (2, 5, 1), R₂(2, 5, 1) = (2, 5, 29).
- Consequently M is the orbit of (1, 1, 1) under the group generated by the moves, and a fortiori under Γ (Bourgain–Gamburd–Sarnak).

**Prerequisites.** `markoff-descent-inequality`, `markoff-vieta-involutive`

**Sources.**

- zhang-markoff-congruence-2006 — Ying Zhang, *Congruence and uniqueness of certain Markoff numbers*, §1, Theorem A, p. 2.

  > Theorem A (Markoff [11]). Every Markoff triple can be traced back to (1, 1, 1) by repeatedly performing the following operation on Markoff triples: (a, b, c) ↦ (a, b, c′) := (a, b, 3ab − c)

  Markoff's theorem, in the source's form with the coordinates rearranged in ascending order before each step.

- zhang-markoff-uniqueness-2006 — Ying Zhang, *An elementary proof of Markoff conjecture for prime powers*, §3, p. 2.

  > Markoff Theorem. Every Markoff triple, (x, y, z), can be reduced to the trivial triple (1, 1, 1) by repeated use of the following operation on Markoff triples: (x, y, z) ↦ (x, y, z′),

  The same theorem with a complete two-line proof; the ordered-triple form here removes the rearrangements by moving in the position of the largest coordinate.

- bgs-markoff-2016 — Jean Bourgain, Alexander Gamburd and Peter Sarnak, *Markoff surfaces and strong approximation: 1*, §1, p. 1.

  > The Markoff triples M are the positive integer solutions to (1). Let Γ be a group of affine integral morphisms of A³ generated by the permutations of the coordiantes and the Vieta involutions R₁, R₂, R₃, where R₃(x₁, x₂, x₃) = (x₁, x₂, 3x₁x₂ − x₃)

  The set M, the moves R_i and the coordinate permutations.

---

#### `markoff-tree` — The positive Markoff graph is a tree rooted at (1, 1, 1)

*theorem.* **Planet:** *Markoff tree*.

**Statement.** The integer Markoff graph (vertices M, x adjacent to y iff x ≠ y and y = R_i x for some i) is a tree. Every vertex has degree exactly 3, and every vertex other than (1, 1, 1) has exactly one neighbour with smaller largest coordinate.

**Hypotheses.**

- Vertices are ordered positive triples; the statement is false for the signed nonzero integer solutions, whose graph is a disjoint union of four copies of this tree (one for each sign pattern with product +1).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Connected: by markoff-root-generation every vertex is joined to (1, 1, 1) by the path of successive words.
2. Degree 3: the three moves change three different coordinates, and no move fixes a vertex of M ((R_i x)_i = x_i would give 2x_i = 3x_{i+1}x_{i+2} and x_i² = x_{i+1}² + x_{i+2}², hence 9x_{i+1}²x_{i+2}² = 4(x_{i+1}² + x_{i+2}²), impossible for positive integers).
3. Height function h(x) = max(x): along every edge h changes (a move changes one coordinate to a different value and the descent inequality compares the maxima), and every vertex x ≠ (1, 1, 1) has exactly one neighbour with smaller h (markoff-descent-inequality: the move in the unique largest coordinate decreases h, the other two increase it; for the permutations of (1, 1, 2) the descending neighbour is (1, 1, 1)).
4. Acyclic: in a cycle, a vertex of maximal h has two distinct neighbours on the cycle with smaller h, contradicting uniqueness of the descending neighbour; so the graph is a tree (Mathlib's SimpleGraph.IsTree: connected and acyclic).

**Acceptance.**

- The three neighbours of (1, 1, 1) are (2, 1, 1), (1, 2, 1), (1, 1, 2); the neighbours of (1, 1, 2) are (1, 1, 1), (5, 1, 2), (1, 5, 2).
- This is the positive-triple form of the statement the source attributes to Markoff; the finite-field graphs G_p of the Part II are not trees.

**Prerequisites.** `markoff-root-generation`, `markoff-descent-inequality`, `markoff-vieta-involution`, `mathlib:SimpleGraph.IsTree`, `mathlib:SimpleGraph.Connected`, `mathlib:SimpleGraph.IsAcyclic`

**Sources.**

- martin-markoff-2025 — Daniel E. Martin, *A new proof of Chen's theorem for Markoff graphs*, §1, arXiv v1 p. 1 (published version p. 623).

  > Markoff proved that the graph obtained from integer solutions is a tree [8]. In particular, it is connected

  The tree statement, which holds for the positive solutions M (see sourceIssue ClassicalArithmeticCompletion/E501 for the restriction).

- zhang-markoff-uniqueness-2006 — Ying Zhang, *An elementary proof of Markoff conjecture for prime powers*, §3, p. 2, after the proof of the Markoff Theorem.

  > Markoff Theorem tells us there is a natural infinite binary tree structure on the permutation classes of all the Markoff triples.

  The tree up to permutation; the ordered form, with degree 3 at every vertex including the root, is the statement planned here.

---

#### `markoff-triple-coordinates-coprime` — The coordinates of a positive Markoff triple are pairwise coprime

*lemma.*

**Statement.** If (a, b, c) ∈ M then gcd(a, b) = gcd(b, c) = gcd(a, c) = 1.

**Hypotheses.**

- (a, b, c) ∈ M.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. From the equation, gcd(a, b) divides c², and in fact gcd(a, b) = gcd(b, c) = gcd(c, a) = gcd(a, b, c) (a prime dividing two coordinates divides the square of the third and hence the third).
2. gcd(a, b, c) is invariant under each move, since gcd(a, b, 3ab − c) = gcd(a, b, c); by markoff-root-generation it equals gcd(1, 1, 1) = 1.

**Acceptance.**

- (5, 13, 194): gcd(5, 13) = gcd(13, 194) = gcd(5, 194) = 1.
- Consequently no prime divides all three coordinates, so the reduction of x ∈ M modulo a prime p is never (0, 0, 0).

**Prerequisites.** `markoff-root-generation`

**Sources.**

- zhang-markoff-congruence-2006 — Ying Zhang, *Congruence and uniqueness of certain Markoff numbers*, §1, Theorem B and its proof, p. 2.

  > By (1), gcd(a, b) = gcd(b, c) = gcd(c, a) = gcd(a, b, c), and by Theorem A, gcd(a, b, c) = gcd(a, b, 3ab − c) = · · · = gcd(1, 1, 1) = 1.

  Frobenius's Theorem B(a) with the source's proof.

---

#### `markoff-reduction-mod-p` — Reduction of Markoff triples modulo p

*construction.*

**Statement.** For a natural number p, red_p : ℤ³ → (ℤ/pℤ)³ is coordinatewise reduction. It maps X(ℤ) into X(ℤ/pℤ), commutes with every move R_i and every coordinate permutation P_σ, sends (1, 1, 1) to (1, 1, 1), and for p prime it maps M into X*(𝔽_p).

**Hypotheses.**

- p ≥ 2 for the last clause (for p = 1 the ring is trivial and X*(ℤ/1ℤ) is empty); the first clauses hold for every p.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. red_p = (Int.castRingHom (ZMod p)) ∘ −; the first clause is functoriality of X (markoff-triples), the commutation with moves is naturality of R_i (markoff-vieta-involution), and the commutation with permutations is immediate.
2. Nonzero image for p prime: by markoff-triple-coordinates-coprime no prime divides all coordinates of x ∈ M, so red_p x ≠ 0; alternatively, by markoff-root-generation x is a word applied to (1, 1, 1), red_p x is the same word applied to (1, 1, 1) ∈ X*(𝔽_p), and the moves preserve X*(𝔽_p) (markoff-vieta-permutes-nonzero-locus).
3. Reduction may collapse an edge: distinct neighbours in M can reduce to the same triple, and R_i may fix a triple modulo p.

**Acceptance.**

- red₂(2, 5, 29) = (0, 1, 1); red₃(2, 5, 29) = (−1, −1, −1); red₅(1, 2, 5) = (1, 2, 0) ∈ X*(𝔽₅).
- red₂ R₂ (1, 1, 1) = R₂ red₂ (1, 1, 1) = (1, 1, 0).

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `markoffReduce` | data | red_p : (Fin 3 → ℤ) → (Fin 3 → ZMod p), coordinatewise reduction. |
| `markoffReduce_apply` | simp | red_p x i = (x i : ZMod p). |
| `markoffReduce_mem_markoffSet` | functoriality | x ∈ X(ℤ) → red_p x ∈ X(ℤ/pℤ). |
| `markoffReduce_markoffVieta` | compatibility | red_p (R_i x) = R_i (red_p x). |
| `markoffReduce_comp_perm` | compatibility | red_p (x ∘ σ⁻¹) = (red_p x) ∘ σ⁻¹. |
| `markoffReduce_ones` | simp | red_p (1, 1, 1) = (1, 1, 1). |
| `markoffReduce_mem_markoffSetNonzero` | other | For p prime and x ∈ M, red_p x ∈ X*(𝔽_p). |

**Uses.**

- Martin, §1: Baragar's conjecture compares the reduction of the integer tree with the Markoff graph G_p
- Bourgain–Gamburd–Sarnak, §1: the reduction M → X*(p) whose surjectivity is strong approximation
- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/markoff-strong-approximation: the map whose surjectivity is defined there
- ArithmeticDynamics Part II (PAPER-MARTIN-25 items 14 onwards): orbit comparisons Γ·(1, 1, 1) = V·(1, 1, 1) modulo p pass through this map

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `markoffReduce_two_five_twentynine_two` (computation): red₂(2, 5, 29) = (0, 1, 1).
- `markoffReduce_one_two_five_five` (computation): red₅(1, 2, 5) = (1, 2, 0), which is a nonzero element of X(𝔽₅).
- `markoffReduce_three_eq_neg_ones` (computation): red₃(2, 5, 29) = (−1, −1, −1) in 𝔽₃³.
- `markoffReduce_one_trivial` (degenerate): For p = 1 every reduction is (0, 0, 0), so the prime hypothesis of markoffReduce_mem_markoffSetNonzero cannot be dropped.
- `markoffReduce_vieta_compat` (compatibility): red₇(R₁(2, 1, 1)) = R₁(red₇(2, 1, 1)) = (2, 5, 1) in 𝔽₇³.

**Prerequisites.** `markoff-triples`, `markoff-vieta-involution`, `markoff-triple-coordinates-coprime`, `markoff-vieta-permutes-nonzero-locus`, `markoff-permutation-equivariance`, `mathlib:Int.castRingHom`, `mathlib:ZMod`

**Sources.**

- martin-markoff-2025 — Daniel E. Martin, *A new proof of Chen's theorem for Markoff graphs*, §1, arXiv v1 p. 1 (published version p. 623).

  > Given a prime number p, every Markoff triple reduces to a solution mod p, but it is not clear if every solution mod p lifts to a solution in Z.

  The reduction map and the lifting question; the node adds the commutation with the moves that the graph comparison uses (item 12 of the paper extraction).

- bgs-markoff-2016 — Jean Bourgain, Alexander Gamburd and Peter Sarnak, *Markoff surfaces and strong approximation: 1*, §1, pp. 1–2.

  > For p a prime number the action of Γ and ∆ on X descends to a permutation action on the finite set X(Z/pZ) of solutions to (1) in Z/pZ.

  Descent of the moves and permutations to the reduction, which is the commutation clause.

#### `markoff-strong-approximation` — Strong approximation for positive Markoff triples at a prime

*definition.*

**Statement.** For a prime p, strong approximation holds at p if the reduction red_p : M → X*(𝔽_p) is surjective: every nonzero solution of x₀² + x₁² + x₂² = 3x₀x₁x₂ over 𝔽_p lifts to a positive Markoff triple. Only the definition is planned here; the theorems about it (Bourgain–Gamburd–Sarnak, Chen, Martin) belong to the ArithmeticDynamics Part II.

**Hypotheses.**

- p prime; the target is X*(𝔽_p), not X(𝔽_p): the origin is never the reduction of an element of M.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Define the predicate as surjectivity of red_p from M onto X*(𝔽_p) (Set.SurjOn).
2. Equivalent orbit form: by markoff-root-generation and the commutation of red_p with the moves, the image of M is the orbit of (1, 1, 1) under the group generated by the moves acting on X*(𝔽_p); so strong approximation at p holds iff that orbit is all of X*(𝔽_p).

**Acceptance.**

- Strong approximation holds at p = 2 and p = 3 (by the lifts in the tests).
- Surjectivity onto X(𝔽_p) always fails, since (0, 0, 0) is not a reduction of M.

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `MarkoffStrongApproximation` | data | The predicate Set.SurjOn red_p M X*(𝔽_p). |
| `markoffStrongApproximation_iff` | characterisation | MarkoffStrongApproximation p ↔ ∀ y ∈ X*(𝔽_p), ∃ x ∈ M, red_p x = y. |
| `markoffStrongApproximation_iff_orbit` | characterisation | MarkoffStrongApproximation p ↔ every y ∈ X*(𝔽_p) is a word in the moves applied to (1, 1, 1) ∈ 𝔽_p³. |
| `not_surjOn_markoffReduce_markoffSet` | other | red_p never maps M onto X(𝔽_p), because (0, 0, 0) has no preimage. |

**Uses.**

- Bourgain–Gamburd–Sarnak, Theorems 1–2: their results bound the set of primes where strong approximation fails
- Martin, Theorem 1.3 and Conjecture 1.1: Baragar's conjecture, in its graph form, is strong approximation at p
- ArithmeticDynamics Part II (PAPER-MARTIN-25 items 14 onwards): the target statement of the Part II, which imports this definition

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `markoffStrongApproximation_two` (computation): Strong approximation holds at p = 2: X*(𝔽₂) consists of the reductions of (1, 1, 1), (2, 1, 1), (1, 2, 1), (1, 1, 2).
- `markoffStrongApproximation_three` (computation): Strong approximation holds at p = 3: each of the eight elements of {±1}³ is the reduction of a word in the moves applied to (1, 1, 1).
- `not_surjOn_markoffSet_zmod_five` (non-example): The reduction M → X(𝔽₅) (with the origin) is not surjective.
- `markoffStrongApproximation_iff_compat` (compatibility): MarkoffStrongApproximation p unfolds to Mathlib's Set.SurjOn (markoffReduce p) M X*(𝔽_p).

**Prerequisites.** `markoff-reduction-mod-p`, `markoff-root-generation`

**Sources.**

- bgs-markoff-2016 — Jean Bourgain, Alexander Gamburd and Peter Sarnak, *Markoff surfaces and strong approximation: 1*, §1, pp. 1–2, after Conjecture 1.

  > Clearly the above conjecture implies strong approximation for M and X(Z) in the form that their reductions mod p M → X∗(p) and X(Z) → X(Z/pZ) are onto.

  The definition is the first of these two forms, for a single prime (item 13 of the paper extraction).

- martin-markoff-2025 — Daniel E. Martin, *A new proof of Chen's theorem for Markoff graphs*, §1, arXiv v1 p. 1 (published version p. 623).

  > In particular, it is connected, which means Baragar’s conjecture can be restated as follows.

  The orbit form: surjectivity of reduction is equivalent to connectedness of the orbit of the image of (1, 1, 1).

### Legendre's three-square theorem

A natural number is a sum of three squares exactly when it is not of the form 4ᵃ(8b + 7). Necessity is a congruence modulo 8 and a descent on powers of 4. Sufficiency reduces to squarefree m with m ≡ 1, 2, 3, 5, 6 (mod 8), and follows Ankeny: Dirichlet's theorem gives an auxiliary prime q with prescribed Jacobi symbols, quadratic reciprocity makes −m a square modulo q, Minkowski's convex body theorem applied to an explicit ellipsoid of volume (4π/3)2^{3/2} > 8 produces m = R² + 2v (or R² + v) with v a value of a positive definite binary form, and a Legendre-symbol computation shows that every prime ≡ 3 (mod 4) divides v to an even power, so that the two-square theorem finishes. Ankeny proves the case m ≡ 3 (mod 8) and outlines the others; the outline is completed in the proof steps (sourceIssue E502). All the inputs (Dirichlet's theorem, Minkowski's theorem, Jacobi reciprocity, the two-square theorem) are in Mathlib.

#### `three-squares-necessary-condition` — Integers of the form 4ᵃ(8b + 7) are not sums of three squares

*lemma.*

**Statement.** If n = 4ᵃ(8b + 7) with a, b ∈ ℕ, then there are no integers x, y, z with x² + y² + z² = n.

**Hypotheses.**

- a, b ∈ ℕ.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Squares are ≡ 0, 1 or 4 (mod 8), so a sum of three squares is never ≡ 7 (mod 8): this is the case a = 0.
2. Squares are ≡ 0 or 1 (mod 4), so x² + y² + z² ≡ 0 (mod 4) forces x, y, z all even; then (x/2)² + (y/2)² + (z/2)² = 4^{a−1}(8b + 7), and induction on a finishes.

**Acceptance.**

- 7, 15, 23, 28 = 4·7 and 60 = 4·15 are not sums of three squares; 12 = 4·3 is (2² + 2² + 2²).

**Prerequisites.** none beyond routine arithmetic.

**Sources.**

- sun-three-squares-2017 — Zhi-Wei Sun, *The three-square theorem and its applications*, §1, p. 1, the proof of the only-if direction.

  > If x, y, z ∈ Z and x² + y² + z² ≡ 3 (mod 4), then 2 ∤ xyz and thus x² + y² + z² ≡ 3 ≢ 7 (mod 8). If 4ᵏ(8l+7) = x² + y² + z² with k ∈ Z⁺ = {1, 2, 3, . . . }, l ∈ N and x, y, z ∈ Z, then x, y, z are all even

  The two proof steps, verbatim in substance.

---

#### `three-squares-squarefree-reduction` — Reduction of the three-square theorem to squarefree m ≢ 7 (mod 8)

*lemma.*

**Statement.** If n ≥ 1 is not of the form 4ᵃ(8b + 7), then n = 4ᵃ k² m with a ∈ ℕ, k odd and m squarefree with m mod 8 ∈ {1, 2, 3, 5, 6}. Consequently, if every squarefree m with m mod 8 ∈ {1, 2, 3, 5, 6} is a sum of three squares, so is every such n.

**Hypotheses.**

- n ≥ 1 not of the form 4ᵃ(8b + 7).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Write n = 4ᵃ n′ with 4 ∤ n′; then n′ is not ≡ 7 (mod 8).
2. Write n′ = k² m with m squarefree (Mathlib's Nat.sq_mul_squarefree_of_pos). If k were even, 4 would divide n′; so k is odd, k² ≡ 1 (mod 8) and m ≡ n′ (mod 8).
3. m is squarefree, so 4 ∤ m, and m ≢ 7 (mod 8): hence m mod 8 ∈ {1, 2, 3, 5, 6}.
4. A representation m = x² + y² + z² gives n = (2ᵃkx)² + (2ᵃky)² + (2ᵃkz)².

**Acceptance.**

- n = 72 = 4·18 = 4·3²·2: a = 1, k = 3, m = 2.
- n = 28 is excluded; n = 44 = 4·11 gives m = 11 ≡ 3 (mod 8).

**Prerequisites.** `mathlib:Nat.sq_mul_squarefree_of_pos`, `mathlib:Squarefree`

**Sources.**

- ankeny-three-squares-1957 — N. C. Ankeny, *Sums of three squares*, Introduction, p. 316.

  > Without loss of generality we will prove Theorem 1 only when m is square free. (In the following m will be assumed to be square free.)

  The reduction the source asserts; the proof steps supply the argument that removing an odd square factor preserves the class modulo 8.

---

#### `three-squares-auxiliary-prime` — Ankeny's auxiliary prime

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let m be a squarefree positive integer with m mod 8 ∈ {1, 2, 3, 5, 6}. Then there is a prime q ≡ 1 (mod 4) such that: (a) if m ≡ 3 (mod 8), the Jacobi symbol (−2q/p) = 1 for every prime p ∣ m; (b) otherwise (−q/p) = 1 for every odd prime p ∣ m, and, if m = 2m₁ is even, (−2/q) = (−1)^{(m₁−1)/2}. In both cases the Jacobi symbol (−m/q) = 1, so −m is a quadratic residue modulo q.

**Hypotheses.**

- m squarefree, m ≥ 1, m mod 8 ∈ {1, 2, 3, 5, 6}.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Each condition on q is a condition on q modulo 4 or 8 and modulo the odd primes p ∣ m, and each is satisfied by a nonempty set of residue classes prime to p (half of the nonzero classes modulo p satisfy (−2r/p) = 1, respectively (−r/p) = 1, and (−2/q) is fixed by q mod 8); by the Chinese remainder theorem these conditions define a nonempty set of unit residue classes modulo 8m.
2. Dirichlet's theorem on primes in arithmetic progressions (Mathlib's Nat.forall_exists_prime_gt_and_eq_mod) gives a prime q in such a class, which may be taken larger than m so that q ∤ m.
3. Case (a): 1 = ∏_{p∣m}(−2q/p) = (−2/m)∏(q/p) = (−2/m)∏(p/q) = (−2/m)(m/q) = (−2/m)(−m/q) = (−m/q), using quadratic reciprocity for q ≡ 1 (mod 4) (Mathlib's jacobiSym.quadratic_reciprocity_one_mod_four), (−1/q) = 1, and (−2/m) = 1 for m ≡ 3 (mod 8) (jacobiSym.at_neg_two).
4. Case (b): (q/p) = (−1/p) for odd p ∣ m, so ∏_{p odd}(p/q) = (−1/m_odd) = (−1)^{(m_odd−1)/2} by reciprocity and jacobiSym.at_neg_one; for m odd this gives (−m/q) = (m/q) = (−1)^{(m−1)/2} = 1 as m ≡ 1 (mod 4); for m = 2m₁ it gives (−m/q) = (2/q)(−1)^{(m₁−1)/2} = 1 because (2/q) = (−2/q) for q ≡ 1 (mod 4).

**Acceptance.**

- m = 3: the conditions say q ≡ 1 (mod 12). q = 13 works, and (−3/13) = 1 since 6² ≡ −3 (mod 13); q = 5 fails, since (−10/3) = (2/3) = −1.
- m = 1: the conditions are vacuous apart from q ≡ 1 (mod 4), and (−1/q) = 1.

**Prerequisites.** `mathlib:Nat.forall_exists_prime_gt_and_eq_mod`, `mathlib:jacobiSym`, `mathlib:jacobiSym.quadratic_reciprocity_one_mod_four`, `mathlib:jacobiSym.at_neg_one`, `mathlib:jacobiSym.at_neg_two`, `mathlib:jacobiSym.at_two`

**Sources.**

- ankeny-three-squares-1957 — N. C. Ankeny, *Sums of three squares*, §1, conditions (1)–(2) and computation (3), pp. 316–317.

  > Denote by q a positive prime which satisfies (1) (−2q/pⱼ) = +1, j = 1, 2, · · ·, r, (2) q ≡ 1 (mod 4) with (a/b) denoting the Jacobi Symbol. We see that such a prime exists by Dirichlet's theorem regarding primes in an arithmetic progression

  Case (a) and its proof.

- ankeny-three-squares-1957 — N. C. Ankeny, *Sums of three squares*, §2, p. 319.

  > Let q be a prime, (−q/pⱼ) = +1 for all odd prime divisors of m, q ≡ 1 (mod 4), and if m is even, m = 2m₁, (−2/q) = (−1)^{(m₁−1)/2}

  Case (b); the source outlines it (sourceIssue ClassicalArithmeticCompletion/E502) and the last proof step supplies the computation of (−m/q).

---

#### `three-squares-lattice-point` — The Minkowski step in Ankeny's proof

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let m and q be as in three-squares-auxiliary-prime. (a) If m ≡ 3 (mod 8), there are integers b (odd) and h with b² − 4qh = −m, and integers R, x, y such that R² + 2v = m for v = qx² + bxy + hy²; moreover 4qv = (2qx + by)² + my². (b) Otherwise there are integers b, h with b² − qh = −m and integers R, x, y such that R² + v = m for v = qx² + 2bxy + hy²; moreover qv = (qx + by)² + my². In both cases v ≥ 0.

**Hypotheses.**

- m, q as in the auxiliary prime lemma.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Choose b with b² ≡ −m (mod q) (possible since (−m/q) = 1), odd in case (a); set h₁ = (b² + m)/q. In case (a), reducing modulo 4 with b odd, q ≡ 1 (mod 4) and m ≡ 3 (mod 8) gives 4 ∣ h₁; put h = h₁/4. In case (b) put h = h₁.
2. Choose t with t² ≡ −1/(2q) (mod m) in case (a) (possible by (−2q/p) = 1 for p ∣ m and the Chinese remainder theorem), respectively t odd with t² ≡ −1/q (mod p) for odd p ∣ m in case (b).
3. Case (a): R = 2tqx + tby + mz, S = (2q)^{1/2}x + b(2q)^{−1/2}y, T = m^{1/2}(2q)^{−1/2}y. This linear map ℤ³ → ℝ³ has determinant m^{3/2}; the ball R² + S² + T² < 2m has volume (4π/3)(2m)^{3/2}, so its preimage is a convex symmetric body of volume (4π/3)·2^{3/2} > 8. By Minkowski's convex body theorem (Mathlib's MeasureTheory.exists_ne_zero_mem_lattice_of_measure_mul_two_pow_lt_measure, with the volume from InnerProductSpace.volume_ball and MeasureTheory.Measure.addHaar_image_linearMap) there is a nonzero (x, y, z) ∈ ℤ³ with R² + S² + T² < 2m. Case (b): the same with R = tqx + tby + mz, S = q^{1/2}x + bq^{−1/2}y, T = m^{1/2}q^{−1/2}y.
4. Algebra: S² + T² = 2v in case (a) and = v in case (b), because b² + m = 4qh (resp. qh); and 4qv = (2qx + by)² + my² (resp. qv = (qx + by)² + my²).
5. Congruence: R ≡ t(2qx + by) (mod m) gives R² + 2v ≡ −(2qx + by)²/(2q) + (2qx + by)²/(2q) ≡ 0 (mod m) in case (a); in case (b) the same computation modulo each odd p ∣ m, and modulo 2 when m is even: with t odd, R² + v ≡ 0 (mod 2) whether b is odd or even.
6. So m ∣ R² + 2v (resp. R² + v); this number is positive because the triangular transformation is nondegenerate and (x, y, z) ≠ 0, and it is less than 2m; hence it equals m.

**Acceptance.**

- For m = 3, q = 13: b = 7 (odd, 49 + 3 = 52 = 4·13·1, so h = 1) and t = 1 (t² ≡ −1/26 ≡ 1 mod 3); the lattice point (x, y, z) = (0, 1, −2) gives R = 1, v = 13·0 + 7·0 + 1 = 1 and R² + S² + T² = 3 < 6, so R² + 2v = 3 = m.
- The body has volume (4π/3)2^{3/2} ≈ 11.85 > 8 = 2³, the Minkowski threshold in dimension 3.

**Prerequisites.** `three-squares-auxiliary-prime`, `mathlib:MeasureTheory.exists_ne_zero_mem_lattice_of_measure_mul_two_pow_lt_measure`, `mathlib:InnerProductSpace.volume_ball`, `mathlib:MeasureTheory.Measure.addHaar_image_linearMap`

**Sources.**

- ankeny-three-squares-1957 — N. C. Ankeny, *Sums of three squares*, §1, equations (4)–(12), pp. 317–318.

  > In the (R, S, T) space (7) defines a convex, symmetric (about the origin) body of volume 4/3π(2m)^{3/2}. The determinant of the transformations (8) is m^{3/2}. Hence, in the (x, y, z) space, (7) represents a convex symmetric body of volume 1/3(2^{7/2}π), and certainly 1/3(2^{7/2}π) > 8.

  The volume computation of the third proof step, case (a).

- ankeny-three-squares-1957 — N. C. Ankeny, *Sums of three squares*, §1, equation (12), p. 318.

  > We note that R₁ is a rational integer and by (9), (10), and (11) that m | R₁² + 2v, but by (7) R₁² + 2v < 2m. Furthermore R₁² + 2v ≠ 0, by the nondegenerate triangular transformation (8) and the fact that not all x₁, y₁, z₁ equal zero. Hence, (12) R₁² + 2v = m.

  The conclusion of case (a); case (b) is the source's §2, whose details are supplied in the proof steps (sourceIssues E502, E503).

---

#### `three-squares-remainder-sum-of-two-squares` — The remainder in Ankeny's proof is a sum of two squares

*lemma.*

**Statement.** In the situation of three-squares-lattice-point, every odd prime p that divides v to an odd power satisfies p ≡ 1 (mod 4). Hence 2v (case m ≡ 3 mod 8), respectively v (other cases), is a sum of two squares of integers.

**Hypotheses.**

- m, q, b, h, R, x, y as in three-squares-lattice-point.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Let p be odd with v_p(v) odd. If p ∤ m: from R² + 2v = m (resp. R² + v = m), R² ≡ m (mod p), so (m/p) = 1.
2. Still p ∤ m: if p = q, then (−m/q) = 1 by the choice of b. If p ≠ q, p divides X² + mY² = 4qv (resp. qv) to an odd power, with X = 2qx + by (resp. qx + by), Y = y; dividing out p² while p divides both X and Y (then p² divides the sum) leaves p ∤ Y with p ∣ X² + mY², so (−m/p) = 1.
3. From (m/p) = (−m/p) = 1, (−1/p) = 1, i.e. p ≡ 1 (mod 4).
4. If p ∣ m: from R² + 2v = m, p ∣ R, and dividing R² + (1/2q)((2qx + by)² + my²) = m (resp. R² + (1/q)((qx + by)² + my²) = m) shows p ∣ 2qx + by (resp. qx + by); since m is squarefree, dividing by p gives y² ≡ 2q (mod p) (resp. y² ≡ q), so (2q/p) = 1 (resp. (q/p) = 1) unless p ∣ y, which would force p ∣ q; combined with (−2q/p) = 1 (resp. (−q/p) = 1) this gives (−1/p) = 1.
5. So the exponent of every prime ≡ 3 (mod 4) in v (and in 2v) is even, and Mathlib's Nat.eq_sq_add_sq_iff gives the representation as a sum of two squares.

**Acceptance.**

- In the example m = 3, q = 13 of the previous lemma, 2v = 2 = 1² + 1², and 3 = 1² + 1² + 1².
- The argument never needs p = 2, which is harmless for sums of two squares.

**Prerequisites.** `three-squares-lattice-point`, `three-squares-auxiliary-prime`, `mathlib:Nat.eq_sq_add_sq_iff`, `mathlib:jacobiSym`

**Sources.**

- ankeny-three-squares-1957 — N. C. Ankeny, *Sums of three squares*, §1, equations (13)–(16), p. 318.

  > Let p be an odd prime which exactly divides v to an odd power, i.e. p^{2n+1} ∥ v. If p does not divide m, then by (12), (13) (m/p) = +1.

  The first two proof steps.

- ankeny-three-squares-1957 — N. C. Ankeny, *Sums of three squares*, §1, after (17), p. 319.

  > Thus all odd primes which exactly divide v to an odd power are ≡ 1 (mod 4). Thus 2v is the sum of two square integers.

  The conclusion of the lemma in case (a).

---

#### `three-squares-squarefree-case` — Squarefree m ≢ 7 (mod 8) is a sum of three squares

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Every squarefree positive integer m with m mod 8 ∈ {1, 2, 3, 5, 6} is a sum of three squares of integers.

**Hypotheses.**

- m squarefree, m ≥ 1, m mod 8 ∈ {1, 2, 3, 5, 6}.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Take q from three-squares-auxiliary-prime, then R, x, y from three-squares-lattice-point, with R² + 2v = m (case m ≡ 3 mod 8) or R² + v = m (otherwise).
2. By three-squares-remainder-sum-of-two-squares, 2v (respectively v) = s² + u², so m = R² + s² + u².

**Acceptance.**

- m = 3 = 1 + 1 + 1, m = 6 = 4 + 1 + 1, m = 10 = 9 + 1 + 0, m = 11 = 9 + 1 + 1; m = 7 is excluded.

**Prerequisites.** `three-squares-auxiliary-prime`, `three-squares-lattice-point`, `three-squares-remainder-sum-of-two-squares`

**Sources.**

- ankeny-three-squares-1957 — N. C. Ankeny, *Sums of three squares*, §2, p. 319.

  > The proof will proceed exactly as in §1, which will complete the proof when m ≡ 1, 2, 3, 5, 6 (mod 8), and thus for all square free m.

  The squarefree case of Theorem 1, assembled from §1 and §2.

---

#### `legendre-three-square-theorem` — Legendre's three-square theorem

*theorem.* **Planet:** *Legendre's three-square theorem*.

**Statement.** A natural number n is a sum of three squares of integers if and only if n is not of the form 4ᵃ(8b + 7) with a, b ∈ ℕ.

**Hypotheses.**

- n ∈ ℕ; squares of integers (equivalently of natural numbers).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Only if: three-squares-necessary-condition.
2. If: n = 0 is 0² + 0² + 0²; for n ≥ 1, three-squares-squarefree-reduction reduces to squarefree m with m mod 8 ∈ {1, 2, 3, 5, 6}, which is three-squares-squarefree-case.

**Acceptance.**

- 7, 15, 23, 28, 31, 39, 47, 55, 60, 63 are the numbers below 64 that are not sums of three squares.
- Every n ≡ 3 (mod 8) is a sum of three odd squares (a consequence of the theorem and the parity of squares), e.g. 11 = 9 + 1 + 1.
- The theorem is not in Mathlib or Tau Ceti; Mathlib has the two-square characterisation (Nat.eq_sq_add_sq_iff) and the four-square theorem (Nat.sum_four_squares).

**Prerequisites.** `three-squares-necessary-condition`, `three-squares-squarefree-reduction`, `three-squares-squarefree-case`

**Sources.**

- ankeny-three-squares-1957 — N. C. Ankeny, *Sums of three squares*, Introduction, Theorem 1, p. 316.

  > Theorem 1. If m is a positive integer not of the form 4ᵃ(8w+7), then m is the sum of three squares.

  Legendre's theorem, the sufficiency direction.

- sun-three-squares-2017 — Zhi-Wei Sun, *The three-square theorem and its applications*, §1, p. 1.

  > Three-Square-Theorem (Legendre, 1797; Gauss, 1801). n ∈ N = {0, 1, 2, . . . } can be written as the sum of three squares if and only if it is not of the form 4ᵏ(8l + 7) with k, l ∈ N.

  The statement in the form planned, including n = 0.

---

### Representation numbers: Jacobi's two- and four-square theorems

The representation number r_k(n) counts x ∈ ℤᵏ with x₁² + ⋯ + x_k² = n, with order and signs. Jacobi's two-square theorem r₂(n) = 4Σ_{d∣n}χ₄(d) is proved through the Gaussian integers: r₂(n)/4 is the number of associate classes of Gaussian integers of norm n, it is multiplicative by unique factorisation, and its values at prime powers come from the classification of Gaussian primes (all in Mathlib). Jacobi's four-square theorem r₄(n) = 8Σ_{d∣n, 4∤d}d follows Hirschhorn: square Jacobi's identity for ∏(1 − xⁿ)³, coalesce the resulting double sum, rewrite it through the product forms of the two theta-type series and the product rule, and divide to obtain θ(x)⁴ = 1 + 8Σ_{4∤n}nxⁿ/(1 − xⁿ). The q-series inputs (the Jacobi triple product identity and four of its specialisations) are requested from QSeriesPartitionsAndMockModularForms:QM.0.

#### `sum-of-squares-representation-count` — The representation numbers r_k(n)

*definition.*

**Statement.** For k, n ∈ ℕ, r_k(n) is the number of x ∈ ℤᵏ with x₁² + ⋯ + x_k² = n, counting order and signs. It is finite, since every coordinate satisfies |x_i| ≤ √n.

**Hypotheses.**

- Ordered representations with signs, including zero coordinates.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Define r_k(n) = Nat.card {x : Fin k → ℤ // ∑ x_i² = n}; the set is contained in the finite box |x_i| ≤ n.
2. Convolution: splitting ℤ^{k+l} = ℤᵏ × ℤˡ gives r_{k+l}(n) = Σ_{a+b=n} r_k(a) r_l(b); equivalently the generating function Σ_n r_k(n)xⁿ is θ(x)ᵏ with θ(x) = Σ_{m∈ℤ} x^{m²}.

**Acceptance.**

- r₂(5) = 8, r₂(25) = 12, r₂(3) = 0, r₄(1) = 8, r₂(0) = 1.

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `sumSquaresCount` | data | r_k(n) = Nat.card {x : Fin k → ℤ // ∑ i, x i ^ 2 = n}. |
| `sumSquaresCount_finite` | other | {x : Fin k → ℤ | ∑ x_i² = n} is finite. |
| `sumSquaresCount_zero_right` | simp | r_k(0) = 1. |
| `sumSquaresCount_zero_left` | simp | r₀(n) = 1 if n = 0 and 0 otherwise. |
| `sumSquaresCount_pos_iff` | characterisation | 0 < r_k(n) ↔ ∃ x : Fin k → ℤ, ∑ x_i² = n. |
| `sumSquaresCount_add` | relation | r_{k+l}(n) = Σ_{a+b=n} r_k(a) r_l(b). |
| `sumSquaresCount_two_pos_iff` | compatibility | 0 < r₂(n) ↔ every prime q ≡ 3 (mod 4) divides n to an even power (Mathlib's Nat.eq_sq_add_sq_iff). |
| `sumSquaresCount_four_pos` | compatibility | 0 < r₄(n) for every n (Mathlib's Nat.sum_four_squares). |

**Uses.**

- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/jacobi-two-square-theorem: the quantity computed there
- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/jacobi-four-square-theorem: the quantity computed there
- QSeriesPartitionsAndMockModularForms:QM.1: the coefficients of powers of the theta series θ(x) = Σ x^{m²} are the r_k(n)
- GeometryOfNumbersAndQuadraticArithmetic:GN.3: representation numbers of the forms x₁² + ⋯ + x_k² are the prototype of the theta-series interface

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `sumSquaresCount_two_five` (computation): r₂(5) = 8: (±1, ±2) and (±2, ±1).
- `sumSquaresCount_two_twentyfive` (computation): r₂(25) = 12.
- `sumSquaresCount_two_two` (non-example): r₂(2) = 4, not 1: order and signs are counted.
- `sumSquaresCount_two_zero` (degenerate): r₂(0) = 1 (only (0, 0)).
- `sumSquaresCount_four_one` (computation): r₄(1) = 8.
- `sumSquaresCount_two_three` (compatibility): r₂(3) = 0, in agreement with Nat.eq_sq_add_sq_iff (3 ≡ 3 mod 4 divides 3 once).

**Prerequisites.** `mathlib:Nat.card`, `mathlib:Nat.eq_sq_add_sq_iff`, `mathlib:Nat.sum_four_squares`

**Sources.**

- wuthrich-gaussian-integers-2011 — Chris Wuthrich, *Further Number Theory G13FNT, Chapter 5: Gaussian integers and sums of squares*, Theorem 5.12 and the Remark after it, p. 5.

  > Theorem 5.12. For each n ∈ N, the number of integral solutions x, y to the equation n = x² + y² is given by 4 Σ_{d|n} χ₁(d).

  The count r₂(n) as the number of integral solutions, with order and signs.

- hirschhorn-four-squares-1987 — M. D. Hirschhorn, *A simple proof of Jacobi's four-square theorem*, Theorem 1, p. 436.

  > The number r₄(n) of representations of the positive integer n as a sum of four squares is given by r₄(n) = 8 Σ_{d|n, 4∤d} d.

  The count r₄(n) in the same convention.

---

#### `gaussian-norm-count-multiplicative` — The number of Gaussian integers of norm n, divided by 4, is multiplicative

*lemma.*

**Statement.** For n ≥ 1 let ρ(n) = r₂(n)/4. Then r₂(n) = #{α ∈ ℤ[i] : N(α) = n} is divisible by 4, ρ(n) is the number of associate classes of Gaussian integers of norm n, and ρ is multiplicative: ρ(mn) = ρ(m)ρ(n) for coprime m, n.

**Hypotheses.**

- n ≥ 1; m, n coprime for multiplicativity.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. (x, y) ↦ x + yi identifies the representations of n with the Gaussian integers of norm n (Mathlib's Zsqrtd.norm for d = −1).
2. The unit group {±1, ±i} acts freely on the nonzero Gaussian integers and preserves the norm, so r₂(n) = 4ρ(n) with ρ(n) the number of associate classes.
3. ℤ[i] is a Euclidean domain, hence a unique factorisation domain (Mathlib's GaussianInt instances). For coprime m, n, every α of norm mn factors, uniquely up to units, as α = βγ with N(β) = m and N(γ) = n: group the prime factors of α according to whether the rational prime below them divides m or n.
4. Hence (β, γ) ↦ βγ induces a bijection between pairs of associate classes of norms m and n and associate classes of norm mn.

**Acceptance.**

- ρ(5) = 2 (classes of 2 + i and 2 − i), ρ(13) = 2, ρ(65) = 4 = ρ(5)ρ(13); r₂(65) = 16.
- ρ is not completely multiplicative: ρ(25) = 3 ≠ 4 = ρ(5)².

**Prerequisites.** `sum-of-squares-representation-count`, `mathlib:GaussianInt`, `mathlib:Zsqrtd.norm`, `mathlib:Zsqrtd.norm_mul`, `mathlib:UniqueFactorizationMonoid`, `mathlib:ArithmeticFunction.IsMultiplicative`

**Sources.**

- wuthrich-gaussian-integers-2011 — Chris Wuthrich, *Further Number Theory G13FNT, Chapter 5: Gaussian integers and sums of squares*, Proof of Theorem 5.12, p. 5.

  > By unique factorisation in Z[i], the latter sum has an Euler product expansion: Σ_{0≠α∈Z[i]} 1/N(α)ˢ = 4 ∏_{π∈Pi} 1/(1 − N(π)⁻ˢ), where the product is over all prime elements π of Z[i], choosing one from each set of four associate primes, and the factor of 4 allows for the unit factor

  The Euler product is the analytic form of the statement that ρ is multiplicative with the local factors of the next lemma; the node states the coefficientwise content.

- wuthrich-gaussian-integers-2011 — Chris Wuthrich, *Further Number Theory G13FNT, Chapter 5: Gaussian integers and sums of squares*, Theorem 5.8, p. 3.

  > Theorem 5.8. Let Pi be a complete set of non-associate Gaussian primes. Every 0 ≠ α ∈ Z[i] can be written as α = iⁿ · ∏_{π∈Pi} π^{aπ} for some 0 ⩽ n < 4 and aπ ⩾ 0.

  Unique factorisation, used in the third proof step.

---

#### `gaussian-norm-count-prime-power` — The number of associate classes of Gaussian integers of prime-power norm

*lemma.*

**Statement.** With ρ as in gaussian-norm-count-multiplicative and k ≥ 0: ρ(2ᵏ) = 1; ρ(pᵏ) = k + 1 for primes p ≡ 1 (mod 4); ρ(qᵏ) = 1 if k is even and 0 if k is odd, for primes q ≡ 3 (mod 4). In every case ρ(pᵏ) = Σ_{j=0}^{k} χ₄(p)ʲ, where χ₄ is the nontrivial character modulo 4 (χ₄(2) = 0).

**Hypotheses.**

- p prime, k ∈ ℕ.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Classification of Gaussian primes (Wuthrich, Proposition 5.7): 1 + i is the unique prime of norm 2 up to units; for p ≡ 1 (mod 4) there are exactly two non-associate primes π, π̄ of norm p (Mathlib's Nat.Prime.sq_add_sq gives p = a² + b²); for q ≡ 3 (mod 4), q is prime in ℤ[i] (Mathlib's GaussianInt.prime_iff_mod_four_eq_three_of_nat_prime), of norm q².
2. An element of norm pᵏ is, up to units, a product of primes above p with norms multiplying to pᵏ: (1 + i)ᵏ for p = 2; π^j π̄^{k−j} for j = 0, …, k when p ≡ 1 (mod 4); q^{k/2} when q ≡ 3 (mod 4) and k even, and none when k is odd.
3. Compare with Σ_{j≤k} χ₄(p)ʲ, which is 1 for p = 2, k + 1 for χ₄(p) = 1, and (1 + (−1)ᵏ)/2 for χ₄(p) = −1.

**Acceptance.**

- ρ(2) = 1 (only 1 + i), ρ(9) = 1 (only 3), ρ(3) = 0, ρ(25) = 3 (classes of (2 + i)², 5, (2 − i)²).

**Prerequisites.** `gaussian-norm-count-multiplicative`, `mathlib:GaussianInt.prime_iff_mod_four_eq_three_of_nat_prime`, `mathlib:Nat.Prime.sq_add_sq`, `mathlib:ZMod.χ₄`

**Sources.**

- wuthrich-gaussian-integers-2011 — Chris Wuthrich, *Further Number Theory G13FNT, Chapter 5: Gaussian integers and sums of squares*, Proposition 5.7, p. 3.

  > Proposition 5.7. Up to associates, the Gaussian primes are the following : • 1 + i is a Gaussian prime of norm 2. • For each prime number p ≡ 1 (mod 4) there are exactly two Gaussian primes π and π̄ of norm p. • Each prime number q ≡ 3 (mod 4) is a Gaussian prime of norm q².

  The classification used in the first two proof steps.

- wuthrich-gaussian-integers-2011 — Chris Wuthrich, *Further Number Theory G13FNT, Chapter 5: Gaussian integers and sums of squares*, Proof of Theorem 5.12, p. 5.

  > (iii). each π = q ≡ 3 (mod 4) with N(π) = q² contributes a factor of 1/(1 − q⁻²ˢ).

  The local factor at q ≡ 3 (mod 4), whose coefficients are ρ(qᵏ).

---

#### `jacobi-two-square-theorem` — Jacobi's two-square theorem

*theorem.*

**Statement.** For every n ≥ 1, r₂(n) = 4 Σ_{d∣n} χ₄(d) = 4(d₁(n) − d₃(n)), where χ₄ is the nontrivial Dirichlet character modulo 4 and d_j(n) is the number of divisors of n congruent to j modulo 4.

**Hypotheses.**

- n ≥ 1 (for n = 0, r₂(0) = 1).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Both n ↦ r₂(n)/4 (gaussian-norm-count-multiplicative) and n ↦ Σ_{d∣n} χ₄(d) (a Dirichlet convolution of multiplicative functions, Mathlib's ArithmeticFunction.IsMultiplicative.mul) are multiplicative.
2. They agree on prime powers by gaussian-norm-count-prime-power, since Σ_{d∣pᵏ} χ₄(d) = Σ_{j≤k} χ₄(p)ʲ.
3. Mathlib's ArithmeticFunction.IsMultiplicative.eq_iff_eq_on_prime_powers concludes.

**Acceptance.**

- r₂(1) = 4, r₂(2) = 4, r₂(5) = 8, r₂(25) = 12, r₂(65) = 16, r₂(3) = r₂(21) = 0.
- Consequence: d₁(n) ≥ d₃(n) for every n ≥ 1, and for a prime p ≡ 1 (mod 4) the representation p = a² + b² with 0 < a < b is unique.

**Prerequisites.** `gaussian-norm-count-multiplicative`, `gaussian-norm-count-prime-power`, `mathlib:ArithmeticFunction.IsMultiplicative.mul`, `mathlib:ArithmeticFunction.IsMultiplicative.eq_iff_eq_on_prime_powers`, `mathlib:ZMod.χ₄`

**Sources.**

- wuthrich-gaussian-integers-2011 — Chris Wuthrich, *Further Number Theory G13FNT, Chapter 5: Gaussian integers and sums of squares*, Theorem 5.12 and the end of its proof, p. 5.

  > Comparing coefficients: (1/4)aₙ = Σ_{md=n} χ₁(d) = Σ_{d|n} χ₁(d).

  The conclusion of the source's proof; χ₁ there is χ₄ here.

---

#### `hirschhorn-sixth-power-identity` — Hirschhorn's coalescence identity for ∏(1 − xⁿ)⁶

*lemma.*

**Statement.** In ℤ[[x]], ∏_{n≥1}(1 − xⁿ)⁶ = ½ Σ_{r,s∈ℤ} ((2r + 1)² − (2s)²) x^{r² + r + s²}.

**Hypotheses.**

- Identity of formal power series; each coefficient on the right is a finite sum and an integer.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Square Jacobi's identity ∏(1 − xⁿ)³ = ½ Σ_{n∈ℤ} (−1)ⁿ(2n + 1)x^{(n²+n)/2} (supplied by QSeriesPartitionsAndMockModularForms:QM.0) to get ¼ Σ_{m,n}(−1)^{m+n}(2m + 1)(2n + 1)x^{(m²+n²+m+n)/2}.
2. Split according to the parity of m + n. For m ≡ n put r = (m + n)/2, s = (m − n)/2; for m ≢ n put r = (m − n − 1)/2, s = (m + n + 1)/2. Each substitution is a bijection onto ℤ², the exponent becomes r² + r + s², and the signed coefficient becomes (2r + 1)² − (2s)² in both cases.
3. The two sums coalesce into twice the same sum, giving the factor ½.

**Acceptance.**

- Constant coefficient: the pairs with r² + r + s² = 0 are (0, 0) and (−1, 0), each contributing 1, so the right side has constant term ½·2 = 1.
- Coefficient of x: pairs (0, ±1), (−1, ±1) give ½·4·(1 − 4) = −6, matching (1 − x)⁶ = 1 − 6x + ⋯.

**Prerequisites.** `QSeriesPartitionsAndMockModularForms:QM.0`

**Sources.**

- hirschhorn-four-squares-1987 — M. D. Hirschhorn, *A simple proof of Jacobi's four-square theorem*, §2, p. 437.

  > In the first sum, set r = ½(m + n), s = ½(m − n), and in the second set r = ½(m − n − 1), s = ½(m + n + 1), and, remarkably, the two sums coalesce to give ∏(1 − xⁿ)⁶ = ½ Σ_{r,s=−∞}^{∞} ((2r + 1)² − (2s)²)x^{r²+s²+r}.

  The identity and its proof.

---

#### `hirschhorn-logarithmic-derivative-identity` — Hirschhorn's product-rule identity

*lemma.*

**Statement.** In ℤ[[x]], ∏_{n≥1}(1 − xⁿ)⁶ = ∏_{n≥1}(1 + x^{2n−1})²(1 + x^{2n})²(1 − x^{2n})² · (1 − 8 Σ_{n≥1}((2n − 1)x^{2n−1}/(1 + x^{2n−1}) − 2n x^{2n}/(1 + x^{2n}))).

**Hypotheses.**

- Identity of formal power series; x^{j}/(1 + x^{j}) denotes the power series Σ_{t≥1}(−1)^{t−1}x^{jt}.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Split the right side of hirschhorn-sixth-power-identity as ½(Σ_s x^{s²} · (1 + 4x d/dx)Σ_r x^{r²+r} − Σ_r x^{r²+r} · 4x d/dx Σ_s x^{s²}), using (2r + 1)²x^{r²+r} = (1 + 4x d/dx)x^{r²+r} and (2s)²x^{s²} = 4x d/dx x^{s²}.
2. Substitute the product forms Σ_s x^{s²} = ∏(1 + x^{2n−1})²(1 − x^{2n}) and Σ_r x^{r²+r} = 2∏(1 + x^{2n})²(1 − x^{2n}) (both consequences of the Jacobi triple product identity, supplied by QM.0).
3. Apply the product rule in logarithmic-derivative form, x d/dx log ∏(1 + x^j)^{e} = e Σ j x^j/(1 + x^j) and x d/dx log ∏(1 − x^j)^{e} = −e Σ j x^j/(1 − x^j), and collect terms: the Σ 2n x^{2n}/(1 − x^{2n}) terms cancel.

**Acceptance.**

- Both sides have constant term 1 and coefficient of x equal to −6: on the right, 1 − 8·1 contributes −8 and the product contributes 2 (from (1 + x)²).

**Prerequisites.** `hirschhorn-sixth-power-identity`, `QSeriesPartitionsAndMockModularForms:QM.0`, `mathlib:PowerSeries`, `mathlib:PowerSeries.derivative`

**Sources.**

- hirschhorn-four-squares-1987 — M. D. Hirschhorn, *A simple proof of Jacobi's four-square theorem*, §2, p. 437.

  > Making use of the triple-product identity, we obtain ∏(1 − xⁿ)⁶ = ½{∏(1 + x^{2n−1})²(1 − x^{2n}) × (1 + 4x d/dx)2∏(1 + x^{2n})²(1 − x^{2n}) − 2∏(1 + x^{2n})²(1 − x^{2n}) × 4x d/dx ∏(1 + x^{2n−1})²(1 − x^{2n})}.

  The substitution step; the product-rule evaluation follows on the same page.

---

#### `theta-fourth-power-lambert-series` — θ(x)⁴ as a Lambert series

*lemma.*

**Statement.** In ℤ[[x]], (Σ_{n∈ℤ} x^{n²})⁴ = 1 + 8 Σ_{n≥1, 4∤n} n xⁿ/(1 − xⁿ).

**Hypotheses.**

- Identity of formal power series; nxⁿ/(1 − xⁿ) = Σ_{t≥1} n x^{nt}.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Divide hirschhorn-logarithmic-derivative-identity by ∏(1 + xⁿ)⁴(1 − xⁿ)² = ∏(1 + x^{2n−1})²(1 + x^{2n})²(1 − x^{2n})² to get ∏((1 − xⁿ)/(1 + xⁿ))⁴ = 1 − 8Σ((2n − 1)x^{2n−1}/(1 + x^{2n−1}) − 2nx^{2n}/(1 + x^{2n})).
2. Use Gauss's identity ∏(1 − xⁿ)/(1 + xⁿ) = Σ_{n∈ℤ}(−1)ⁿx^{n²} (a consequence of the triple product identity, supplied by QM.0) and substitute −x for x.
3. Rewrite: Σ((2n − 1)x^{2n−1}/(1 − x^{2n−1}) + 2nx^{2n}/(1 + x^{2n})) = Σ nxⁿ/(1 − xⁿ) − Σ 4nx^{4n}/(1 − x^{4n}), since 2nx^{2n}/(1 − x^{2n}) − 2nx^{2n}/(1 + x^{2n}) = 4nx^{4n}/(1 − x^{4n}).
4. The difference Σ nxⁿ/(1 − xⁿ) − Σ 4nx^{4n}/(1 − x^{4n}) is Σ_{4∤n} nxⁿ/(1 − xⁿ).

**Acceptance.**

- Coefficient of x: 8·1 = 8 = r₄(1). Coefficient of x⁴: 8(1 + 2) = 24 = r₄(4) (the divisor 4 is excluded).

**Prerequisites.** `hirschhorn-logarithmic-derivative-identity`, `QSeriesPartitionsAndMockModularForms:QM.0`, `mathlib:PowerSeries`

**Sources.**

- hirschhorn-four-squares-1987 — M. D. Hirschhorn, *A simple proof of Jacobi's four-square theorem*, §2, p. 438.

  > Now, it is a simple consequence of the triple-product identity that ∏((1 − xⁿ)/(1 + xⁿ)) = Σ_{−∞}^{∞}(−1)ⁿx^{n²}

  The identity used in the second proof step.

- hirschhorn-four-squares-1987 — M. D. Hirschhorn, *A simple proof of Jacobi's four-square theorem*, §2, p. 438.

  > or, (Σ_{−∞}^{∞} x^{n²})⁴ = 1 + 8 Σ_{4∤n} nxⁿ/(1 − xⁿ) from which Theorem 1 follows directly.

  The statement of the lemma.

---

#### `jacobi-four-square-theorem` — Jacobi's four-square theorem

*theorem.* **Planet:** *Jacobi's four-square theorem*.

**Statement.** For every n ≥ 1, r₄(n) = 8 Σ_{d∣n, 4∤d} d. Equivalently r₄(n) = 8σ(n) for n odd and r₄(n) = 24σ(n_odd) for n even, where n_odd is the odd part of n.

**Hypotheses.**

- n ≥ 1 (r₄(0) = 1).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. The generating function Σ_n r₄(n)xⁿ is θ(x)⁴ with θ(x) = Σ_{m∈ℤ} x^{m²} (sumSquaresCount_add of sum-of-squares-representation-count).
2. The coefficient of xⁿ in Σ_{4∤m} m x^m/(1 − x^m) is Σ_{m∣n, 4∤m} m.
3. Compare coefficients in theta-fourth-power-lambert-series.

**Acceptance.**

- r₄(1) = 8, r₄(2) = 24, r₄(3) = 32, r₄(4) = 24, r₄(5) = 48.
- It implies Lagrange's four-square theorem (r₄(n) ≥ 8 > 0), which Mathlib already has as Nat.sum_four_squares.
- The two forms of the formula agree: for n = 12, 8(1 + 2 + 3 + 6) = 96 = 24σ(3).

**Prerequisites.** `theta-fourth-power-lambert-series`, `sum-of-squares-representation-count`, `mathlib:ArithmeticFunction.sigma`

**Sources.**

- hirschhorn-four-squares-1987 — M. D. Hirschhorn, *A simple proof of Jacobi's four-square theorem*, Theorem 1, p. 436.

  > The number r₄(n) of representations of the positive integer n as a sum of four squares is given by r₄(n) = 8 Σ_{d|n, 4∤d} d.

  The theorem, verbatim.

- wuthrich-gaussian-integers-2011 — Chris Wuthrich, *Further Number Theory G13FNT, Chapter 5: Gaussian integers and sums of squares*, Theorem 5.14, p. 6.

  > Theorem 5.14 (Jacobi). Let n ⩾ 1 be an integer. Let Aₙ be the number of solutions x, y, z, w ∈ Z to the equation x² + y² + z² + w² = n.

  The equivalent odd/even form of the formula, stated without proof in this source.

---

### Pell-type equations

The solutions of x² − dy² = n are the elements of norm n in ℤ[√d], and the Pell solutions (norm 1) act on them by multiplication. When the negative equation x² − dy² = −1 is solvable, its least positive solution ε generates all solutions of x² − dy² = ±1 up to sign, odd powers giving norm −1 and even powers norm 1, and ε² is Mathlib's fundamental solution. Chebyshev's bound puts a Pell multiple of every solution of x² − dy² = n in an explicit box, so the generalised equation has finitely many classes and is decided by a finite search; this is the stage's requirement that a finite search be complete only after a proven bound. For |n| < √d the positive solutions come from the convergents of √d (Lagrange, through Mathlib's Legendre theorem), which gives the continued-fraction decision procedure; with Chebyshev's bound only finitely many convergents are checked, so the periodicity of the continued fraction of √d is not needed. Congruence conditions give the classical obstructions to the negative equation.

#### `generalised-pell-solution-set` — The solution set of the generalised Pell equation x² − dy² = n

*definition.*

**Statement.** For d, n ∈ ℤ, S(d, n) = {z ∈ ℤ[√d] : N(z) = n}, where ℤ[√d] is Mathlib's Zsqrtd d and N(x + y√d) = x² − dy² is Zsqrtd.norm. Its elements are identified with the integer solutions (x, y) of x² − dy² = n. The group of solutions of the Pell equation (Mathlib's Pell.Solution₁ d, the elements of norm 1) acts on S(d, n) by multiplication; an orbit is a class of 'Pell multiples'.

**Hypotheses.**

- d, n ∈ ℤ arbitrary in the definition; the finiteness theorems assume d > 0 not a square and n ≠ 0.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Define S(d, n) as the fibre of the multiplicative map Zsqrtd.norm over n.
2. Multiplicativity of the norm (Mathlib's Zsqrtd.norm_mul) gives S(d, n)·S(d, n′) ⊆ S(d, nn′) and the action of S(d, 1) = Pell.Solution₁ d on S(d, n) (the identification S(d, 1) = unitary elements is Zsqrtd.norm_eq_one_iff_mem_unitary).
3. Conjugation x + y√d ↦ x − y√d and negation preserve S(d, n).

**Acceptance.**

- (3, 1) and (27, 11) lie in S(6, 3), and (27 + 11√6) = (3 + √6)(5 + 2√6) with (5, 2) ∈ S(6, 1).
- S(3, −1) is empty (reduce mod 3), S(34, −1) is empty although x² − 34y² = −1 has rational solutions such as (5/3, 1/3).

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `pellNormSet` | data | S(d, n) = {z : ℤ√d | Zsqrtd.norm z = n}. |
| `mem_pellNormSet_iff` | characterisation | ⟨x, y⟩ ∈ S(d, n) ↔ x² − d·y² = n. |
| `pellNormSet.mul_mem` | relation | z ∈ S(d, n) → w ∈ S(d, n′) → z·w ∈ S(d, n·n′). |
| `pellNormSet.solution_mul_mem` | structure | For a : Pell.Solution₁ d and z ∈ S(d, n), a·z ∈ S(d, n); this is a MulAction of Pell.Solution₁ d on S(d, n). |
| `pellNormSet.star_mem` | relation | z ∈ S(d, n) → conj(z) ∈ S(d, n), and −z ∈ S(d, n). |
| `pellNormSet_one` | compatibility | S(d, 1) is the set of elements of Mathlib's unitary submonoid, i.e. the image of Pell.Solution₁ d. |
| `pellNormSet_zero` | other | For d not a square, S(d, 0) = {0}. |

**Uses.**

- Conrad, Pell's equation II, Theorem 3.3 and Corollary 3.5: every element of S(d, n) is a Pell multiple of one in an explicit box
- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/negative-pell-classification: S(d, −1) ∪ S(d, 1) is a cyclic group up to sign when S(d, −1) is nonempty
- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/small-norm-solutions-are-convergents: positive elements of S(d, n) with |n| < √d come from convergents of √d
- LogicAndDefinabilityInNumberTheory:LD.4: Pell-type equations x² − (a² − 1)y² = 1 and their norm-n variants enter the Diophantine definition of exponentiation

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `mem_pellNormSet_six_three` (computation): ⟨3, 1⟩ ∈ S(6, 3) and ⟨27, 11⟩ ∈ S(6, 3).
- `pellNormSet_three_neg_one` (non-example): S(3, −1) = ∅: x² ≡ −1 (mod 3) is impossible.
- `pellNormSet_zero_of_not_square` (degenerate): S(2, 0) = {0}.
- `pellNormSet_one_compat` (compatibility): z ∈ S(d, 1) ↔ z ∈ unitary (ℤ√d), the carrier of Mathlib's Pell.Solution₁ d.

**Prerequisites.** `mathlib:Zsqrtd`, `mathlib:Zsqrtd.norm`, `mathlib:Zsqrtd.norm_mul`, `mathlib:Pell.Solution₁`, `mathlib:Zsqrtd.norm_eq_one_iff_mem_unitary`

**Sources.**

- conrad-pell-two-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, II*, §3, p. 4, the paragraph before Example 3.1.

  > The key algebraic idea is that solutions to x² − dy² = n remain solutions when multiplied by solutions of x² − dy² = 1: if a² − db² = 1 and x² − dy² = n then the coefficients of the product x′ + y′√d := (a + b√d)(x + y√d) satisfy x′² − dy′² = n

  The set S(d, n) and the action of the Pell solutions.

- conrad-pell-one-2025 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, I*, §7, Theorem 7.1, p. 10.

  > Theorem 7.1. If x² − dy² = n and x′² − dy′² = n′ then the coefficients of (x + y√d)(x′ + y′√d) are a solution of X² − dY² = nn′.

  The multiplicativity API item.

---

#### `negative-pell-solution-ordering` — Positive solutions of x² − dy² = −1 are ordered by either coordinate

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let d > 0 be a nonsquare. (a) If x² − dy² = −1 with x, y ∈ ℤ and x + y√d > 1, then x ≥ 1 and y ≥ 1. (b) If x² − dy² = −1 and a² − db² = −1 with x, y, a, b ≥ 1, then a + b√d < x + y√d ⇔ (a < x and b < y) ⇔ (a < x or b < y).

**Hypotheses.**

- d > 0 not a square; solutions in integers.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. (a): 1/(x + y√d) = −(x − y√d) = −x + y√d, so x + y√d > 1 > −x + y√d > 0; adding gives x > 0, and then y√d > x > 0.
2. (b): if x + y√d > a + b√d ≥ 1 + √d > 1, taking reciprocals gives −x + y√d < −a + b√d; adding the two inequalities gives a < x; then db² = 1 + a² < 1 + x² = dy² gives b < y. The remaining equivalences are the same monotonicity.

**Acceptance.**

- For d = 2: (1, 1) < (7, 5) < (41, 29) in both coordinates.

**Prerequisites.** `mathlib:Zsqrtd.norm`

**Sources.**

- conrad-pell-one-2025 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, I*, §7, Lemma 7.3, p. 11.

  > Lemma 7.3. If x² − dy² = −1 in Z and x + y√d > 1 then x ≥ 1 and y ≥ 1.

  Part (a).

- conrad-pell-one-2025 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, I*, §7, Lemma 7.4, p. 11.

  > Lemma 7.4. Suppose x² − dy² = −1 and a² − db² = −1 in Z where x, y, a, b ≥ 1. Then a + b√d < x + y√d ⇐⇒ a < x and b < y ⇐⇒ a < x or b < y.

  Part (b).

---

#### `negative-pell-least-solution-squares-to-fundamental` — The square of the least negative Pell solution is the fundamental Pell solution

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let d > 0 be a nonsquare such that x² − dy² = −1 has a solution in positive integers, and let (x₁, y₁) be the one with least y₁. Then (x₁ + y₁√d)² = X₁ + Y₁√d, where (X₁, Y₁) = (x₁² + dy₁², 2x₁y₁) is the fundamental solution of x² − dy² = 1 in the sense of Mathlib's Pell.IsFundamental.

**Hypotheses.**

- d > 0 nonsquare; a positive solution of the negative equation exists.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. (x₁ + y₁√d)² has norm 1 and positive coordinates, so by Mathlib's Pell.IsFundamental.eq_zpow_or_neg_zpow and positivity it is u^k for the fundamental solution u = X₁ + Y₁√d and some k ≥ 1.
2. If k = 2ℓ were even, x₁ + y₁√d = ±u^ℓ would have norm 1, not −1; so k = 2ℓ + 1.
3. Then u = (x₁ + y₁√d)²u^{−2ℓ} = (a + b√d)² with a + b√d = (x₁ + y₁√d)u^{−ℓ} of norm −1; 2ab = Y₁ > 0, so a, b may be taken positive.
4. If ℓ > 0 then (x₁ + y₁√d)² = u^{2ℓ+1} > u = (a + b√d)² > 1, so x₁ + y₁√d > a + b√d and y₁ > b by negative-pell-solution-ordering, contradicting minimality; hence ℓ = 0 and (x₁ + y₁√d)² = u.

**Acceptance.**

- d = 2: (1 + √2)² = 3 + 2√2, the fundamental solution of x² − 2y² = 1.
- d = 5: (2 + √5)² = 9 + 4√5.

**Prerequisites.** `negative-pell-solution-ordering`, `mathlib:Pell.IsFundamental`, `mathlib:Pell.IsFundamental.eq_zpow_or_neg_zpow`

**Sources.**

- conrad-pell-one-2025 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, I*, §7, proof of Theorem 7.5, p. 12.

  > We will show X₁ + Y₁√d = (x₁ + y₁√d)².

  The lemma is the first half of the source's proof of Theorem 7.5.

---

#### `negative-pell-classification` — Classification of the solutions of x² − dy² = ±1 when the negative equation is solvable

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Let d > 0 be a nonsquare such that x² − dy² = −1 has a solution in positive integers, and let (x₁, y₁) be the one with least y₁; put ε = x₁ + y₁√d, a unit of ℤ[√d] of norm −1. Then the integer solutions of x² − dy² = ±1 are exactly the x + y√d = ±ε^k with k ∈ ℤ; those of x² − dy² = −1 have k odd and those of x² − dy² = 1 have k even.

**Hypotheses.**

- d > 0 nonsquare; S(d, −1) contains an element with positive coordinates. (No solvability criterion is asserted: whether S(d, −1) is nonempty is decided by generalised-pell-finite-classes-and-bounded-search, and necessary local conditions are negative-pell-local-obstruction.)

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. ±ε^k has norm (−1)^k (multiplicativity of the norm).
2. A positive solution of norm 1 is a positive power of the fundamental solution ε² (negative-pell-least-solution-squares-to-fundamental and Mathlib's Pell.IsFundamental.eq_zpow_or_neg_zpow), hence an even power of ε.
3. A positive solution α of norm −1: α² has norm 1 and is ε^{2k}, so α = ε^k by taking positive square roots, and k is odd because the norm is −1.
4. Solutions that are not positive: by negative-pell-solution-ordering(a), α ∉ (1, ∞); if α ≠ ±1 then exactly one of α⁻¹, −α⁻¹, −α lies in (1, ∞) and has coordinates equal to those of α up to sign, so it is ε^K for some K ≥ 1.

**Acceptance.**

- d = 2, ε = 1 + √2: the solutions of x² − 2y² = −1 in positive integers are (1, 1), (7, 5), (41, 29), from ε, ε³, ε⁵.
- d = a² + 1 (a ≥ 1): ε = a + √(a² + 1).
- The statement complements Mathlib's Pell.IsFundamental.eq_zpow_or_neg_zpow, which classifies only the norm-one solutions.

**Prerequisites.** `negative-pell-least-solution-squares-to-fundamental`, `negative-pell-solution-ordering`, `generalised-pell-solution-set`, `mathlib:Pell.IsFundamental.eq_zpow_or_neg_zpow`, `mathlib:Zsqrtd.isUnit_iff_norm_isUnit`

**Sources.**

- conrad-pell-one-2025 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, I*, §7, Theorem 7.5, p. 12.

  > Theorem 7.5. Assume x² − dy² = −1 has a solution in positive integers and let (x₁, y₁) be that solution where y₁ is minimal. The integral solutions to both equations x² − dy² = ±1 are, up to sign, generated from (x₁, y₁) by taking integral powers of x₁ + y₁√d

  The theorem, verbatim.

---

#### `negative-pell-local-obstruction` — Congruence obstructions to x² − dy² = −1

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let d ∈ ℤ. If 4 ∣ d, or if some prime p ≡ 3 (mod 4) divides d, then x² − dy² = −1 has no integer solution. (More generally, x² − dy² = n has no solution when x² ≡ n has no solution modulo some p dividing d with p ∤ n.)

**Hypotheses.**

- d ∈ ℤ; no nonsquare hypothesis is needed.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. A solution gives x² ≡ −1 (mod p) for every p ∣ d; for p ≡ 3 (mod 4) this is impossible by Mathlib's ZMod.mod_four_ne_three_of_sq_eq_neg_one.
2. If 4 ∣ d, x² ≡ −1 (mod 4) is impossible because squares are 0 or 1 modulo 4.

**Acceptance.**

- x² − 3y² = −1 and x² − 21y² = −1 have no solution; x² − 34y² = −1 passes these tests but has no solution (so the condition is necessary, not sufficient).
- The generalised form rules out x² − 5y² = 2 (2 is not a square mod 5).

**Prerequisites.** `mathlib:ZMod.mod_four_ne_three_of_sq_eq_neg_one`, `mathlib:ZMod`

**Sources.**

- conrad-pell-two-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, II*, §3, Remark 3.6, p. 7.

  > Remark 3.6. For the negative Pell equation x² − dy² = −1, with squarefree d, the existence of a solution in Z requires no prime factor of d to be 3 mod 4.

  The obstruction at primes ≡ 3 (mod 4), stated in the source without proof; the node drops the squarefree hypothesis and adds the obstruction at 4.

- conrad-pell-one-2025 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, I*, §6, Examples 6.1–6.2 and Remark 6.4, pp. 8–9.

  > Example 6.1. If integers x and y satisfy x² − 3y² = −1 then reduce both sides mod 3 to get x² ≡ −1 mod 3. This congruence has no solution: the only squares mod 3 are 0 and 1.

  The generalised congruence form and the example d = 3; Remark 6.4 supplies d = 34 as the non-sufficiency example.

---

#### `chebyshev-bound-generalised-pell` — Chebyshev's bound for the generalised Pell equation

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Let d > 0 be a nonsquare, and u = a + b√d with a, b positive integers and a² − db² = 1. For every n ≠ 0, every integer solution of x² − dy² = n is (x′ + y′√d)u^k for some k ∈ ℤ and some solution (x′, y′) of x′² − dy′² = n with |x′| ≤ √|n|(√u + 1/√u)/2 and |y′| ≤ √|n|(√u + 1/√u)/(2√d). If n > 0 one may take |y′| ≤ √n(√u − 1/√u)/(2√d).

**Hypotheses.**

- d > 0 nonsquare; u > 1 a Pell solution with positive coordinates (for instance the fundamental one, which exists by Mathlib's Pell.exists_of_not_isSquare); n ≠ 0.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. For α = x + y√d with norm n put L(α) = (log|x + y√d|, log|x − y√d|) ∈ ℝ²; L is multiplicative-to-additive, and L(u) = (log u)(1, −1) because |a − b√d| = 1/u.
2. Write L(α) = c₁(1, 1) + c₂L(u); adding coordinates gives c₁ = (log|n|)/2. Choose k ∈ ℤ with |c₂ − k| ≤ 1/2 and set α′ = αu^{−k} = x′ + y′√d, which has integer coordinates and norm n.
3. Then {|x′ + y′√d|, |x′ − y′√d|} = {s, |n|/s} with √|n| ≤ s ≤ √(|n|u), and |x′| ≤ (s + |n|/s)/2, |y′| ≤ (s + |n|/s)/(2√d); t ↦ t + |n|/t is increasing for t ≥ √|n|, which gives the bounds.
4. For n > 0 the two conjugates have the same sign, so |y′| = |s − n/s|/(2√d) ≤ (√(nu) − √(n/u))/(2√d).

**Acceptance.**

- x² − 6y² = 3 with u = 5 + 2√6: the bounds give |y′| ≤ 1 and the fundamental solutions (±3, ±1).
- x² − 37y² = 11 with u = 73 + 12√37: no solution inside the box, hence none at all (Conrad, Example 4.5).

**Prerequisites.** `generalised-pell-solution-set`, `mathlib:Pell.exists_of_not_isSquare`, `mathlib:Zsqrtd.norm_mul`

**Sources.**

- conrad-pell-two-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, II*, §3, Theorem 3.3, p. 4.

  > Theorem 3.3. Fix u = a + b√d where a² − db² = 1 with a and b in Z⁺. For each n ∈ Z − {0}, every integral solution of x² − dy² = n is (x′ + y′√d)u^k where x′² − dy′² = n, k ∈ Z, and

  The theorem; the displayed bounds (3.1) and the refinement for n > 0 follow on the same page.

- conrad-pell-two-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, II*, §3, Remark 3.4, p. 7.

  > the bounds in Theorem 3.3 were found by Chebyshev in 1851 [2, pp. 260, 262].

  The attribution in the node's title.

---

#### `generalised-pell-finite-classes-and-bounded-search` — Finitely many classes of solutions, and the complete bounded search

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Let d > 0 be a nonsquare, n ≠ 0, and u = a + b√d as in chebyshev-bound-generalised-pell. (a) There is a finite set F ⊆ S(d, n) such that every element of S(d, n) is a Pell multiple ±f·u^k of an element of F; one may take F = S(d, n) ∩ B with B the box |x| ≤ √|n|(√u + 1/√u)/2, |y| ≤ √|n|(√u + 1/√u)/(2√d). (b) Consequently x² − dy² = n has an integer solution if and only if it has one in B, which is a finite check: the generalised Pell equation, and in particular the negative Pell equation, is decidable once a nontrivial Pell solution u is known.

**Hypotheses.**

- d > 0 nonsquare; n ≠ 0; u a Pell solution with positive coordinates.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. (a) is chebyshev-bound-generalised-pell together with finiteness of the integer points of B.
