2. (b): a solution outside B is a Pell multiple of one inside B; conversely a solution in B is a solution.
3. Mathlib's Pell.exists_of_not_isSquare provides u, so the bound is effective in d and n once u is computed.

**Acceptance.**

- x² − 194y² = −1 has no solution: the box for n = −1 contains no solution (Conrad, Example 4.6), although 194 = 2·97 passes the congruence test of negative-pell-local-obstruction.
- The search is complete only because of the proved bound, as the stage's acceptance requires.

**Prerequisites.** `chebyshev-bound-generalised-pell`, `mathlib:Pell.exists_of_not_isSquare`

**Sources.**

- conrad-pell-two-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, II*, §3, Corollary 3.5, p. 7.

  > Corollary 3.5. For a generalized Pell equation x² − dy² = n with n ≠ 0 there is a finite set of solutions such that every solution is a Pell multiple of one of these solutions.

  Part (a).

- conrad-pell-two-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, II*, §3, proof of Corollary 3.5, p. 7.

  > Proof #1: Each solution is a Pell multiple of a solution with |x| ≤ √|n|(√u + 1/√u)/2 and |y| ≤ √|n|(√u + 1/√u)/(2√d) by Theorem 3.3, so x and y have finitely many choices.

  Part (b): the source uses the same box to prove nonexistence in Examples 4.5–4.7.

---

#### `small-norm-solutions-are-convergents` — Solutions of x² − dy² = n with |n| < √d come from convergents of √d

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Let d > 0 be a nonsquare and n an integer with 0 < |n| < √d. If x, y are positive integers with x² − dy² = n, then the rational number x/y is a convergent of the regular continued fraction of √d: x/y = Real.convergent (√d) k for some k.

**Hypotheses.**

- d > 0 nonsquare, 0 < |n| < √d, x, y > 0.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. By Legendre's theorem (Mathlib's Real.exists_rat_eq_convergent), it suffices to show |ξ − q| < 1/(2 den(q)²) for the relevant ξ and q.
2. If n > 0 then x > y√d, and |x/y − √d| = |n|/(y²(x/y + √d)) < 1/(2y²) since x/y + √d > 2√d > 2|n|; as den(x/y) ≤ y this gives the hypothesis.
3. If n < 0 then x < y√d, and the same computation gives |y/x − 1/√d| < 1/(2x²), so y/x is a convergent of 1/√d; since 0 < 1/√d < 1, Mathlib's Real.convergent_succ gives Real.convergent (1/√d) (k + 1) = (Real.convergent (√d) k)⁻¹, so x/y is a convergent of √d.

**Acceptance.**

- d = 6: √6 = [2; 2, 4], convergents 2, 5/2, 22/9, 49/20, …; 5² − 6·2² = 1 and 2² − 6·1² = −2 appear.
- d = 13: the only n with 0 < |n| < √13 for which x² − 13y² = n is solvable are ±1 and ±3 (Conrad, Example 5.4).
- Only the equality x/y = p_k/q_k of rationals is asserted: for non-squarefree n, (x, y) can be a multiple (gp_k, gq_k) with g² ∣ n.

**Prerequisites.** `mathlib:Real.exists_rat_eq_convergent`, `mathlib:Real.convergent`, `mathlib:Real.convergent_succ`

**Sources.**

- conrad-pell-two-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, II*, §5, Theorem 5.1, p. 10.

  > Theorem 5.1. If positive integers x and y satisfy x² − dy² = n with |n| < √d then x/y is a convergent to the continued fraction of √d.

  The theorem, verbatim; the source's proof is the one in the proof steps.

---

#### `pell-unit-solutions-are-convergents` — Positive solutions of x² − dy² = ±1 are numerators and denominators of convergents

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let d > 1 be a nonsquare. If x, y are positive integers with x² − dy² = ±1, then there is k with Real.convergent (√d) k = x/y in lowest terms, i.e. x and y are the numerator and denominator of that convergent.

**Hypotheses.**

- d > 1 nonsquare (so that 1 < √d), x, y > 0.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Apply small-norm-solutions-are-convergents with n = ±1 (|n| = 1 < √d).
2. x² − dy² = ±1 forces gcd(x, y) = 1, so x/y is already in lowest terms and its numerator and denominator are x and y.

**Acceptance.**

- d = 2: (1, 1), (3, 2), (7, 5), (17, 12) are the numerators and denominators of the convergents 1, 3/2, 7/5, 17/12 of √2.

**Prerequisites.** `small-norm-solutions-are-convergents`

**Sources.**

- conrad-pell-two-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, II*, §5, Corollary 5.2, p. 11.

  > Corollary 5.2. For each positive solution to x² − dy² = ±1, there is a convergent p/q to √d such that x = p and y = q.

  The statement, verbatim.

---

#### `small-norm-pell-decision-by-convergents` — The continued-fraction decision procedure for x² − dy² = n with |n| < √d

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Let d > 1 be a nonsquare, 0 < |n| < √d, and u = a + b√d a Pell solution with positive coordinates; let Y = √|n|(√u + 1/√u)/(2√d). Then x² − dy² = n has a solution in positive integers if and only if either n is a perfect square, or there are k, g ∈ ℕ with g ≥ 1, g² ∣ n, g·q_k ≤ Y and p_k² − dq_k² = n/g², where p_k/q_k is the k-th convergent Real.convergent (√d) k in lowest terms. Only the finitely many k with q_k ≤ Y need to be checked, since q_k ≥ F_{k+1} (Fibonacci numbers).

**Hypotheses.**

- d > 1 nonsquare, 0 < |n| < √d, u as in chebyshev-bound-generalised-pell.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. If n = g² is a perfect square, (ga, gb) is a positive solution. If k, g are as in the statement, (gp_k, gq_k) is a positive solution (p_k ≥ 1 because √d > 1).
2. Conversely, let a positive solution exist and n not be a square. By generalised-pell-finite-classes-and-bounded-search there is a solution (x′, y′) in the box B; y′ ≠ 0 because n is not a square, and x′ ≠ 0 because |n| < d; so (|x′|, |y′|) is a positive solution with |y′| ≤ Y.
3. By small-norm-solutions-are-convergents, |x′|/|y′| = p_k/q_k for some k; with g = gcd(x′, y′), |x′| = gp_k, |y′| = gq_k, g² ∣ n, p_k² − dq_k² = n/g² and gq_k ≤ Y.
4. Finiteness: the denominators of the convergents satisfy q_k ≥ F_{k+1} (Mathlib's GenContFract.succ_nth_fib_le_of_nth_den, transported to Real.convergent by Real.convs_eq_convergent, with p_k, q_k coprime by SimpContFract.determinant).

**Acceptance.**

- d = 13, n = 3: 4² − 13·1² = 3 with 4/1 a convergent of √13 = [3; 1, 1, 1, 1, 6].
- d = 13, n = 2: no convergent up to the bound gives norm 2 or 2/g², so x² − 13y² = 2 has no solution.
- This decides the negative Pell equation (n = −1) without the periodicity of the continued fraction of √d.

**Prerequisites.** `generalised-pell-finite-classes-and-bounded-search`, `small-norm-solutions-are-convergents`, `mathlib:Real.convs_eq_convergent`, `mathlib:GenContFract.succ_nth_fib_le_of_nth_den`, `mathlib:SimpContFract.determinant`

**Sources.**

- conrad-pell-two-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, II*, §5, p. 11, after Example 5.3.

  > Not only is the continued fraction of √d periodic, but so is x² − dy² when x/y runs through the convergents to √d. All distinct values of x² − dy² when x/y is a convergent to √d occur before the last term in the second period of the continued fraction.

  The source's decision procedure uses the periodicity of the continued fraction of √d, which it states without proof; the node replaces periodicity by the proved bound of Theorem 3.3, so that only finitely many convergents are checked.

- conrad-pell-two-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, II*, §5, Theorem 5.1, p. 10.

  > If positive integers x and y satisfy x² − dy² = n with |n| < √d then x/y is a convergent to the continued fraction of √d.

  The link used in the second proof step.

---

### Exponential equations

The Ramanujan–Nagell equation x² + 7 = 2ⁿ has exactly the solutions n = 3, 4, 5, 7, 15. The even case is a factorisation of 7. The odd case works in ℤ[(1 + √−7)/2], which is norm-Euclidean with units ±1 and where 2 = θθ′ splits into two non-associate primes: the factorisation of (x² + 7)/4 = 2^m forces θ^m − θ′^m = ±(θ − θ′), a congruence modulo θ′² fixes the sign, a binomial expansion modulo 7 restricts m to three classes modulo 42, and a 7-adic argument using the lifting-the-exponent lemma (in Mathlib) allows at most one m per class. A complete formalisation of this proof against Mathlib exists outside Tau Ceti (Banwait, arXiv:2604.09808, using the same QuadraticAlgebra ℤ (−2) 1); an implementation should coordinate with that work and check its licence before reusing it. Levi ben Gershon's theorem, that the only powers of 2 and 3 differing by 1 are (1, 2), (2, 3), (3, 4) and (8, 9), is proved by congruences modulo 3, 4 and 8. Catalan–Mihăilescu, whose proof is cyclotomic rather than elementary, is not planned in this layer; it is recorded as a gap for the maintainer to route.

#### `ramanujan-nagell-ring-norm-euclidean` — ℤ[(1 + √−7)/2] is norm-Euclidean

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let R = ℤ[θ] with θ = (1 + √−7)/2, realised as Mathlib's QuadraticAlgebra ℤ (−2) 1 (θ² = θ − 2); θ′ = 1 − θ; norm N(x + yθ) = x² + xy + 2y² (Mathlib's QuadraticAlgebra.norm). For all α, β ∈ R with β ≠ 0 there are q, r ∈ R with α = βq + r and N(r) < N(β). Consequently R is a Euclidean domain, hence a principal ideal domain and a unique factorisation domain.

**Hypotheses.**

- The norm is the one of Mathlib's QuadraticAlgebra.norm for a = −2, b = 1.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Write γ = α/β = ξ + η√−7 with ξ, η ∈ ℚ; it suffices to find q ∈ R with N(γ − q) < 1, since N is multiplicative (N(α − βq) = N(β)N(γ − q)).
2. Elements of R are (j + k√−7)/2 with j ≡ k (mod 2). Choose k ∈ ℤ nearest to 2η (|η − k/2| ≤ 1/4) and j ≡ k (mod 2) nearest to 2ξ (|ξ − j/2| ≤ 1/2).
3. Then N(γ − q) = (ξ − j/2)² + 7(η − k/2)² ≤ 1/4 + 7/16 = 11/16 < 1.
4. A Euclidean domain is a principal ideal domain and a unique factorisation monoid (Mathlib's instances from EuclideanDomain).

**Acceptance.**

- Rounding both coordinates of ℤ[√−7] would only give N ≤ 2: the half-integers are essential.
- The same computation fails for ℤ[√−5], whose class number is 2.

**Prerequisites.** `mathlib:QuadraticAlgebra`, `mathlib:QuadraticAlgebra.norm`, `mathlib:EuclideanDomain`, `mathlib:UniqueFactorizationMonoid`

**Sources.**

- banwait-ramanujan-nagell-2026 — Barinder S. Banwait, *A formal proof of the Ramanujan–Nagell theorem in Lean 4*, §2.3, pp. 3–4.

  > Hence N is a Euclidean function on R , so R is a Euclidean domain, and therefore a principal ideal domain (PID) and a unique factorization domain (UFD).

  The conclusion; the rounding argument precedes it on p. 3.

---

#### `ramanujan-nagell-ring-units-and-primes` — Units and the primes above 2 in ℤ[(1 + √−7)/2]

*lemma.*

**Statement.** In R (as in ramanujan-nagell-ring-norm-euclidean): the units are ±1; θθ′ = 2, θ + θ′ = 1 and (θ − θ′)² = −7; θ and θ′ are prime elements of norm 2 and are not associates.

**Hypotheses.**

- R as above.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. 4N(x + yθ) = (2x + y)² + 7y², so N = 1 forces y = 0 and x = ±1; units have norm 1.
2. N(θ) = N(θ′) = 2 is a rational prime, so θ, θ′ are irreducible, hence prime in the UFD R.
3. θ/θ′ is not in R (its norm is 1 but it is not ±1), so θ and θ′ are not associates; the identities are direct computations with θ² = θ − 2.

**Acceptance.**

- θ′ = 1 − θ; θ − θ′ = 2θ − 1 squares to 4θ² − 4θ + 1 = −7.

**Prerequisites.** `ramanujan-nagell-ring-norm-euclidean`, `mathlib:QuadraticAlgebra.norm`

**Sources.**

- banwait-ramanujan-nagell-2026 — Barinder S. Banwait, *A formal proof of the Ramanujan–Nagell theorem in Lean 4*, §2.3, p. 4.

  > The units of R are precisely ±1 : a unit has norm 1 , and 1/4((2x + y)² + 7y²) = 1 forces y = 0 and x = ±1 . Finally, N(θ) = N(θ′) = 2 is prime in Z , so θ and θ′ are irreducible, hence prime; and they are not associates, since their ratio is not a unit.

  The lemma, verbatim in substance.

---

#### `ramanujan-nagell-even-case` — The Ramanujan–Nagell equation with even exponent

*lemma.*

**Statement.** If x ∈ ℤ, n ∈ ℕ is even and x² + 7 = 2ⁿ, then n = 4 and x = ±3.

**Hypotheses.**

- n even.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. (2^{n/2} + |x|)(2^{n/2} − |x|) = 7 with both factors positive integers, so 2^{n/2} + |x| = 7 and 2^{n/2} − |x| = 1; adding, 2^{n/2+1} = 8, so n = 4 and |x| = 3.

**Acceptance.**

- n = 0 and n = 2 give x² = −6 and x² = −3, impossible.

**Prerequisites.** none beyond routine arithmetic.

**Sources.**

- banwait-ramanujan-nagell-2026 — Barinder S. Banwait, *A formal proof of the Ramanujan–Nagell theorem in Lean 4*, §2.2, p. 3.

  > If n is even, then x² + 7 = 2ⁿ gives a factorization of integers (2^{n/2} + x)(2^{n/2} − x) = 7 . Since 7 is prime, we must have 2^{n/2} + x = 7 and 2^{n/2} − x = 1 , giving 2^{1+n/2} = 8 , hence n = 4 and x = 3 .

  The lemma, with the sign of x restored.

---

#### `ramanujan-nagell-factorisation` — Factorisation of x² + 7 = 2ⁿ in ℤ[(1 + √−7)/2]

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let n ≥ 5 be odd, m = n − 2, and x a positive odd integer with x² + 7 = 2ⁿ; write x = 2k + 1. Then α = k + θ = (x + √−7)/2 and β = k + θ′ = (x − √−7)/2 satisfy αβ = 2^m = θ^mθ′^m and α − β = θ − θ′ = √−7, and α ∈ {±θ^m, ±θ′^m}. Consequently θ^m − θ′^m = ±(θ − θ′).

**Hypotheses.**

- n odd, n ≥ 5 (so m ≥ 3 odd); x > 0 (x is odd because 2ⁿ − 7 is odd).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. x² + 7 = 4(k² + k + 2), so αβ = k² + k + 2 = 2^m = (θθ′)^m.
2. A common prime factor of α and β divides α − β = θ − θ′, of norm 7, and divides 2^m, so it is θ or θ′ (ramanujan-nagell-ring-units-and-primes) of norm 2; 2 ∤ 7, so α and β are coprime.
3. By unique factorisation (ramanujan-nagell-ring-norm-euclidean) and units ±1, α = ±θ^m or α = ±θ′^m.
4. Taking conjugates (θ ↔ θ′) gives β, and subtracting gives θ − θ′ = α − β = ±(θ^m − θ′^m).

**Acceptance.**

- x = 5, n = 5, m = 3, k = 2: θ³ = −θ − 2 and θ′³ = θ − 3, so α = 2 + θ = −θ³ and θ³ − θ′³ = −(2θ − 1) = −(θ − θ′).

**Prerequisites.** `ramanujan-nagell-ring-units-and-primes`, `ramanujan-nagell-ring-norm-euclidean`

**Sources.**

- banwait-ramanujan-nagell-2026 — Barinder S. Banwait, *A formal proof of the Ramanujan–Nagell theorem in Lean 4*, §2.4, p. 4.

  > Since R is a unique factorization domain with units ±1 , comparing the two factorizations of θ^mθ′^m forces (x + √−7)/2 = ± θ^m or ± θ′^m.

  The factorisation conclusion; §2.5 opens with the consequence θ^m − θ′^m = ±√−7.

---

#### `ramanujan-nagell-sign` — The sign in θ^m − θ′^m = ±(θ − θ′)

*lemma.*

**Statement.** If m ≥ 3 is odd and θ^m − θ′^m = ±(θ − θ′) in R, then the sign is negative: θ^m − θ′^m = −(θ − θ′).

**Hypotheses.**

- m odd, m ≥ 3 (for m = 1 the positive sign holds trivially).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Suppose θ^m − θ′^m = θ − θ′. Modulo θ′² (using θθ′ = 2 and θ = 1 − θ′), θ² = (1 − θ′)² ≡ 1, so θ^m ≡ θ as m is odd, and θ′^m ≡ 0 as m ≥ 2.
2. Then θ − θ′ ≡ θ^m − θ′^m ≡ θ (mod θ′²), so θ′ ≡ 0 (mod θ′²), impossible since θ′ is not a unit.

**Acceptance.**

- m = 3: θ³ − θ′³ = (−θ − 2) − (−θ′ − 2) = −(θ − θ′), the negative sign.

**Prerequisites.** `ramanujan-nagell-ring-units-and-primes`

**Sources.**

- banwait-ramanujan-nagell-2026 — Barinder S. Banwait, *A formal proof of the Ramanujan–Nagell theorem in Lean 4*, §2.5, p. 4.

  > We claim that the positive sign cannot occur. For, if it does, then we have θ^m−θ′^m = θ−θ′ , and working modulo θ′² and using θθ′ = 2 and θ = 1 −θ′ , we have θ² = (1 −θ′)² ≡ 1 (mod θ′²)

  The lemma and its proof.

---

#### `ramanujan-nagell-residues-mod-42` — The exponent lies in three classes modulo 42

*lemma.*

**Statement.** If m is odd and θ^m − θ′^m = −(θ − θ′), then −2^{m−1} ≡ m (mod 7), and hence m ≡ 3, 5 or 13 (mod 42).

**Hypotheses.**

- m odd.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Binomial expansion of ((1 + √−7)/2)^m − ((1 − √−7)/2)^m gives 2^{1−m}√−7(C(m,1) − 7C(m,3) + 7²C(m,5) − ⋯); comparing with −√−7 and multiplying by 2^{m−1} gives −2^{m−1} = C(m,1) − 7C(m,3) + ⋯, so −2^{m−1} ≡ m (mod 7).
2. 2 has order 3 modulo 7, so −2^{m−1} mod 7 depends on m mod 3; together with m mod 7 and m odd this is a condition on m mod 42, and a direct check of the 21 odd residues gives exactly 3, 5, 13.

**Acceptance.**

- m = 3: −4 ≡ 3; m = 5: −16 ≡ 5; m = 13: −4096 ≡ 13 (mod 7).

**Prerequisites.** `ramanujan-nagell-sign`

**Sources.**

- banwait-ramanujan-nagell-2026 — Barinder S. Banwait, *A formal proof of the Ramanujan–Nagell theorem in Lean 4*, §2.6, p. 5.

  > A direct check shows that the only solutions to (4) modulo 42 are m ≡ 3, 5, or 13 (mod 42).

  The conclusion; congruence (4) is −2^{m−1} ≡ m (mod 7), derived just before by the binomial expansion.

---

#### `ramanujan-nagell-one-per-class` — At most one exponent in each class modulo 42

*lemma.*

**Statement.** There are no odd m < m′ with m ≡ m′ (mod 42) such that both satisfy θ^m − θ′^m = −(θ − θ′) = θ^{m′} − θ′^{m′}.

**Hypotheses.**

- m, m′ odd; the identity for both.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Let d = m′ − m, so 42 ∣ d; let l = v₇(d) ≥ 1; work modulo 7^{l+1} in R.
2. Binomial expansion: (1 + √−7)^d ≡ 1 + d√−7 (mod 7^{l+1}), because every term with j ≥ 1 has 7-adic valuation at least j + l − v₇(k) ≥ l + 1 (using k·C(d,k) = d·C(d−1,k−1)).
3. Lifting the exponent (Mathlib's Int.emultiplicity_pow_sub_pow with 8 − 1 = 7 and d/3): v₇(2^d − 1) = v₇(8^{d/3} − 1) = 1 + l, so 2^d ≡ 1 (mod 7^{l+1}) and θ^d ≡ 1 + d√−7, θ′^d ≡ 1 − d√−7.
4. Then θ^{m′} − θ′^{m′} ≡ (θ^m − θ′^m) + d√−7(θ^m + θ′^m), so d√−7·P ≡ 0 with P = θ^m + θ′^m.
5. P_k = θ^k + θ′^k satisfies P_{k+2} = P_{k+1} − 2P_k with P₀ = 2, P₁ = 1, and modulo 7 it cycles 2, 1, 4, never 0; so P is invertible modulo 7^{l+1} and d√−7 ≡ 0, i.e. the coordinates (−d, 2d) of d(2θ − 1) are divisible by 7^{l+1}, contradicting v₇(d) = l.

**Acceptance.**

- The three classes contain m = 3, 5, 13 respectively, so these are the only odd m ≥ 3 with the negative-sign identity.

**Prerequisites.** `ramanujan-nagell-residues-mod-42`, `mathlib:Int.emultiplicity_pow_sub_pow`

**Sources.**

- banwait-ramanujan-nagell-2026 — Barinder S. Banwait, *A formal proof of the Ramanujan–Nagell theorem in Lean 4*, §2.7, pp. 5–6.

  > It remains to show that each class in (5) contains at most one value of m . Suppose, for contradiction, that two solutions give exponents m < m′ with m ≡ m′ (mod 42) .

  The lemma; the proof steps follow the source's (6)–(9).

---

#### `ramanujan-nagell-theorem` — The Ramanujan–Nagell theorem

*theorem.* **Planet:** *Ramanujan–Nagell equation*.

**Statement.** The integer solutions (x, n) ∈ ℤ × ℕ of x² + 7 = 2ⁿ are exactly (±1, 3), (±3, 4), (±5, 5), (±11, 7) and (±181, 15).

**Hypotheses.**

- x ∈ ℤ, n ∈ ℕ.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. The listed pairs are solutions (direct computation).
2. n even: ramanujan-nagell-even-case gives (±3, 4).
3. n odd: n ≤ 3 gives only n = 3, x = ±1; for n ≥ 5, ramanujan-nagell-factorisation and ramanujan-nagell-sign give θ^m − θ′^m = −(θ − θ′) with m = n − 2; ramanujan-nagell-residues-mod-42 and ramanujan-nagell-one-per-class leave m ∈ {3, 5, 13}, i.e. n ∈ {5, 7, 15}, with x = ±5, ±11, ±181.

**Acceptance.**

- 2¹⁵ − 7 = 32761 = 181².
- A complete Lean 4 formalisation against Mathlib, with no unfinished proofs, exists outside Tau Ceti (Banwait, arXiv:2604.09808); the roadmap cites it as prior work for coordination.

**Prerequisites.** `ramanujan-nagell-even-case`, `ramanujan-nagell-factorisation`, `ramanujan-nagell-sign`, `ramanujan-nagell-residues-mod-42`, `ramanujan-nagell-one-per-class`

**Sources.**

- banwait-ramanujan-nagell-2026 — Barinder S. Banwait, *A formal proof of the Ramanujan–Nagell theorem in Lean 4*, §1, p. 1 (Abstract).

  > the only integer solutions to the Diophantine equation x² + 7 = 2ⁿ are (n, x) ∈ {(3, ±1), (4, ±3), (5, ±5), (7, ±11), (15, ±181)} .

  The theorem, verbatim.

- banwait-ramanujan-nagell-2026 — Barinder S. Banwait, *A formal proof of the Ramanujan–Nagell theorem in Lean 4*, §2.8, p. 6.

  > By (5) and the uniqueness just proved, each of the residue classes m ≡ 3, 5, 13 (mod 42) contributes at most one value of m from a solution, and these are realised by m = 3, 5, 13 , that is n = 5, 7, 15 , with x = 5, 11, 181 respectively

  The assembly of the proof.

---

#### `powers-of-two-and-three-differing-by-one` — Levi ben Gershon's theorem: powers of 2 and 3 differing by 1

*theorem.*

**Statement.** The solutions (a, b) ∈ ℕ² of |2ᵃ − 3ᵇ| = 1 are (1, 0), (1, 1), (2, 1) and (3, 2); that is, the only pairs (2ᵃ, 3ᵇ) of consecutive integers are (2, 1), (2, 3), (4, 3) and (8, 9).

**Hypotheses.**

- a, b ∈ ℕ (exponent 0 allowed).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. 3ᵇ − 2ᵃ = 1: if a = 0 then 3ᵇ = 2, impossible; if a = 1 then b = 1. If a ≥ 2 then 3ᵇ ≡ 1 (mod 4), so b is even, b = 2c, and (3ᶜ − 1)(3ᶜ + 1) = 2ᵃ; the factors are powers of 2 differing by 2, so 3ᶜ − 1 = 2, c = 1, b = 2, a = 3.
2. 2ᵃ − 3ᵇ = 1: if b = 0 then a = 1. If b ≥ 1 then 2ᵃ ≡ 1 (mod 3), so a is even, a = 2c, and (2ᶜ − 1)(2ᶜ + 1) = 3ᵇ; the factors are powers of 3 differing by 2, so 2ᶜ − 1 = 1, c = 1, a = 2, b = 1.
3. Levi's own argument works modulo 8: 3ᵇ ≡ 1 or 3 (mod 8) according to the parity of b, while 2ᵃ ≡ 0 (mod 8) for a ≥ 3; the only surviving infinite case is 3^{2k} − 1 = 2ᵃ, treated as above.

**Acceptance.**

- This is the case {x, y} = {2, 3} of Catalan's equation xᵖ − y^q = 1, which (with exponents ≥ 2) has only 3² − 2³ = 1; Catalan–Mihăilescu in general is not planned here (see the gaps).
- The equation 2ᵃ − 3ᵇ = −1 with a = 3, b = 2 is the only solution with both exponents at least 2.

**Prerequisites.** none beyond routine arithmetic.

**Sources.**

- simonson-gersonides-2005 — Shai Simonson, *The Mathematics of Levi ben Gershon, the Ralbag*, the section on Levi's harmonic numbers, pp. 7–8.

  > An elementary proof that the only pairs of harmonic numbers (numbers in the form 2ⁿ3ᵐ) that differ by 1 are the pairs (1, 2), (2, 3), (3, 4) and (8, 9).

  The statement; the source sketches Levi's mod-8 argument, reproduced in the third proof step.

- leonetti-catalan-2013 — Paolo Leonetti, *On consecutive perfect powers with elementary methods*, §1, p. 1.

  > The history of the problem dates back to at least Levi ben Gerson (also known as Gersonides), who considered in 1343 the Diophantine equations 2ᵖ − 3^q = 1 and 3ᵖ − 2^q = 1.

  The same equations in the context of Catalan's conjecture.

---

### Numerical semigroups

A numerical semigroup is a submonoid S of ℕ with finite complement, equivalently one whose elements have gcd 1; its invariants are the gaps, the genus g(S) (their number), the Frobenius number F(S) (the largest gap, −1 for S = ℕ) and the multiplicity. Mathlib has the Frobenius number of a set of generators and its value mn − m − n for two coprime generators; the rest is planned here from Assi and García-Sánchez. For n ∈ S ∖ {0} the Apéry set Ap(S, n) = {s ∈ S : s − n ∉ S} has one element in each residue class modulo n, every element of S is uniquely kn + w with w ∈ Ap(S, n), and Selmer's formulas F(S) = max Ap(S, n) − n and g(S) = (1/n)ΣAp(S, n) − (n − 1)/2 follow. For two coprime generators this gives Sylvester's count g = (a − 1)(b − 1)/2. In general 2g(S) ≥ F(S) + 1, with equality exactly for the symmetric semigroups (those in which x ∉ S implies F(S) − x ∈ S), which include all two-generator semigroups and are characterised by the pairing a_i + a_{n−1−i} = a_{n−1} of the sorted Apéry set.

#### `numerical-semigroup` — Numerical semigroups, their gaps, genus and Frobenius number

*definition.* **Planet:** *Numerical semigroup*.

**Statement.** A numerical semigroup is an additive submonoid S of ℕ whose complement G(S) = ℕ ∖ S (the gaps) is finite; equivalently the subgroup of ℤ generated by S contains 1 (Assi–García-Sánchez, Proposition 1). Its genus is g(S) = #G(S), its Frobenius number is F(S) = max G(S) ∈ ℤ with the convention F(ℕ) = −1, its conductor is C(S) = F(S) + 1, and its multiplicity m(S) is the least positive element of S.

**Hypotheses.**

- S is an AddSubmonoid of ℕ (so 0 ∈ S and S is closed under addition).
- The Frobenius number is integer-valued so that S = ℕ is covered (F = −1, g = 0).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Define the predicate 'the complement of S is finite' on Mathlib's AddSubmonoid ℕ, and the invariants g, F, C, m from the finite set of gaps.
2. Proposition 1 (finite complement ⟺ gcd 1): if G(S) is finite, some s and s + 1 lie in S; conversely, if the elements of S have gcd 1, all large integers lie in S, which is Mathlib's Nat.exists_mem_closure_of_ge applied to S (a submonoid is the closure of itself).
3. Every x > F(S) lies in S, and F(S) ∉ S when S ≠ ℕ; the conductor is the least c with c + ℕ ⊆ S.
4. For S = ⟨s⟩ the submonoid generated by a set s, F(S) is the Frobenius number of Mathlib's FrobeniusNumber predicate whenever S ≠ ℕ.

**Acceptance.**

- ⟨3, 5⟩ = {0, 3, 5, 6, 8, 9, 10, …}: gaps {1, 2, 4, 7}, g = 4, F = 7, C = 8, m = 3.
- ⟨5, 7, 9⟩: F = 13, g = 8 (the source's GAP example 15).
- ⟨2, 4⟩ is a submonoid but not a numerical semigroup (all odd numbers are gaps).

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `AddSubmonoid.IsNumericalSemigroup` | data | The predicate that (S : Set ℕ)ᶜ is finite. |
| `AddSubmonoid.isNumericalSemigroup_iff_setGcd` | characterisation | S is a numerical semigroup ↔ Nat.setGcd S = 1 (Proposition 1, via Mathlib's Nat.exists_mem_closure_of_ge). |
| `AddSubmonoid.numericalGaps` | data | G(S) = ℕ ∖ S as a Finset. |
| `AddSubmonoid.numericalGenus` | data | g(S) = #G(S). |
| `AddSubmonoid.numericalFrobenius` | data | F(S) ∈ ℤ: the largest gap, or −1 if there are none. |
| `AddSubmonoid.numericalMultiplicity` | data | m(S): the least positive element of S. |
| `AddSubmonoid.mem_of_numericalFrobenius_lt` | relation | (x : ℤ) > F(S) → x ∈ S. |
| `AddSubmonoid.numericalGenus_eq_zero_iff` | characterisation | g(S) = 0 ↔ S = ⊤ ↔ F(S) = −1. |
| `AddSubmonoid.frobeniusNumber_numericalFrobenius` | compatibility | If S = AddSubmonoid.closure s ≠ ⊤, then Mathlib's FrobeniusNumber F(S).toNat s holds. |
| `AddSubmonoid.isNumericalSemigroup_closure_pair_iff` | characterisation | ⟨a, b⟩ is a numerical semigroup ↔ gcd(a, b) = 1. |

**Uses.**

- Assi–García-Sánchez, §§1–2: Apéry sets, Selmer's formulas and symmetry are statements about these invariants
- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4 (Frobenius-number target): extends Mathlib's Frobenius number of two coprime generators to arbitrary numerical semigroups and adds genus and gaps
- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/integer-linear-systems: ⟨a₁, …, a_k⟩ is the set of right-hand sides b for which a₁x₁ + ⋯ + a_kx_k = b has a solution in nonnegative integers (the source's Introduction)
- Assi–García-Sánchez, §3: semigroups of values of plane curve branches are numerical semigroups (context for the invariant conductor)

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `numericalGenus_three_five` (computation): For S = ⟨3, 5⟩: G(S) = {1, 2, 4, 7}, g(S) = 4 and F(S) = 7.
- `numericalFrobenius_five_seven_nine` (computation): For S = ⟨5, 7, 9⟩: F(S) = 13 and g(S) = 8.
- `numericalFrobenius_top` (degenerate): For S = ℕ: g(S) = 0 and F(S) = −1.
- `not_isNumericalSemigroup_two_four` (non-example): ⟨2, 4⟩ is not a numerical semigroup.
- `numericalFrobenius_pair_compat` (compatibility): F(⟨3, 5⟩) = 3·5 − 3 − 5, agreeing with Mathlib's frobeniusNumber_pair.

**Prerequisites.** `mathlib:FrobeniusNumber`, `mathlib:Nat.exists_mem_closure_of_ge`, `mathlib:Nat.setGcd`, `mathlib:exists_frobeniusNumber_iff`

**Sources.**

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §1, p. 2, the definitions of numerical semigroup, gaps and genus.

  > If 1 ∈ G, then we say that S is a numerical semigroup. We set G(S) = N\S and we call it the set of gaps of S. We denote by g(S) the cardinality of G(S), and we call g(S) the genus of S.

  The definition; Proposition 1 gives the equivalent finite-complement form used here.

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §1, Proposition 1, p. 3.

  > Proposition 1. Let S be a submonoid of N. Then S is a numerical semigroup if and only if N \ S is a finite set.

  The characterisation taken as the definition.

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §1, p. 5, the definitions of Frobenius number and conductor.

  > We set F(S) = max(N \ S) and we call it the Frobenius number of S. We set C(S) = F(S) + 1 and we call it the conductor of S.

  The Frobenius number and the conductor; the convention F(ℕ) = −1 pins the empty maximum.

---

#### `apery-set` — The Apéry set of a numerical semigroup

*definition.*

**Statement.** For a numerical semigroup S and n ∈ S ∖ {0}, Ap(S, n) = {s ∈ S : s − n ∉ S} (with s − n computed in ℤ, so every s < n in S belongs to it). It has exactly n elements, one in each residue class modulo n: Ap(S, n) = {w(0) = 0, w(1), …, w(n − 1)}, where w(i) is the least element of S congruent to i modulo n (Lemma 4).

**Hypotheses.**

- S a numerical semigroup; n ∈ S, n > 0.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Define Ap(S, n) as a subset of ℕ.
2. Each w(i) exists because S contains all large integers; w(i) ∈ Ap(S, n) since w(i) − n is smaller and congruent to i.
3. Two elements of Ap(S, n) are never congruent modulo n (the larger minus a multiple of n would lie in S), so Ap(S, n) = {w(0), …, w(n − 1)} and #Ap(S, n) = n.

**Acceptance.**

- Ap(⟨3, 5⟩, 3) = {0, 5, 10}; Ap(⟨5, 9, 21⟩, 5) = {0, 9, 18, 21, 27} (the source's GAP example 5).
- For n ∉ S the same formula gives a set of a different size: for S = ⟨5, 9, 21⟩ and n = 6 it has 9 elements.

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `AddSubmonoid.aperySet` | data | Ap(S, n) = {s ∈ S | ¬(n ≤ s ∧ s − n ∈ S)} as a Set ℕ. |
| `AddSubmonoid.mem_aperySet_iff` | characterisation | s ∈ Ap(S, n) ↔ s ∈ S ∧ ((s : ℤ) − n ∉ S). |
| `AddSubmonoid.zero_mem_aperySet` | simp | 0 ∈ Ap(S, n). |
| `AddSubmonoid.mem_aperySet_iff_isLeast` | characterisation | w ∈ Ap(S, n) ↔ w is the least element of S in its residue class modulo n. |
| `AddSubmonoid.ncard_aperySet` | other | #Ap(S, n) = n for n ∈ S, n > 0. |
| `AddSubmonoid.aperySet_mod_bijOn` | structure | w ↦ w mod n is a bijection from Ap(S, n) onto {0, …, n − 1}. |

**Uses.**

- Assi–García-Sánchez, Proposition 12 (Selmer's formulas): F(S) and g(S) are computed from Ap(S, n)
- Assi–García-Sánchez, Proposition 47: symmetry is the pairing a_i + a_{n−1−i} = a_{n−1} on the sorted Apéry set
- Assi–García-Sánchez, Corollary 7: S is generated by n and Ap(S, n) ∖ {0}, so every numerical semigroup is finitely generated

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `aperySet_three_five` (computation): Ap(⟨3, 5⟩, 3) = {0, 5, 10}.
- `aperySet_five_nine_twentyone` (computation): Ap(⟨5, 9, 21⟩, 5) = {0, 9, 18, 21, 27}.
- `aperySet_top_one` (degenerate): Ap(ℕ, 1) = {0}.
- `ncard_aperySet_of_not_mem` (non-example): For S = ⟨5, 9, 21⟩ and 6 ∉ S, the set {s ∈ S | s − 6 ∉ S} has 9 elements, not 6: the hypothesis n ∈ S is needed for #Ap = n.

**Prerequisites.** `numerical-semigroup`

**Sources.**

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §1, p. 3, the definition before Lemma 4.

  > We define the Apéry set of S with respect to n, denoted Ap(S, n), to be the set Ap(S, n) = {s ∈ S | s − n ∉ S}.

  The definition.

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §1, Lemma 4, p. 3.

  > Lemma 4. Let S be a numerical semigroup and let n ∈ S∗. For all i ∈ {1, . . . , n}, let w(i) be the smallest element of S such that w(i) ≡ i mod n. Then Ap(S, n) = {0, w(1), . . . , w(n − 1)}.

  The description by least representatives, from which #Ap(S, n) = n.

---

#### `apery-set-unique-decomposition` — Unique decomposition through the Apéry set

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let S be a numerical semigroup and n ∈ S ∖ {0}. Every s ∈ S can be written uniquely as s = kn + w with k ∈ ℕ and w ∈ Ap(S, n). Consequently S is generated by n and Ap(S, n) ∖ {0}, and is finitely generated.

**Hypotheses.**

- S numerical, n ∈ S, n > 0.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Existence: subtract n while the result stays in S; the process stops at an element of Ap(S, n).
2. Uniqueness: two decompositions give two elements of Ap(S, n) congruent modulo n, which are equal (apery-set).

**Acceptance.**

- In ⟨3, 5⟩ with n = 3: 13 = 1·3 + 10 and 10 ∈ Ap.

**Prerequisites.** `apery-set`

**Sources.**

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §1, Proposition 6 and Corollary 7, p. 4.

  > Proposition 6. Let S be a numerical semigroup and let n ∈ S∗. For all s ∈ S, there exists a unique (k, w) ∈ N × Ap(S, n) such that s = kn + w.

  The lemma; Corollary 7 is the finite generation.

---

#### `selmer-frobenius-formula` — Selmer's formula for the Frobenius number

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let S be a numerical semigroup and n ∈ S ∖ {0}. Then F(S) = max Ap(S, n) − n.

**Hypotheses.**

- S numerical, n ∈ S, n > 0 (for S = ℕ and n = 1: max Ap = 0 and F = −1).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. max Ap(S, n) − n ∉ S by definition of Ap.
2. If x > max Ap(S, n) − n, write x + n = qn + i and let w(i) ∈ Ap(S, n) be the least element of S in the class i; x + n > w(i), so x + n = kn + w(i) with k ≥ 1 and x = (k − 1)n + w(i) ∈ S.

**Acceptance.**

- ⟨3, 5⟩: max Ap(S, 3) − 3 = 10 − 3 = 7 = F.
- ⟨5, 7, 9⟩: Ap(S, 5) = {0, 16, 7, 18, 9}, 18 − 5 = 13 = F.

**Prerequisites.** `apery-set`

**Sources.**

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §1, Proposition 12(i), p. 5.

  > Proposition 12 (Selmer’s formulas). Let S be a numerical semigroup and let n ∈ S∗. We have the following: (i) F(S) = max(Ap(S, n)) − n,

  Part (i), with the source's proof.

---

#### `selmer-genus-formula` — Selmer's formula for the genus

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let S be a numerical semigroup and n ∈ S ∖ {0}. Then g(S) = (1/n)Σ_{w∈Ap(S,n)} w − (n − 1)/2; equivalently 2n·g(S) = 2Σ_{w∈Ap(S,n)} w − n(n − 1).

**Hypotheses.**

- S numerical, n ∈ S, n > 0.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Write the element of Ap(S, n) in the class i as w(i) = k_i n + i (0 ≤ i < n, k₀ = 0).
2. An integer x ≡ i (mod n), x ≥ 0, lies in S iff x ≥ w(i); so the gaps in the class i are i, i + n, …, i + (k_i − 1)n, k_i of them.
3. g(S) = Σ k_i = (1/n)Σ(k_i n + i) − (1/n)Σ i = (1/n)Σ w − (n − 1)/2.

**Acceptance.**

- ⟨5, 7, 9⟩: (0 + 16 + 7 + 18 + 9)/5 − 2 = 10 − 2 = 8 = g.
- ⟨3, 5⟩: (0 + 5 + 10)/3 − 1 = 4 = g.

**Prerequisites.** `apery-set`

**Sources.**

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §1, Proposition 12(ii), p. 5.

  > (ii) g(S) = (1/n)(Σ_{w∈Ap(S,n)} w) − (n−1)/2 .

  Part (ii), with the source's proof.

---

#### `sylvester-gap-count` — Sylvester's formulas for two generators

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Let a, b ≥ 1 be coprime and S = ⟨a, b⟩. Then Ap(S, a) = {0, b, 2b, …, (a − 1)b}, F(S) = ab − a − b and g(S) = (a − 1)(b − 1)/2 = (F(S) + 1)/2.

**Hypotheses.**

- a, b ≥ 1, gcd(a, b) = 1 (for a = 1 or b = 1, S = ℕ, F = −1 and g = 0, consistently with the formulas).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. The multiples jb, 0 ≤ j < a, are pairwise incongruent modulo a (gcd(a, b) = 1), and jb − a ∉ S because a representation jb − a = ua + vb with u, v ≥ 0 would force vb < jb, so 0 ≤ v < j < a, together with v ≡ j (mod a), which is impossible. So Ap(S, a) = {jb : 0 ≤ j < a}.
2. selmer-frobenius-formula gives F(S) = (a − 1)b − a.
3. selmer-genus-formula gives g(S) = (1/a)·b·a(a − 1)/2 − (a − 1)/2 = (a − 1)(b − 1)/2.

**Acceptance.**

- ⟨3, 5⟩: F = 7, g = 4; ⟨4, 9⟩: F = 23, g = 12.
- F agrees with Mathlib's frobeniusNumber_pair; the gap count g = (a − 1)(b − 1)/2 is new.

**Prerequisites.** `apery-set`, `selmer-frobenius-formula`, `selmer-genus-formula`, `mathlib:frobeniusNumber_pair`

**Sources.**

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §1, Example 13, p. 6.

  > Example 13. Let S = ⟨a, b⟩ be a numerical semigroup. We have Ap(S, a) = {0, b, 2b, . . . , (a − 1)b}. Hence (i) F(S) = (a − 1)b − a = ab − a − b.

  The Apéry set and the Frobenius number; part (ii), the genus, is printed with a misprint recorded as sourceIssue ClassicalArithmeticCompletion/E504.

---

#### `genus-frobenius-inequality` — The genus is at least half the conductor

*lemma.*

**Statement.** For every numerical semigroup S, 2g(S) ≥ F(S) + 1. More precisely n(S) + g(S) = F(S) + 1 and n(S) ≤ g(S), where n(S) = #{s ∈ S : s ≤ F(S)}.

**Hypotheses.**

- S numerical (for S = ℕ both sides are 0).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. {0, …, F(S)} is the disjoint union of the gaps and the elements of S below F(S), so n(S) + g(S) = F(S) + 1.
2. s ↦ F(S) − s maps {s ∈ S : s ≤ F(S)} injectively into the gaps, because s, F(S) − s ∈ S would give F(S) ∈ S.

**Acceptance.**

- ⟨5, 7, 9⟩: 2·8 = 16 ≥ 14.
- ⟨3, 5⟩: 2·4 = 8 = F + 1 (equality: symmetric).

**Prerequisites.** `numerical-semigroup`

**Sources.**

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §1, Lemma 14, p. 6.

  > Lemma 14. Let S be a numerical semigroup. We have g(S) ≥ (F(S)+1)/2 . Proof. Let s ∈ N. If s ∈ S, then F(S) − s ∉ S. Thus g(S) is greater than or equal to n(S). But n(S) + g(S) = F(S) + 1.

  The lemma with its proof.

---

#### `symmetric-numerical-semigroup` — Symmetric numerical semigroups

*definition.*

**Statement.** A numerical semigroup S is symmetric if for every x ∈ ℤ with x ∉ S (negative integers are never in S) one has F(S) − x ∈ S. Equivalently, for 0 ≤ x ≤ F(S) exactly one of x and F(S) − x lies in S.

**Hypotheses.**

- S numerical; ℕ counts as symmetric (F = −1). The source defines symmetric as 'irreducible with F(S) odd' and proves the equivalence with this condition in Proposition 43(i); the condition is taken as the definition here.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Define the predicate on the numerical semigroup S, testing x ∈ ℤ against (S : Set ℕ) through the cast.
2. For x < 0 the condition says F(S) − x ∈ S, which holds as F(S) − x > F(S); for x > F(S) it is vacuous; so only 0 ≤ x ≤ F(S) matters, and there at most one of x, F(S) − x is in S (else F(S) ∈ S).
3. A symmetric S ≠ ℕ has F(S) odd: if F(S) = 2y, then y ∉ S and F(S) − y = y ∉ S.

**Acceptance.**

- ⟨3, 5⟩ is symmetric; ⟨3, 4, 5⟩ (gaps {1, 2}, F = 2) is not; ⟨5, 7, 9⟩ is not (2g = 16 ≠ 14).

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `AddSubmonoid.IsSymmetricNumerical` | data | The predicate ∀ x : ℤ, x ∉ S → F(S) − x ∈ S (for S numerical). |
| `AddSubmonoid.isSymmetricNumerical_iff` | characterisation | S symmetric ↔ ∀ x : ℕ, x ≤ F(S) → (x ∈ S ↔ F(S) − x ∉ S). |
| `AddSubmonoid.IsSymmetricNumerical.odd_frobenius` | relation | If S is symmetric and S ≠ ℕ then F(S) is odd. |
| `AddSubmonoid.isSymmetricNumerical_top` | simp | ℕ is symmetric. |

**Uses.**

- Assi–García-Sánchez, Corollary 44(i): symmetric semigroups are those of least genus for their Frobenius number
- Assi–García-Sánchez, Corollary 45 and Proposition 61: two-generator and free semigroups (in particular semigroups of plane branches, §3) are symmetric
- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/symmetric-iff-apery-pairing: the Apéry-set test for symmetry

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `isSymmetricNumerical_three_five` (computation): ⟨3, 5⟩ is symmetric.
- `not_isSymmetricNumerical_three_four_five` (non-example): ⟨3, 4, 5⟩ is not symmetric: 1 ∉ S and F − 1 = 1 ∉ S.
- `not_isSymmetricNumerical_five_seven_nine` (non-example): ⟨5, 7, 9⟩ is not symmetric.
- `isSymmetricNumerical_top_test` (degenerate): ℕ (F = −1) is symmetric.

**Prerequisites.** `numerical-semigroup`

**Sources.**

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §2, Proposition 43(i), p. 12.

  > Proposition 43. Let S be a numerical semigroup. (i) S is symmetric if and only if for all x ∈ Z \ S, we have F(S) − x ∈ S.

  The characterisation taken as the definition.

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §2, p. 11, the definition after Theorem 40.

  > Let S be a numerical semigroup. We say that S is symmetric if (i) S is irreducible, (ii) F(S) is odd.

  The source's definition, equivalent by Proposition 43(i).

---

#### `symmetric-iff-genus` — Symmetric semigroups are those of least genus

*theorem.*

**Statement.** A numerical semigroup S is symmetric if and only if 2g(S) = F(S) + 1.

**Hypotheses.**

- S numerical.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. By genus-frobenius-inequality, s ↦ F(S) − s is an injection from {s ∈ S : s ≤ F(S)} into the gaps, and n(S) + g(S) = F(S) + 1.
2. S is symmetric iff this injection is onto the gaps (a gap x has F(S) − x ∈ S, and F(S) − x ≤ F(S) as x ≥ 0), iff n(S) = g(S), iff 2g(S) = F(S) + 1.

**Acceptance.**

- ⟨3, 5⟩: 2·4 = 8 = 7 + 1; ⟨5, 7, 9⟩: 16 ≠ 14.
- The source proves this through irreducibility (Theorem 40, Proposition 43); with the classical definition the proof is the counting argument above.

**Prerequisites.** `symmetric-numerical-semigroup`, `genus-frobenius-inequality`

**Sources.**

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §2, Corollary 44(i), pp. 12–13.

  > Corollary 44. Let S be a numerical semigroup. (i) S is symmetric if and only if g(S) = (F(S)+1)/2 .

  The theorem, part (i).

---

#### `two-generator-semigroup-symmetric` — Two-generator numerical semigroups are symmetric

*lemma.*

**Statement.** For coprime a, b ≥ 1, the numerical semigroup ⟨a, b⟩ is symmetric.

**Hypotheses.**

- gcd(a, b) = 1.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. sylvester-gap-count gives 2g = (a − 1)(b − 1) = F + 1; apply symmetric-iff-genus.

**Acceptance.**

- ⟨3, 5⟩, ⟨4, 9⟩, ⟨2, 2k + 1⟩ are symmetric.

**Prerequisites.** `sylvester-gap-count`, `symmetric-iff-genus`

**Sources.**

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §2, Corollary 45, p. 13.

  > Corollary 45. Let S be a numerical semigroup. If e(S) = 2, then S is symmetric.

  The lemma (embedding dimension 2 means two minimal generators, necessarily coprime).

---

#### `apery-set-summand-closed` — Apéry sets are closed under summands

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let S be a numerical semigroup and n ∈ S ∖ {0}. If x, y ∈ S and x + y ∈ Ap(S, n), then x, y ∈ Ap(S, n).

**Hypotheses.**

- S numerical, n ∈ S, n > 0.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. If y − n ∈ S then x + y − n ∈ S, so x + y ∉ Ap(S, n); similarly for x.

**Acceptance.**

- In ⟨3, 5⟩ with n = 3: 10 = 5 + 5 ∈ Ap and 5 ∈ Ap.

**Prerequisites.** `apery-set`

**Sources.**

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §2, Lemma 46, p. 13.

  > Lemma 46. Let S be a numerical semigroup and let n ∈ S∗. If x, y ∈ S and x+y ∈ Ap(S, n), then x, y ∈ Ap(S, n).

  The lemma, verbatim.

---

#### `symmetric-iff-apery-pairing` — The Apéry-set criterion for symmetry

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Let S be a numerical semigroup, n ∈ S ∖ {0}, and Ap(S, n) = {0 = a₀ < a₁ < ⋯ < a_{n−1}}. Then S is symmetric if and only if a_i + a_{n−1−i} = a_{n−1} for all i.

**Hypotheses.**

- S numerical, n ∈ S, n > 0.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. If S is symmetric: F(S) = a_{n−1} − n (selmer-frobenius-formula); a_i − n ∉ S gives a_{n−1} − a_i = F(S) − (a_i − n) ∈ S, and apery-set-summand-closed puts it in Ap(S, n); the map a_i ↦ a_{n−1} − a_i is an order-reversing bijection of Ap(S, n), so it sends a_i to a_{n−1−i}.
2. Conversely, if the pairing holds, every w ∈ Ap(S, n) satisfies a_{n−1} − w ∈ S. For a gap x, choose w ∈ Ap(S, n) with w ≡ x (mod n); then x = w − kn with k ≥ 1 and F(S) − x = (a_{n−1} − w) + (k − 1)n ∈ S. So S is symmetric.

**Acceptance.**

- ⟨3, 5⟩, n = 3: Ap = {0, 5, 10} and 0 + 10 = 5 + 5 = 10.
- ⟨3, 4, 5⟩, n = 3: Ap = {0, 4, 5} and 4 + 4 ≠ 5, so it is not symmetric.

**Prerequisites.** `apery-set-summand-closed`, `selmer-frobenius-formula`, `symmetric-numerical-semigroup`, `apery-set-unique-decomposition`

**Sources.**

- assi-garcia-sanchez-numerical-semigroups-2014 — Abdallah Assi and Pedro A. García-Sánchez, *Numerical semigroups and applications*, §2, Proposition 47, p. 13.

  > Proposition 47. Let S be a numerical semigroup and let n ∈ S∗. Let Ap(S, n) = {0 = a₀ < a₁ < · · · < a_{n−1}}. Then S is symmetric if and only if a_i + a_{n−1−i} = a_{n−1} for all i ∈ {0, . . . , n − 1}.

  The theorem; the source's converse goes through pseudo-Frobenius numbers (Propositions 21–22), replaced here by the direct argument of the second proof step.

---

### Egyptian fractions

An Egyptian expansion of r is a finite set of positive integers whose reciprocals sum to r. The Fibonacci–Sylvester greedy algorithm subtracts the largest unit fraction not exceeding the remainder; for r = m/n with 0 < m < n the next remainder has numerator (−n) mod m < m, so the algorithm stops after at most m steps with strictly increasing denominators (the precise finite claim). Every positive rational has an expansion: take the longest initial segment of the harmonic series not exceeding r and expand the remainder greedily. Sylvester's sequence 2, 3, 7, 43, 1807, … satisfies Σ_{j<k}1/s_j = 1 − 1/(s_k − 1), grows doubly exponentially, and is the greedy underapproximation sequence of 1.

#### `egyptian-fraction-expansion` — Egyptian fraction expansions

*definition.*

**Statement.** An Egyptian fraction expansion of a rational number r is a finite set D of positive integers with Σ_{d∈D} 1/d = r. (The denominators are distinct because D is a set; a representation with repetitions is not an Egyptian expansion.)

**Hypotheses.**

- D a finite set of positive integers; r ∈ ℚ.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Define the predicate on (r, D) with D a Finset ℕ not containing 0.
2. Basic calculus: the empty set expands 0 and only 0; {n} expands 1/n; a disjoint union of expansions of r and s expands r + s; the splitting identity 1/n = 1/(n + 1) + 1/(n(n + 1)) replaces one denominator by two larger ones.

**Acceptance.**

- 5/6 = 1/2 + 1/3; 2/3 = 1/2 + 1/6; 1 = 1/2 + 1/3 + 1/6.
- 2/3 = 1/3 + 1/3 is not an Egyptian expansion.

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `IsEgyptianExpansion` | data | IsEgyptianExpansion r D ↔ 0 ∉ D ∧ Σ_{d∈D} (1 : ℚ)/d = r. |
| `isEgyptianExpansion_empty_iff` | simp | IsEgyptianExpansion r ∅ ↔ r = 0. |
| `isEgyptianExpansion_singleton` | simp | For n ≠ 0, IsEgyptianExpansion (1/n) {n}. |
| `IsEgyptianExpansion.union` | relation | Expansions of r and s with disjoint denominator sets combine to an expansion of r + s. |
| `IsEgyptianExpansion.split` | relation | If n ∈ D, n + 1 ∉ D and n(n + 1) ∉ D, then replacing n by n + 1 and n(n + 1) gives another expansion of the same r. |
| `IsEgyptianExpansion.pos` | other | If D is nonempty then r > 0. |

**Uses.**

- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/greedy-egyptian-algorithm: the greedy algorithm produces an expansion
- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/positive-rational-egyptian-expansion: the existence theorem for every positive rational
- Bloom, Unit fractions with shifted prime denominators, Introduction: expansions with denominators restricted to a set A (primes minus one, squares) are the modern variants of this notion

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `isEgyptianExpansion_five_sixths` (computation): IsEgyptianExpansion (5/6) {2, 3}.
- `isEgyptianExpansion_one` (computation): IsEgyptianExpansion 1 {2, 3, 6}.
- `isEgyptianExpansion_zero_iff` (degenerate): IsEgyptianExpansion 0 D ↔ D = ∅.
- `not_isEgyptianExpansion_two_thirds_three` (non-example): IsEgyptianExpansion (2/3) {3} is false: 1/3 ≠ 2/3 (the multiset {3, 3} is not a set).
- `isEgyptianExpansion_compat_sum` (compatibility): IsEgyptianExpansion r D ↔ 0 ∉ D ∧ Σ_{d∈D} ((d : ℚ))⁻¹ = r, the form of Mathlib's Finset.sum of inverses.

**Prerequisites.** `mathlib:Rat.num_div_den`

**Sources.**

- chun-egyptian-2011 — Ji Hoon Chun, *Egyptian fractions, Sylvester's sequence, and the Erdős–Straus conjecture*, §1.1, p. 1.

  > An Egyptian fraction is a number of distinct unit fractions with positive denominators added together.

  The definition, with distinct denominators.

- chun-egyptian-2011 — Ji Hoon Chun, *Egyptian fractions, Sylvester's sequence, and the Erdős–Straus conjecture*, §1.1, p. 1.

  > The restriction on distinct unit fractions is needed, because otherwise all fractions can easily be made into Egyptian fractions simply by using a/b = 1/b + · · · + 1/b.

  Why D is a set.

#### `greedy-egyptian-step` — The greedy step decreases the numerator

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let r = m/n with 0 < m < n coprime, and c = ⌈n/m⌉ ≥ 2. Then r − 1/c = m′/(nc) with m′ = (−n) mod m, so 0 ≤ m′ < m; and if m′ > 0, then ⌈nc/m′⌉ > c, i.e. the next greedy denominator is strictly larger.

**Hypotheses.**

- 0 < m < n, gcd(m, n) = 1 (the lowest-terms numerator of r − 1/c is at most m′).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. mc − n = m⌈n/m⌉ − n = (−n) mod m ∈ [0, m), and r − 1/c = (mc − n)/(nc).
2. Since c − 1 < n/m, r − 1/c < 1/(c − 1) − 1/c = 1/(c(c − 1)) ≤ 1/c, so the next greedy denominator ⌈1/(r − 1/c)⌉ exceeds c(c − 1) ≥ c.

**Acceptance.**

- 5/31: c = 7, 5/31 − 1/7 = 4/217 (numerator 4 < 5).
- 2/(2k + 1): c = k + 1 and the remainder is 1/((k + 1)(2k + 1)), a unit fraction.

**Prerequisites.** none beyond routine arithmetic.

**Sources.**

- chun-egyptian-2011 — Ji Hoon Chun, *Egyptian fractions, Sylvester's sequence, and the Erdős–Straus conjecture*, §1.4.1, p. 2.

  > 0 < −n (mod m) < m. So, the numerator of the second fraction in the expansion continues to decrease until it reaches 1.

  The numerator decrease; the identity m/n = 1/⌈n/m⌉ + ((−n) mod m)/(n⌈n/m⌉) is displayed just before.

- errthum-sylvester-2015 — Eric Errthum, *A division algorithm approach to p-adic Sylvester expansions*, §3, Theorem 3.1, p. 6.

  > Theorem 3.1 (Modifed Classical Division Algorithm). For all a, b ∈ Z, a > 0, there exist unique q, r ∈ Z such that b = aq − r with 0 ≤ r < a.

  The division step b = mc − m′ with 0 ≤ m′ < m used in the first proof step.

---

#### `greedy-egyptian-algorithm` — The Fibonacci–Sylvester greedy algorithm

*construction.*

**Statement.** For a rational 0 < r ≤ 1, the greedy expansion is the list c₁ < c₂ < ⋯ < c_k defined by c₁ = ⌈1/r⌉ and c_{j+1} = ⌈1/(r − Σ_{i≤j} 1/c_i)⌉ as long as the remainder is positive. It terminates after k ≤ num(r) steps (num(r) the numerator in lowest terms), its denominators are strictly increasing, and Σ_{i≤k} 1/c_i = r; so {c₁, …, c_k} is an Egyptian expansion of r.

**Hypotheses.**

- 0 < r ≤ 1 rational (r = 1 gives the one-term list [1]).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Define the list by well-founded recursion on the numerator of the remainder, which strictly decreases by greedy-egyptian-step.
2. Termination with at most num(r) terms follows from the strict decrease; the sum is r by telescoping; the denominators increase strictly by greedy-egyptian-step.

**Acceptance.**

- 5/31 ↦ [7, 55, 3979, 23744683, 1127619917796295] (five terms, the maximum num(r) = 5).
- 4/13 ↦ [4, 18, 468]; 2/7 ↦ [4, 28]; 1/n ↦ [n].
- The greedy expansion need not be the shortest: 5/31 = 1/7 + 1/62 + 1/434 has three terms.

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `egyptianGreedy` | data | egyptianGreedy r : List ℕ, the greedy denominators of r (empty for r ≤ 0). |
| `egyptianGreedy_sum` | characterisation | For 0 < r ≤ 1: Σ_{c ∈ egyptianGreedy r} 1/c = r. |
| `egyptianGreedy_sorted` | structure | egyptianGreedy r is strictly increasing. |
| `egyptianGreedy_length_le` | other | (egyptianGreedy r).length ≤ r.num. |
| `egyptianGreedy_head` | simp | For 0 < r ≤ 1 the first entry is ⌈1/r⌉. |
| `egyptianGreedy_isEgyptianExpansion` | compatibility | IsEgyptianExpansion r (egyptianGreedy r).toFinset for 0 < r ≤ 1. |

**Uses.**

- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/positive-rational-egyptian-expansion: expands the remainder after the harmonic partial sum
- Chun, §1.4.2 (Freitag–Phillips): the fractions whose greedy expansion has the maximal number m of terms
- Nathanson, §1: the variant with strict inequality is the greedy underapproximation, whose value at θ = 1 is Sylvester's sequence

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `egyptianGreedy_five_thirtyone` (computation): egyptianGreedy (5/31) = [7, 55, 3979, 23744683, 1127619917796295].
- `egyptianGreedy_four_thirteen` (computation): egyptianGreedy (4/13) = [4, 18, 468].
- `egyptianGreedy_unit` (degenerate): egyptianGreedy (1/n) = [n] for n ≥ 1.
- `egyptianGreedy_not_shortest` (non-example): egyptianGreedy (5/31) has length 5, although 5/31 = 1/7 + 1/62 + 1/434 has three terms.
- `egyptianGreedy_two_sevenths` (computation): egyptianGreedy (2/7) = [4, 28].

**Prerequisites.** `greedy-egyptian-step`, `egyptian-fraction-expansion`

**Sources.**

- errthum-sylvester-2015 — Eric Errthum, *A division algorithm approach to p-adic Sylvester expansions*, §3, Algorithm 3.2 and Theorem 3.3, p. 7.

  > Theorem 3.3. Algorithm 3.2 terminates in a finite number of steps and a/b = Σ_{i=0}^{N} 1/q_i .

  Termination and the sum identity, stated in the source with a reference for the computation.

- chun-egyptian-2011 — Ji Hoon Chun, *Egyptian fractions, Sylvester's sequence, and the Erdős–Straus conjecture*, §1.4.1, p. 2.

  > The greedy algorithm is a method that will convert any fraction into an Egyptian fraction.

  The construction, with the worked example 5/31.

- chun-egyptian-2011 — Ji Hoon Chun, *Egyptian fractions, Sylvester's sequence, and the Erdős–Straus conjecture*, §1.4.2, p. 3.

  > Depending on the fraction m/n , the greedy algorithm may give m terms in the resulting Egyptian fraction, or fewer than x terms.

  The bound k ≤ num(r) (the source's 'x' is a misprint for 'm').

#### `positive-rational-egyptian-expansion` — Every positive rational number is a finite sum of distinct unit fractions

*theorem.*

**Statement.** For every rational r > 0 there is a finite set D of positive integers with Σ_{d∈D} 1/d = r.

**Hypotheses.**

- r ∈ ℚ, r > 0.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. The harmonic series diverges (Mathlib's Real.tendsto_sum_range_one_div_nat_succ_atTop), so there is a largest N ≥ 0 with H_N = Σ_{d=1}^{N} 1/d ≤ r.
2. If H_N = r, take D = {1, …, N}. Otherwise 0 < s = r − H_N < 1/(N + 1), so the greedy expansion of s (greedy-egyptian-algorithm) has first denominator ⌈1/s⌉ > N + 1 and increasing denominators, all larger than N.
3. D = {1, …, N} ∪ egyptianGreedy s is a disjoint union, and its reciprocal sum is r.

**Acceptance.**

- r = 2: H₃ = 11/6 ≤ 2 < H₄, s = 1/6, D = {1, 2, 3, 6}.
- r = 5/2: H₆ = 49/20 ≤ 5/2 < H₇, s = 1/20, D = {1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 20}.

**Prerequisites.** `greedy-egyptian-algorithm`, `egyptian-fraction-expansion`, `mathlib:Real.tendsto_sum_range_one_div_nat_succ_atTop`

**Sources.**

- bloom-unit-fractions-2023 — Thomas F. Bloom, *Unit fractions with shifted prime denominators*, Introduction, p. 1.

  > It is elementary to prove that such a decomposition is always possible, for instance by using a greedy algorithm.

  The theorem as stated in the source's introduction ('such a decomposition' of a positive rational into distinct unit fractions); the proof steps give the elementary argument, with the harmonic series handling r ≥ 1.

---

#### `sylvester-sequence` — Sylvester's sequence

*definition.*

**Statement.** Sylvester's sequence is s₀ = 2, s_{k+1} = s_k² − s_k + 1; equivalently s_{k+1} = s₀s₁⋯s_k + 1. Its terms are 2, 3, 7, 43, 1807, 3263443, ….

**Hypotheses.**

- Indexing from 0 (Nathanson indexes from 1).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Define s by recursion; the product form follows by induction from s_{k+1} − 1 = s_k(s_k − 1) = s_k(s₀⋯s_{k−1}).
2. Growth: s_{k+1} − 1 = s_k(s_k − 1) ≥ (s_k − 1)², so s_k − 1 ≥ 2^{2^{k−1}} for k ≥ 1 (doubly exponential growth).

**Acceptance.**

- s₄ = 1807 = 2·3·7·43 + 1.
- The terms are pairwise coprime (s_{k+1} ≡ 1 modulo every earlier term).

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `sylvesterSeq` | data | s : ℕ → ℕ, s 0 = 2, s (k+1) = s k ^ 2 − s k + 1. |
| `sylvesterSeq_succ_eq_prod` | characterisation | s (k+1) = ∏_{j<k+1} s j + 1. |
| `sylvesterSeq_strictMono` | other | s is strictly increasing. |
| `sylvesterSeq_coprime` | relation | s i and s j are coprime for i ≠ j. |
| `sylvesterSeq_sub_one_ge` | other | For k ≥ 1, s k − 1 ≥ 2^(2^(k−1)). |

**Uses.**

- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/sylvester-egyptian-identity: the finite identity Σ_{j<k} 1/s_j = 1 − 1/(s_k − 1)
- Nathanson, Corollary 1: Sylvester's sequence is the greedy underapproximation sequence of θ = 1
- Chun, §1.5: the doubly exponential growth of the sequence

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `sylvesterSeq_values` (computation): s 0 = 2, s 1 = 3, s 2 = 7, s 3 = 43, s 4 = 1807.
- `sylvesterSeq_zero_test` (degenerate): s 0 = 2.
- `sylvesterSeq_prod_compat` (compatibility): s 4 = s 0 · s 1 · s 2 · s 3 + 1.
- `sylvesterSeq_not_linear` (non-example): s 3 = 43 ≠ 2·7 + 1: the recursion is multiplicative in all earlier terms, not in the last one only.

**Prerequisites.** none beyond routine arithmetic.

**Sources.**

- nathanson-egyptian-2022 — Melvyn B. Nathanson, *Underapproximation by Egyptian fractions*, §1, p. 2, equation (4).

  > Sylvester’s sequence [8] is the sequence of positive integers (sᵢ)_{i=1}^∞ constructed recursively by the following rule: (4) s₁ = 2 and s_{i+1} = ∏_{j=1}^{i} s_j + 1

  The definition, shifted to start at index 0.

- chun-egyptian-2011 — Ji Hoon Chun, *Egyptian fractions, Sylvester's sequence, and the Erdős–Straus conjecture*, §1.5.2, p. 5.

  > Corollary. e₀ = 2, eₙ = eₙ₋₁ (eₙ₋₁ − 1) + 1.

  The quadratic recursion, indexed from 0 as here.

---

#### `sylvester-egyptian-identity` — The Egyptian fraction identity of Sylvester's sequence

*theorem.*

**Statement.** For every k ≥ 0, Σ_{j<k} 1/s_j = 1 − 1/(s_k − 1). Consequently Σ_j 1/s_j = 1, and s_k is the greedy underapproximation choice for the remainder: s_k = ⌊1/(1 − Σ_{j<k} 1/s_j)⌋ + 1.

**Hypotheses.**

- s = sylvesterSeq.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. 1/s_k = 1/(s_k − 1) − 1/(s_{k+1} − 1), because s_{k+1} − 1 = s_k(s_k − 1); sum telescopically from 1/(s₀ − 1) = 1.
2. The remainder 1 − Σ_{j<k} 1/s_j = 1/(s_k − 1), and ⌊s_k − 1⌋ + 1 = s_k.

**Acceptance.**

- k = 4: 1/2 + 1/3 + 1/7 + 1/43 = 1805/1806 = 1 − 1/1806.
- This is the case p = q = 1 of Nathanson's Theorem 1.

**Prerequisites.** `sylvester-sequence`

**Sources.**

- nathanson-egyptian-2022 — Melvyn B. Nathanson, *Underapproximation by Egyptian fractions*, §1, Theorem 1, p. 3.

  > Theorem 1. Let θ = p/q ∈ (0, 1], where p and q are positive integers such that p divides q + 1, and let (aᵢ)_{i=1}^∞ be the infinite greedy underapproximation sequence of θ. Then a₁ = (q + 1)/p

  With p = q = 1 the theorem gives a_{k+1} = ∏_{i≤k} a_i + 1 and 1 = Σ_{i≤k} 1/a_i + 1/∏_{i≤k} a_i, which is the identity.

- nathanson-egyptian-2022 — Melvyn B. Nathanson, *Underapproximation by Egyptian fractions*, §1, Corollary 1, p. 4.

  > Corollary 1. Sylvester’s sequence is the infinite greedy underapproximation sequence for θ = 1.

  The greedy clause.

---

### Routing equations of higher genus

Equations defining curves of genus at least one are handed to the rational-point owners (HeightsRationalPointsAndObstructions:RP.6, EffectiveDiophantineMethods:ED.3 and Tau Ceti's elliptic-curve roadmap), and the interface must not lose primitivity or local solubility. For a form F with integer coefficients, the primitive zeros (coprime coordinates) are, up to sign, exactly the rational points of the projective hypersurface F = 0; a primitive zero reduces to a zero modulo every prime power with a coordinate prime to p, gives a p-adic zero with a unit coordinate and a real zero, so congruence conditions are necessary conditions, as in the classical examples x² − 5y² = 6 and x² − 34y² = −1. As a worked comparison with the carrier those owners use, the only rational points of the Weierstrass curve y² = x³ + x are the origin and the point at infinity, which is equivalent to Mathlib's descent for x⁴ + y⁴ = z². CA.4 exports this interface and imports nothing from those owners.

#### `primitive-zero-of-a-form` — Primitive integer zeros of a form

*definition.*

**Statement.** Let F ∈ ℤ[x₀, …, x_n] be homogeneous of degree e ≥ 1. A primitive zero of F is x ∈ ℤ^{n+1} with F(x) = 0 and gcd(x₀, …, x_n) = 1.

**Hypotheses.**

- F homogeneous of degree ≥ 1 (for the scaling API); the definition itself makes sense for any F.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Define the predicate F(x) = 0 ∧ gcd = 1 on ℤ^{n+1}, with the gcd of Mathlib's Finset.gcd over the coordinates.
2. Homogeneity gives F(λx) = λᵉF(x), so every nonzero integer zero is g·x for a primitive zero x and g = gcd ≥ 1, and every nonzero rational zero is a rational multiple of a primitive zero (clear denominators, then divide by the gcd); −x is primitive when x is.

**Acceptance.**

- (3, 4, 5) is a primitive zero of x² + y² − z²; (6, 8, 10) is a zero but not primitive; (0, 0, 0) is not primitive.

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `IsPrimitiveZero` | data | IsPrimitiveZero F x ↔ eval x F = 0 ∧ Finset.univ.gcd x = 1. |
| `IsPrimitiveZero.neg` | relation | IsPrimitiveZero F x → IsPrimitiveZero F (−x) (F homogeneous). |
| `exists_isPrimitiveZero_smul` | constructor | For F homogeneous and x ≠ 0 with F(x) = 0, x = g·y with g = gcd(x) ≥ 1 and IsPrimitiveZero F y. |
| `exists_isPrimitiveZero_of_rat` | constructor | For F homogeneous and a nonzero rational zero ξ ∈ ℚ^{n+1}, there are a primitive zero y and λ ∈ ℚ with ξ = λ·y. |
| `IsPrimitiveZero.ne_zero` | other | A primitive zero is nonzero. |
| `isPrimitiveZero_iff_pythagorean` | compatibility | For F = x₀² + x₁² − x₂²: IsPrimitiveZero F (a, b, c) ↔ PythagoreanTriple a b c ∧ gcd(a, b, c) = 1 (Mathlib's PythagoreanTriple). |

**Uses.**

- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/quartic-descent-t4-plus-v4-equals-2u2: Bennett–Siksek pass from the rational point (t, v) to the primitive solution (T, V, U)
- ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4/primitive-zeros-and-rational-projective-points: the dictionary with points of projective hypersurfaces
- HeightsRationalPointsAndObstructions:RP.6 and EffectiveDiophantineMethods:ED.3: the rational-point and descent owners receive equations of genus at least one as projective curves, whose rational points are the primitive zeros up to sign
- DiophantineApproximationAndTranscendence:DT.0: the naive height of a rational projective point is the maximum of the coordinates of its primitive representative

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `isPrimitiveZero_three_four_five` (computation): (3, 4, 5) is a primitive zero of x₀² + x₁² − x₂².
- `not_isPrimitiveZero_six_eight_ten` (non-example): (6, 8, 10) is a zero of x₀² + x₁² − x₂² but not a primitive zero.
- `not_isPrimitiveZero_zero` (degenerate): (0, 0, 0) is not a primitive zero of any F.
- `isPrimitiveZero_pythagorean_compat` (compatibility): (5, 12, 13) is a primitive zero of x₀² + x₁² − x₂² and PythagoreanTriple 5 12 13 holds.

**Prerequisites.** `mathlib:MvPolynomial.IsHomogeneous`, `mathlib:MvPolynomial.eval`, `mathlib:Finset.gcd`

**Sources.**

- conrad-descent-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Proofs by descent*, Corollary 3.3, proof, p. 6.

  > Write x = a/d, y = b/d, and z = c/d with a, b, c, d ∈ Z and d > 0. Then a, b, and c are nonzero. Clearing the denominator in x⁴ + y⁴ = z², we have a⁴ + b⁴ = (cd)².

  Clearing denominators to pass from rational to integral solutions, the second proof step.

- bennett-siksek-erdos-2020 — Michael A. Bennett and Samir Siksek, *A conjecture of Erdős, supersingular primes and short character sums*, §6, Annals printed p. 372.

  > Write t = T/U and v = V/U where, without loss of generality, T, V and U are positive integers with gcd(U, V, T) = 1.

  The primitive representative of a rational point, which the source then feeds into the quartic descent.

---

#### `primitive-zeros-and-rational-projective-points` — Primitive zeros are the rational points of the projective hypersurface, up to sign

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Let F ∈ ℤ[x₀, …, x_n] be homogeneous of degree ≥ 1 and V(F) ⊆ ℙⁿ_ℚ its zero set. The map x ↦ [x] from primitive zeros of F to ℚ-points of V(F) (Mathlib's Projectivization of ℚ^{n+1}) is surjective, and [x] = [y] iff y = ±x.

**Hypotheses.**

- F homogeneous of degree ≥ 1.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Well defined: F(x) = 0 is invariant under scaling by homogeneity, so the zero set in ℙⁿ(ℚ) is defined through any representative.
2. Surjective: a point [ξ] with ξ ∈ ℚ^{n+1} ∖ 0 has a primitive integral representative (exists_isPrimitiveZero_of_rat of primitive-zero-of-a-form).
3. Fibres: [x] = [y] means y = λx with λ ∈ ℚ^×; primitivity of both forces λ = ±1 (write λ = a/b in lowest terms; a divides every coordinate of y and b every coordinate of x).

**Acceptance.**

- For x² + y² − z²: the rational points of the conic correspond to the primitive Pythagorean triples up to sign.
- The correspondence is the interface through which an equation of genus ≥ 1 is handed to the rational-point owners without losing primitivity.

**Prerequisites.** `primitive-zero-of-a-form`, `mathlib:Projectivization`, `mathlib:Projectivization.mk`

**Sources.**

- conrad-descent-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Proofs by descent*, Corollary 3.3, p. 6.

  > Corollary 3.3. Each rational solution to x⁴ + y⁴ = z² has x or y equal to 0.

  An instance of the transfer: the rational statement is proved from the primitive integral one by clearing denominators.

- bennett-siksek-erdos-2020 — Michael A. Bennett and Samir Siksek, *A conjecture of Erdős, supersingular primes and short character sums*, §6, Annals printed p. 372.

  > Write t = T/U and v = V/U where, without loss of generality, T, V and U are positive integers with gcd(U, V, T) = 1.

  The primitive representative of a rational point, unique up to sign once positivity is imposed.

---

#### `local-solubility-of-a-primitive-zero` — A primitive zero is locally soluble everywhere

*lemma.*

**Statement.** Let F ∈ ℤ[x₀, …, x_n] and let x be a primitive zero of F. Then for every prime p and every k ≥ 1, the reduction of x modulo pᵏ is a zero of F modulo pᵏ with some coordinate not divisible by p; x is a zero of F in ℤ_p^{n+1} with a unit coordinate for every p; and x is a nonzero real zero. Contrapositively, if for some p and k every zero of F modulo pᵏ has all coordinates divisible by p, then F has no primitive zero.

**Hypotheses.**

- F with integer coefficients; x primitive.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Reduction is a ring homomorphism, so F(x) = 0 gives F(x mod pᵏ) = 0; gcd(x) = 1 gives a coordinate prime to p.
2. The inclusion ℤ → ℤ_p and ℤ → ℝ are ring homomorphisms; a coordinate prime to p is a unit in ℤ_p.

**Acceptance.**

- x² − 5y² = 6z² (the homogenised x² − 5y² = 6): modulo 3, x² ≡ 2y² forces x ≡ y ≡ 0, and then 9 ∣ 6z² forces z ≡ 0 (mod 3); so there is no primitive zero, and x² − 5y² = 6 has no rational solution (Conrad, Example 6.3 and Remark 6.4).
- x² − 34y² = −z² has primitive zeros (5, 1, 3) over ℤ, although x² − 34y² = −1 has no integer solution: local and rational solubility do not give integral solutions of the dehomogenised equation.

**Prerequisites.** `primitive-zero-of-a-form`, `mathlib:ZMod`, `mathlib:PadicInt`, `mathlib:Int.castRingHom`

**Sources.**

- conrad-pell-one-2025 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, I*, §6, Example 6.3, p. 8.

  > Reduce mod 3 instead: the equation becomes x² − 5y² ≡ 0 mod 3, or x² ≡ 5y² ≡ 2y² mod 3. This too has a solution, namely (0, 0), so it doesn’t seem like progress has been made.

  The local test with a zero that is not primitive modulo 3, the contrapositive clause.

- conrad-pell-one-2025 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, I*, §6, Remark 6.4, p. 8.

  > In contrast, x² − 34y² = −1 has no solution in Z but has solutions in Q, such as (5/3, 1/3), (3/5, 1/5), (27/11, 5/11), and (3/29, 5/29).

  The limit of the test: rational (primitive projective) solubility does not imply integral solubility of the affine equation.

---

#### `rational-points-of-y2-x3-plus-x` — Routing example: the rational points of y² = x³ + x

*comparison.*

**Statement.** Let E be the Weierstrass curve y² = x³ + x over ℚ (Mathlib's WeierstrassCurve with a₁ = a₂ = a₃ = a₆ = 0, a₄ = 1). Its only affine rational point is (0, 0); so E(ℚ) = {O, (0, 0)}. Conversely, this statement implies that x⁴ + y⁴ = z² has no solution in positive integers.

**Hypotheses.**

- Rational points in the sense of Mathlib's WeierstrassCurve.Affine.Equation over ℚ.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Let (x, y) be a rational point with x ≠ 0; then y ≠ 0 and x > 0. Write x = a/b, y = c/d in lowest terms; b³c² = d²(a³ + ab²) forces b = t², d = t³ (the primitive representative of the point of weighted projective space).
2. Then c² = a(a² + t⁴) with a, a² + t⁴ coprime and positive, so a = u² and a² + t⁴ = v², i.e. u⁴ + t⁴ = v² with t ≠ 0.
3. Mathlib's x⁴ + y⁴ = z² theorem (Fermat42.exists_pos_odd_minimal and Fermat42.not_minimal) forces u = 0, so x = 0, a contradiction.
4. Conversely a positive solution of x⁴ + y⁴ = z² gives the rational point ((x/y)², xz/y³) with x-coordinate ≠ 0.

**Acceptance.**

- The analogous statement for y² = x³ − x (points O, (0, 0), (±1, 0)) is the routing of fermat-right-triangle-theorem (Conrad, Corollary 3.19).
- This compares a CA.4 descent statement with Mathlib's Weierstrass-curve points, the carrier used by the elliptic-curve and rational-point owners (Tau Ceti's Mordell–Weil theorem WeierstrassCurve.Affine.fg_point_of_numberField is stated for these points).

**Prerequisites.** `primitive-zero-of-a-form`, `mathlib:WeierstrassCurve`, `mathlib:WeierstrassCurve.Affine.Equation`, `mathlib:Fermat42.exists_pos_odd_minimal`, `mathlib:Fermat42.not_minimal`, `mathlib:Int.sq_of_gcd_eq_one`

**Sources.**

- conrad-descent-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Proofs by descent*, Corollary 3.8 and its proof, pp. 6–7.

  > Corollary 3.8. The only rational solution to y² = x³ + x is (0, 0).

  The comparison, with the source's proof in the proof steps.

- conrad-descent-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Proofs by descent*, Remark 3.9, p. 7.

  > Remark 3.9. Conversely, Corollary 3.8 implies Theorem 3.1. If x⁴ + y⁴ = z² in positive integers then multiplying through by x²/y⁶ gives us (x/y)⁶ + (x/y)² = (xz/y³)²

  The converse clause.

### Dependencies of CA.4

- Inside the roadmap: `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3` (the Smith normal form node `CA.3/smith-normal-form-of-an-integer-matrix` is an admissible diagonalisation for `integer-linear-systems`); `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.1` is the stage's recorded input for residue symbols, and CA.4 uses only the quadratic-reciprocity and Jacobi-symbol results that CA.1 imports from Mathlib. The link FiniteFieldsAndCharacterSums:FF.1 → CA.4 recorded by the restructuring forwards CA.1's finite-field characters; no CA.4 statement needs more than Mathlib's Legendre and Jacobi symbols and χ₄.
- On other roadmaps: `QSeriesPartitionsAndMockModularForms:QM.0` supplies the Jacobi triple product identity and its specialisations (request below).
- Consumers: `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5` and `LogicAndDefinabilityInNumberTheory:LD.4` (Pell-type equations and linear systems); the ArithmeticDynamics Part II on Markoff actions (the Markoff surface, moves, tree, reduction and the definition of strong approximation); `HeightsRationalPointsAndObstructions:RP.6` and `EffectiveDiophantineMethods:ED.3` (the routing interface).

### Requests to other roadmaps

- To `QSeriesPartitionsAndMockModularForms:QM.0`: the Jacobi triple product identity ∏_{n≥1}(1 + ax^{2n−1})(1 + a⁻¹x^{2n−1})(1 − x^{2n}) = Σ_{n∈ℤ}aⁿx^{n²} as an identity of formal power series with Laurent-polynomial coefficients in a, and its specialisations ∏(1 − xⁿ)³ = ½Σ(−1)ⁿ(2n + 1)x^{(n²+n)/2}, ∏(1 + x^{2n−1})²(1 − x^{2n}) = Σx^{n²}, 2∏(1 + x^{2n})²(1 − x^{2n}) = Σx^{r²+r} and ∏(1 − xⁿ)/(1 + xⁿ) = Σ(−1)ⁿx^{n²}. Needed by `hirschhorn-sixth-power-identity`, `hirschhorn-logarithmic-derivative-identity` and `theta-fourth-power-lambert-series`.

### Gaps

- **Catalan–Mihăilescu has no owner.** The audit lists it among CA.4's exponential equations, but its only proof uses the arithmetic of cyclotomic fields (the Stickelberger ideal, Thaine's theorem), not elementary methods, and no layer plans it (DT.4 plans only Tijdeman's effective bound). CA.4 plans the elementary instance of powers of 2 and 3. The maintainer routes Mihăilescu's theorem to a cyclotomic owner.

### Mistakes found in the sources

- E501 (Martin, §1): 'the graph obtained from integer solutions is a tree' holds for the positive solutions; the graph on all nonzero integer solutions is a disjoint union of four trees, one per sign pattern.
- E502 (Ankeny, §2): the cases m ≡ 1, 2, 5, 6 (mod 8) are only outlined; the missing steps (the value of (−m/q), the congruence modulo 2 for m even using t odd, the prime analysis) are supplied in the proof steps and check out.
- E503 (Ankeny, §2): 'R = tqX + tby + mz' should read tqx.
- E504 (Assi–García-Sánchez, Example 13(ii)): the sum in the genus of ⟨a, b⟩ is b + 2b + ⋯ + (a − 1)b, not a + 2a + ⋯ + (b − 1)a.
- E505 (Bourgain–Gamburd–Sarnak, §1): 'R₂, R₃ are defined similarly' should read R₁, R₂.
- E506 (Chun, §1.4.2): 'fewer than x terms' should read 'fewer than m terms'.

### Acceptance tests for the layer

- Linear systems: 6x + 10y = 4 has the solutions (−1 + 5t, 1 − 3t) and 6x + 10y = 3 has none, although it has rational solutions.
- The quartic descent: the positive coprime solutions of T⁴ + V⁴ = 2U² are (1, 1, 1) only, and (2, 2, 4) shows that coprimality is needed; x⁴ − y⁴ = z² has no positive solution.
- Markoff: (2, 5, 29) = R₂R₁R₀(1, 1, 1); the positive graph is a tree with every vertex of degree 3; reduction modulo 2 and 3 is onto X*(𝔽₂) (4 elements) and X*(𝔽₃) (8 elements); the origin is never a reduction.
- Three squares: the numbers below 64 that are not sums of three squares are 7, 15, 23, 28, 31, 39, 47, 55, 60, 63.
- Representation numbers: r₂(25) = 12, r₂(3) = 0, r₄(4) = 24, r₄(12) = 96.
- Pell: the solutions of x² − 2y² = −1 are ±(1 + √2)^k with k odd; x² − 194y² = −1 and x² − 37y² = 11 have no solution, decided by Chebyshev's box; x² − 13y² = n with 0 < |n| < √13 is solvable exactly for n = ±1, ±3.
- Exponential equations: x² + 7 = 2ⁿ exactly for n = 3, 4, 5, 7, 15; |2ᵃ − 3ᵇ| = 1 exactly for (a, b) = (1, 0), (1, 1), (2, 1), (3, 2).
- Numerical semigroups: ⟨3, 5⟩ has gaps {1, 2, 4, 7}, genus 4, Frobenius number 7 and is symmetric; ⟨5, 7, 9⟩ has F = 13 and g = 8 and is not symmetric.
- Egyptian fractions: the greedy expansion of 5/31 has five terms 7, 55, 3979, 23744683, 1127619917796295; 5/2 = Σ_{d≤6}1/d + 1/20; 1/2 + 1/3 + 1/7 + 1/43 = 1 − 1/1806.
- Routing: (3, 4, 5) is a primitive zero of x² + y² − z² and (6, 8, 10) is not; y² = x³ + x has only the rational point (0, 0) besides the point at infinity.
- No statement of the layer is a finite search without a proven bound.

## CA.5 Number-field arithmetic handoff

**Coverage: source_decomposed.** Every comparison this layer keeps is planned from public sources; nothing remains.

### Scope

The intrinsic invariants of a number field K — its ring of integers and integral bases, its units with the regulator, its class group and class number, the factorisation of primes — are in Mathlib and Tau Ceti and in the Tau Ceti roadmaps on number-field arithmetic and global number fields. The certified algorithms that compute them, with their completeness and stopping certificates, belong to `ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.2`. This layer states the **comparisons** between the two sides: how a presentation of K (a generator θ, the ring ℤ√d, an integral basis, a list of ideals given by integer matrices, a relation matrix, a family of units) determines the intrinsic invariant, and exactly which certificate is needed for the determination to be complete. It does not construct the invariants and it does not contain algorithms.

What is not here: the relative Dedekind–Kummer theorem, the power-basis index and the index formula, Dedekind's criterion, monogenicity and the rank-one certification of a named fundamental unit by minimality (the number-field arithmetic roadmap, Layers 3 and 7, whose index formula is already in Tau Ceti and is used here); orders, conductors and Picard groups (the global number fields roadmap, Layer 11); certified computation (`ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.2`); the classification of the solutions of Pell's equations and its continued-fraction decision procedure (`ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4`).

### Conventions

- An integral basis ω = (ω_1, …, ω_n) of 𝓞_K is fixed (for instance Mathlib `NumberField.RingOfIntegers.basis`), and elements are read through their ω-coordinates as column vectors.
- The **Hermite matrix** H_ω(I) of a nonzero ideal I is its Hermite basis in the convention of `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3` (columns, upper triangular, entries to the right of each pivot reduced). With ω_1 = 1 the upper left entry generates I ∩ ℤ.
- The **class-group presentation** of a family of nonzero ideals I_1, …, I_N is φ_I(e) = ∏ [I_i]^{e_i}; its kernel, the **relation lattice** Λ_I ⊆ ℤ^N, is recorded by a matrix whose columns are relations.
- The **Minkowski bound** is Mathlib's M_K = (4/π)^{r_2} · n!/nⁿ · √|d_K|. The **regulator** of a family u of units is Mathlib's `NumberField.Units.regOfFamily`, and R_K is `NumberField.Units.regulator`; the index formula R(u)/R_K = [𝓞_K^× : ⟨u⟩μ_K] is Mathlib's `regOfFamily_div_regulator`.
- The **certificate inequality** is |det A| · R(u) < 2h* for a certified lower bound h* ≤ h_K R_K; h* is an input.
- **ℤ√d** is Mathlib's `Zsqrtd d` with norm x² − dy²; Pell solutions are Mathlib's `Pell.Solution₁ d`.

### Which invariant each comparison certifies

| invariant | what must be certified | comparison | example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| integral basis | disc(b) = d_K, or disc(b) squarefree | `discriminant-of-a-sublattice-basis` | ℤ[√5] has index 2 in 𝓞_{ℚ(√5)} |
| ideal | Hermite form and 𝓞_K-stability of the column lattice | `hermite-matrix-of-an-ideal-criterion` | (1 + i) has Hermite matrix with columns (2, 0), (1, 1) |
| norm of an ideal | the Hermite matrix | `ideal-norm-eq-prod-hermite-diagonal` | N((1 + i)) = 2 · 1 |
| class group | generators including all primes of norm ≤ M_K, and relations spanning Λ_I | `class-group-of-a-relation-matrix` | ℚ(√−14): ℤ/4; ℚ(√−30): ℤ/2 × ℤ/2 |
| a multiple of h_K | found relations of full rank only | `class-number-divides-relation-determinant` | relations of determinant 8 for ℚ(√−14), where h = 4 |
