| fundamental units | maximal rank and a regulator bound R(u) < 2L ≤ 2R_K | `fundamental-units-from-regulator-bound` | (1 + √5)/2 in ℚ(√5) |
| class group and units together | h* ≤ h_K R_K and \|det A\| · R(u) < 2h* | `class-number-regulator-certificate` | ℚ(√−14) with h* ∈ (2, 4] |
| factorisation of p | ℤ[θ] = 𝓞_K | `kummer-dedekind-for-a-monogenic-presentation` | ℤ[i] at 2, 3 and 5 |
| units of ℤ[√d] | 𝓞_K = ℤ[√d] (d ≢ 1 mod 4) | `pell-solutions-are-norm-one-units`, `negative-pell-iff-unit-of-norm-minus-one` | d = 2: 3 + 2√2 = (1 + √2)² |

### What the pinned libraries already contain

Mathlib: the class group and class number, `NumberField.exists_ideal_in_class_of_norm_le` (a representative of norm at most M_K in every class), the PID criterion at the Minkowski bound, Dirichlet's unit theorem with the regulator and the index formula `NumberField.Units.regOfFamily_div_regulator`, the absolute norm with `Ideal.natAbs_det_basis_change`, discriminants with `Algebra.discr_of_matrix_vecMul`, Kummer–Dedekind over ℤ (`NumberField.Ideal.primesOverSpanEquivMonicFactorsMod` with its residue-degree and ramification-index lemmas and `RingOfIntegers.exponent_eq_one_iff`), cyclotomic discriminants, `Zsqrtd` and the Pell equation. Tau Ceti: the class of a height-one prime and the closure lemma `ClassGroup.mk0_mem_closure_of_count_eq`, the discriminant of an integral basis (`NumberField.discr_eq_of_integralBasis`), the rings of integers and discriminants of quadratic fields, the power-basis index and the index formula (`TauCeti.NumberField.IntegralPrimitiveElement.discr_minpoly_eq_index_sq_mul_discr`), monogenicity, and the class numbers of ℚ(√−5) and ℚ(√−21).

### Dependencies

`ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3` (the Smith certificate and the cokernel of a relation matrix; the Hermite normal form, its uniqueness and the Hermite basis of a sublattice; the index as the product of pivots). `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4` (Pell equations). `ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.2` supplies certified outputs in the formats of the table, through the request recorded in the packet. Consumers: `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.6` and `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.7`.

### Acceptance tests of the layer

- The class groups of ℚ(√−14) and ℚ(√−30), both of order 4, are read off from complete relation matrices through their Smith forms, as ℤ/4 and ℤ/2 × ℤ/2.
- A relation matrix without a completeness certificate yields only that h_K divides its determinant; a unit family without a regulator bound yields only its index R(u)/R_K.
- The Hermite matrix of an ideal determines it, its norm and its intersection with ℤ; diag(2, 1) in ℤ[i] is in Hermite form but is not the matrix of an ideal.
- The index of ℤ[√d] in 𝓞_{ℚ(√d)} is 2 exactly when d ≡ 1 (mod 4), as the discriminants 4d and d_K force.
- For d = 2 the fundamental Pell solution is the square of the unit 1 + √2 of norm −1, and for d = 3 there is no unit of norm −1.

**Planets of this layer** (6): *Class group generated by small primes*, *Class group from a relation matrix*, *Class number and regulator certificate*, *Hermite matrix of an ideal*, *Discriminant and index of a sublattice*, *Pell solutions as norm-one units*.

### Nodes (18)

#### `class-group-generated-by-small-primes` — The class group is generated by the primes of norm at most the Minkowski bound

*theorem.* **Planet:** *Class group generated by small primes*.

**Statement.** Let K be a number field of degree n with r_2 complex places and discriminant d_K, and M_K = (4/π)^{r_2} · n!/nⁿ · √|d_K| (Mathlib's Minkowski bound, a local notation M K in Mathlib.NumberTheory.NumberField.ClassNumber). The classes of the nonzero prime ideals P of 𝓞_K with N(P) ≤ M_K generate the class group Cl(K).

**Hypotheses.**

- K any number field; the bound is Minkowski's, in the form Mathlib states it.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Let C be a class. Mathlib NumberField.exists_ideal_in_class_of_norm_le gives a nonzero integral ideal I in C with N(I) ≤ M_K.
2. Every prime P dividing I has N(P) ≤ N(I) ≤ M_K: I ⊆ P gives N(P) | N(I) (Mathlib Ideal.absNorm_dvd_absNorm_of_le).
3. So the multiplicities of I vanish at every prime outside T = {P : N(P) ≤ M_K}; Tau Ceti ClassGroup.mk0_mem_closure_of_count_eq (with x = 1) puts the class of I in the subgroup generated by the classes IsDedekindDomain.HeightOneSpectrum.classGroupMk of the primes in T.

**Acceptance.**

- K = ℚ(i): M_K = 4/π < 2, so no prime is needed and Cl(K) = 1 (Conrad Example 2.1).
- K = ℚ(√−14): M_K ≈ 4.764, so the primes over 2 and 3 generate.
- Compatibility: Mathlib RingOfIntegers.isPrincipalIdealRing_of_isPrincipal_of_norm_le_of_isPrime (all primes of norm ≤ M_K principal ⇒ PID) is the case in which every generator is trivial.

**Prerequisites.**

- `mathlib:NumberField.exists_ideal_in_class_of_norm_le`
- `mathlib:Ideal.absNorm_dvd_absNorm_of_le`
- `mathlib:Ideal.absNorm`
- `mathlib:ClassGroup.mk0`
- `tauceti:ClassGroup.mk0_mem_closure_of_count_eq`
- `tauceti:IsDedekindDomain.HeightOneSpectrum.classGroupMk`
- `mathlib:RingOfIntegers.isPrincipalIdealRing_of_isPrincipal_of_norm_le_of_isPrime`

**Sources.**

- STEIN.ANT — William Stein, *Algebraic Number Theory, a Computational Approach*, Lemma 7.3.1 and its proof, Section 7.3, printed p. 84.

  > Lemma 7.3.1. Let K be a number field with ring of integers OK. Then the class group Cl(K) is generated by the prime ideals p of OK lying over primes p ∈Z with p ≤BK =

  The source proves generation by the primes over rational primes p ≤ B_K; its proof (factor a representative of norm ≤ B_K) shows the sharper statement of the node, generation by primes of norm ≤ B_K.

- conrad-classgroups-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Class group calculations by Minkowski bound*, Section 1, p. 1.

  > In particular, the ideal class group is generated by the prime ideals with norm not exceeding this bound.

  The node verbatim.

#### `class-group-presentation` — The class-group presentation by a family of ideals and its relation lattice

*construction.*

**Statement.** Let I_1, …, I_N be nonzero ideals of 𝓞_K. The presentation map is φ_I : ℤ^N → Cl(K), e ↦ ∏ [I_i]^{e_i} (a group homomorphism from the additive group ℤ^N, written multiplicatively), and the relation lattice is Λ_I = ker φ_I ⊆ ℤ^N: for e ≥ 0, e ∈ Λ_I exactly when the ideal ∏ I_i^{e_i} is principal. If the classes [I_i] generate Cl(K), φ_I induces Cl(K) ≅ ℤ^N / Λ_I and Λ_I has index h_K.

**Hypotheses.**

- The I_i are nonzero ideals (nonzero divisors of the ideal monoid); N ≥ 0.
- Relations are recorded as column vectors (the lattice convention of CA.3).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Define φ_I(e) = ∏_i ClassGroup.mk0(I_i)^{e_i}; it is a homomorphism because Cl(K) is commutative.
2. Define Λ_I as the ℤ-submodule {e | φ_I(e) = 1}.
3. For e ≥ 0, φ_I(e) is the class of ∏ I_i^{e_i} (ClassGroup.mk0 is multiplicative), which is trivial iff that ideal is principal.
4. φ_I is surjective iff the [I_i] generate: its image is the subgroup they generate.
5. If surjective, the first isomorphism theorem (Mathlib QuotientGroup.quotientKerEquivOfSurjective) gives Cl(K) ≅ ℤ^N/Λ_I, and the index of Λ_I is |Cl(K)| = h_K (NumberField.classNumber).

**Acceptance.**

- With no generators Λ = ℤ⁰ = everything.
- In a field with h_K = 1 every vector is a relation.
- For K = ℚ(√−14) and I = (p_2, p_3), Λ contains (2, 0) and (1, 2).

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `classGroupPresentation` | constructor | φ_I : Multiplicative (ℤ^N) →* Cl(K), e ↦ ∏ [I_i]^{e_i}. |
| `relationLattice` | data | Λ_I = ker φ_I as a ℤ-submodule of ℤ^N. |
| `ClassGroupPresentation.single` | simp | φ_I of the i-th basis vector is [I_i]. |
| `ClassGroupPresentation.mem_relationLattice_iff` | characterisation | e ∈ Λ_I iff φ_I(e) = 1. |
| `ClassGroupPresentation.mem_relationLattice_iff_isPrincipal` | characterisation | For e ≥ 0: e ∈ Λ_I iff ∏ I_i^{e_i} is principal. |
| `ClassGroupPresentation.surjective_iff` | characterisation | φ_I is surjective iff the classes [I_i] generate Cl(K). |
| `ClassGroupPresentation.classGroupEquiv` | equivalence | If φ_I is surjective, Cl(K) ≅ ℤ^N/Λ_I (as an additive group). |
| `ClassGroupPresentation.index_relationLattice` | relation | If φ_I is surjective, [ℤ^N : Λ_I] = h_K. |

**Uses.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-group-of-a-relation-matrix`: a matrix whose columns span Λ_I determines Cl(K)
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-number-divides-relation-determinant`: found relations span a sublattice of Λ_I
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-number-regulator-certificate`: the certificate decides whether found relations span Λ_I
- Biasse–Fieker, Subexponential class group and unit group computation, Section 2: relations collected as exponent vectors of principal products
- `ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.2`: certified class-group outputs are relation matrices for a generating family of primes

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `relationLattice_empty` (degenerate): For N = 0, relationLattice I = ⊤.
- `relationLattice_classNumber_one` (compatibility): If classNumber K = 1 then relationLattice I = ⊤ for every family I.
- `relationLattice_principal_generator` (non-example): If I_i is principal then the i-th basis vector is a relation, although I_i need not be the unit ideal (a definition testing equality of ideals instead of classes fails this).
- `relationLattice_order` (characterisation): k times the i-th basis vector lies in Λ_I iff the order of [I_i] divides k.

**Prerequisites.**

- `mathlib:ClassGroup`
- `mathlib:ClassGroup.mk0`
- `mathlib:NumberField.classNumber`
- `mathlib:QuotientGroup.quotientKerEquivOfSurjective`
- `mathlib:Submodule.IsPrincipal`

**Sources.**

- biasse-fieker-classgroup-2014 — Jean-François Biasse and Claus Fieker, *Subexponential class group and unit group computation in large degree number fields*, Section 2, printed p. 386.

  > and the class group Cl(O) is isomorphic to ZN/ker(π◦ϕ). Therefore, computing the class group boils down to computing ker(π ◦ϕ), which is given by the lattice of (e1, . . . , eN) ∈ZN such that

  The node is the map π∘φ of the source and its kernel, for the maximal order; the source takes the p_i to be primes generating the class group.

#### `class-group-of-a-relation-matrix` — The class group from a complete relation matrix

*theorem.* **Planet:** *Class group from a relation matrix*.

**Statement.** Let I_1, …, I_N be nonzero ideals whose classes generate Cl(K), and let A be an N × R integer matrix whose columns span the relation lattice Λ_I. For any Smith normal form certificate of A with invariant factors d_0 | ⋯ | d_{r−1}: r = N, Cl(K) ≅ ⊕_{i<N} ℤ/d_i ℤ, and h_K = ∏ d_i. The invariant factors > 1 are the invariant factors of Cl(K).

**Hypotheses.**

- Generation: the [I_i] generate Cl(K) (for instance all primes of norm ≤ M_K, CA.5/class-group-generated-by-small-primes).
- Completeness: the columns of A span Λ_I, not merely lie in it.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Cl(K) ≅ ℤ^N/Λ_I = ℤ^N/Aℤ^R (CA.5/class-group-presentation).
2. ℤ^N/Aℤ^R ≅ ⊕_{i<r} ℤ/d_i ⊕ ℤ^{N−r} (CA.3/cokernel-of-an-integer-matrix).
3. Cl(K) is finite, so N − r = 0; the order is ∏ d_i.

**Acceptance.**

- K = ℚ(√−14), I = (p_2, p_3): Λ is spanned by (2, 0) and (1, 2); the Smith form of the matrix with these columns is diag(1, 4), so Cl ≅ ℤ/4.
- K = ℚ(√−30), I = (p_2, p_3, p_5): Λ is spanned by 2e_1, 2e_2, 2e_3 and (1, 1, 1); invariant factors 1, 2, 2, so Cl ≅ (ℤ/2)², with the same class number 4 as ℚ(√−14).
- Omitting the relation (1, 2) for ℚ(√−14) leaves a matrix of rank 1 < N: an incomplete relation set is detected by the rank before any class number is read off.

**Prerequisites.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-group-presentation`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3/cokernel-of-an-integer-matrix`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3/smith-normal-form-certificate`
- `mathlib:NumberField.classNumber`

**Sources.**

- biasse-fieker-classgroup-2014 — Jean-François Biasse and Claus Fieker, *Subexponential class group and unit group computation in large degree number fields*, Section 2, printed p. 386.

  > If we have found enough of them, they give us the group structure of Cl(O) via the Smith normal form (SNF) of M.

  The node is this statement with "enough" made precise (the columns span Λ_I) and with the transposed convention (relations as columns).

- belabas-computational-2004 — Karim Belabas, *Topics in computational algebraic number theory*, Section 1, Definition 1.1, printed p. 20.

  > Definition 1.1. a finite abelian group G is known algorithmically when its Smith Normal Form (SNF) G = ⊕_{i=1}^r (Z/diZ) gi, with d1 | · · · | dr in Z, and gi ∈ G, is given, and we can solve the discrete logarithm problem in G.

  The target of the interpretation: the class group is known through the Smith form of its relation matrix. Transcribed from the page image.

#### `class-number-divides-relation-determinant` — Found relations bound the class number from above

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Let I_1, …, I_N be nonzero ideals whose classes generate Cl(K), and A an N × N integer matrix with det A ≠ 0 whose columns are relations (lie in Λ_I). Then h_K · [Λ_I : Aℤ^N] = |det A|. In particular h_K divides |det A|, and h_K = |det A| exactly when the columns of A span Λ_I.

**Hypotheses.**

- Generation of Cl(K) by the [I_i].
- det A ≠ 0 (the found relations have full rank).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. [ℤ^N : Aℤ^N] = |det A| (Mathlib AddSubgroup.index_eq_natAbs_det).
2. [ℤ^N : Λ_I] = h_K (ClassGroupPresentation.index_relationLattice).
3. Multiplicativity of the index along Aℤ^N ⊆ Λ_I ⊆ ℤ^N (Mathlib Subgroup.relIndex_mul_index, additively).

**Acceptance.**

- K = ℚ(i) with I = ((1 + i)): the relation (2) gives |det| = 2, while (1 + i) is principal, so Λ = ℤ, h = 1 and [Λ : 2ℤ] = 2.
- K = ℚ(√−14): the relations (2, 0) and (0, 4) (p_3⁴ ∼ p_2^{−2} ∼ 1) give |det| = 8 = 4 · 2.
- Found relations alone never prove completeness: a lower bound for h_K (or for h_K R_K) is needed (CA.5/class-number-regulator-certificate).

**Prerequisites.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-group-presentation`
- `mathlib:AddSubgroup.index_eq_natAbs_det`
- `mathlib:Subgroup.relIndex_mul_index`
- `mathlib:NumberField.classNumber`

**Sources.**

- biasse-fieker-classgroup-2014 — Jean-François Biasse and Claus Fieker, *Subexponential class group and unit group computation in large degree number fields*, Section 4.3, printed p. 399.

  > Indeed, it allows us to derive a bound h∗in polynomial time under GRH that satisfies h∗⩽hR < 2h∗(see [3]). If the tentative class number and regulator do not satisfy this inequality, we declare a failure (or in practice, collect more relations).

  The node is the fact behind the "tentative class number": the determinant of found relations is a multiple of h, equal to it only for a complete relation set.

#### `fundamental-units-from-regulator-bound` — A regulator lower bound certifies a fundamental system of units

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Let K be a number field of unit rank r, u_1, …, u_r units whose logarithmic embeddings are linearly independent (Mathlib IsMaxRank), and L a real number with L ≤ R_K. If R(u) < 2L, where R(u) is the regulator of the family, then u_1, …, u_r together with the roots of unity generate 𝓞_K^×.

**Hypotheses.**

- L is a certified lower bound for the regulator, supplied independently (analytically, or by an enumeration argument).
- IsMaxRank u: the family has finite index.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Mathlib NumberField.Units.regOfFamily_div_regulator: R(u)/R_K = [𝓞_K^× : ⟨u⟩·μ_K] =: j, a positive integer (finite index by Mathlib isMaxRank_iff_closure_finiteIndex; R_K > 0 by regulator_pos).
2. j = R(u)/R_K ≤ R(u)/L < 2, so j = 1.

**Acceptance.**

- K = ℚ(√5), u = (1 + √5)/2: R(u) = log((1 + √5)/2) ≈ 0.4812 = R_K; any certified L with R_K/2 < L ≤ R_K certifies u.
- u = ((1 + √5)/2)² has R(u) = 2R_K ≥ 2L for every L ≤ R_K: a non-fundamental family is never certified.
- Rank 0 (imaginary quadratic): the empty family has R = 1 = R_K, and any L ∈ (1/2, 1] certifies that μ_K is the whole unit group.

**Prerequisites.**

- `mathlib:NumberField.Units.regOfFamily_div_regulator`
- `mathlib:NumberField.Units.IsMaxRank`
- `mathlib:NumberField.Units.regOfFamily`
- `mathlib:NumberField.Units.regulator`
- `mathlib:NumberField.Units.regulator_pos`
- `mathlib:NumberField.Units.torsion`
- `mathlib:NumberField.Units.isMaxRank_iff_closure_finiteIndex`

**Sources.**

- biasse-fieker-classgroup-2014 — Jean-François Biasse and Claus Fieker, *Subexponential class group and unit group computation in large degree number fields*, Section 4.3, printed p. 400.

  > then certifying the result boils down to verifying that h∗⩽h′R′ < 2h∗.

  The unit half of the certificate: with the class group known, R′ < 2R_K bounds the index of the found units by 1. The node states it with an independent regulator bound L.

#### `class-number-regulator-certificate` — The class-number–regulator certificate

*theorem.* **Planet:** *Class number and regulator certificate*.

**Statement.** Let I_1, …, I_N be nonzero ideals whose classes generate Cl(K), A an N × N integer matrix with det A ≠ 0 whose columns lie in Λ_I, u a family of r units with IsMaxRank, and h* a real number with h* ≤ h_K R_K. If |det A| · R(u) < 2h*, then the columns of A span Λ_I (so Cl(K) ≅ ℤ^N/Aℤ^N, read off from the Smith form of A) and u together with μ_K generates 𝓞_K^×; in particular h_K = |det A| and R_K = R(u).

**Hypotheses.**

- h* is a certified lower bound for h_K R_K; in practice it comes from the analytic class number formula (the residue of ζ_K at 1), evaluated with error control, and is an input here.
- The other hypotheses are those of CA.5/class-number-divides-relation-determinant and CA.5/fundamental-units-from-regulator-bound.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. |det A| = h_K · j_1 with j_1 = [Λ_I : Aℤ^N] (CA.5/class-number-divides-relation-determinant).
2. R(u) = R_K · j_2 with j_2 = [𝓞_K^× : ⟨u⟩μ_K] (Mathlib regOfFamily_div_regulator).
3. |det A| R(u) = h_K R_K j_1 j_2 ≥ h* j_1 j_2; with |det A| R(u) < 2h* this gives j_1 j_2 < 2, so j_1 = j_2 = 1.
4. Then Cl(K) ≅ ℤ^N/Aℤ^N by CA.5/class-group-of-a-relation-matrix.

**Acceptance.**

- K = ℚ(√−14): h = 4, R = 1; the relation matrix with columns (2, 0), (1, 2) and the empty unit family pass for any h* ∈ (2, 4].
- With the incomplete matrix of columns (2, 0), (0, 4) (det 8) no h* ≤ 4 passes: the certificate refuses it.
- The certificate checks the class group and the units together; neither index is certified from found relations or found units alone.

**Prerequisites.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-number-divides-relation-determinant`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/fundamental-units-from-regulator-bound`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-group-of-a-relation-matrix`
- `mathlib:NumberField.Units.regOfFamily_div_regulator`
- `mathlib:NumberField.classNumber`

**Sources.**

- biasse-fieker-classgroup-2014 — Jean-François Biasse and Claus Fieker, *Subexponential class group and unit group computation in large degree number fields*, Section 4.3, printed pp. 399–400.

  > Indeed, it allows us to derive a bound h∗in polynomial time under GRH that satisfies h∗⩽hR < 2h∗(see [3]). If the tentative class number and regulator do not satisfy this inequality, we declare a failure (or in practice, collect more relations).

  The node is the implication the source uses to certify its output (h* ≤ h′R′ < 2h*), stated with h* ≤ hR as a hypothesis; the analytic derivation of h* (under GRH in the source) is not part of this node.

#### `ideal-hermite-matrix` — The Hermite matrix of an ideal in a fixed integral basis

*construction.* **Planet:** *Hermite matrix of an ideal*.

**Statement.** Fix a ℤ-basis ω = (ω_1, …, ω_n) of 𝓞_K. For a nonzero ideal I, the Hermite matrix H_ω(I) is the unique n × n integer matrix in Hermite normal form (CA.3 convention: columns, upper triangular, entries to the right of each pivot reduced) whose columns are the ω-coordinates of a ℤ-basis of I. I ↦ H_ω(I) is injective; H_ω(𝓞_K) = 1; H_ω((m)) = m·1 for m ∈ ℤ_{>0}; x ∈ I iff the coordinate vector of x lies in the column lattice of H_ω(I); and when ω_1 = 1, I ∩ ℤ = H_ω(I)_{1,1} ℤ.

**Hypotheses.**

- ω is a ℤ-basis of 𝓞_K (for instance Mathlib NumberField.RingOfIntegers.basis); I ≠ 0, so its coordinate lattice has full rank n.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. The coordinate map ω.equivFun identifies 𝓞_K with ℤ^n and I with a full-rank sublattice.
2. CA.3/hermite-basis-of-a-sublattice gives a unique Hermite basis of that sublattice; it is square because the rank is n. Define H_ω(I) as that matrix.
3. Injectivity and the characterisation "H = H_ω(I) iff H is in Hermite normal form and its columns span I" follow from uniqueness (CA.3/hermite-normal-form-unique).
4. Membership: x ∈ I iff ω.equivFun x lies in the column lattice.
5. Corner: with ω_1 = 1, the elements of I in ℤ = ℤω_1 are the lattice vectors with coordinates 2, …, n equal to 0; the upper triangular shape forces them to be multiples of the first column H_{1,1} e_1.

**Acceptance.**

- H_ω(𝓞_K) = 1.
- In ℤ[i] with ω = (1, i), H_ω((1 + i)) has columns (2, 0) and (1, 1).
- H_ω((m)) = m·1.

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `idealHermiteMatrix` | constructor | H_ω(I). |
| `latticeOfMatrix` | data | The ℤ-submodule of 𝓞_K spanned by the columns of an integer matrix read in ω. |
| `IdealHermiteMatrix.isHermiteNormalForm` | structure | H_ω(I) is in Hermite normal form. |
| `IdealHermiteMatrix.latticeOfMatrix_eq` | characterisation | The columns of H_ω(I) span I. |
| `IdealHermiteMatrix.injective` | extensionality | I ↦ H_ω(I) is injective. |
| `IdealHermiteMatrix.eq_iff` | characterisation | H_ω(I) = H iff H is in Hermite normal form and its columns span I: the interpretation of a certified matrix. |
| `IdealHermiteMatrix.comap_eq_span_corner` | compatibility | If ω_1 = 1 then I ∩ ℤ = (H_ω(I)_{1,1}). |
| `IdealHermiteMatrix.mem_iff` | characterisation | x ∈ I iff the ω-coordinates of x lie in the column lattice of H_ω(I). |

**Uses.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/ideal-norm-eq-prod-hermite-diagonal`: the norm of I is the product of the pivots
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/hermite-matrix-of-an-ideal-criterion`: which Hermite matrices come from ideals
- Belabas, Topics in computational algebraic number theory, Section 5.3: ideals are stored, multiplied and inverted in HNF form
- `ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.2`: certified ideal outputs (integral bases, ideal decompositions) are Hermite matrices in a fixed integral basis
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-group-presentation`: the generating ideals of a class-group computation are given by their Hermite matrices

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `idealHermiteMatrix_top` (degenerate): H_ω(𝓞_K) = 1.
- `idealHermiteMatrix_intCast` (computation): For m > 0, H_ω((m)) = m·1.
- `idealHermiteMatrix_one_add_i` (computation): In a field with ω = (1, θ), θ² = −1, H_ω((1 + θ)) has columns (2, 0) and (1, 1).

**Prerequisites.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3/hermite-basis-of-a-sublattice`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3/is-hermite-normal-form`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3/hermite-normal-form-unique`
- `mathlib:NumberField.RingOfIntegers.basis`
- `mathlib:Module.Basis`
- `mathlib:Ideal`

**Sources.**

- belabas-computational-2004 — Karim Belabas, *Topics in computational algebraic number theory*, Section 5.3.1, printed p. 36.

  > This matrix is often produced by building a Z-basis from larger generating sets, for instance when adding or multiplying ideals. An efficient way to do this is the HNF algorithm modulo a. It has the added benefit that the HNF representation is canonical, for a fixed (wi), with entries bounded by a.

  The node is the canonical HNF representation of the source, with the canonicity proved by CA.3. Transcribed from the page image.

- belabas-computational-2004 — Karim Belabas, *Topics in computational algebraic number theory*, Section 5.3.2, printed p. 36.

  > Note that a ∩ Z is easily read off from A since w1 = 1, namely |a| is the upper left entry of the HNF matrix A.

  The corner property (API comap_eq_span_corner). Transcribed from the page image.

#### `ideal-norm-eq-prod-hermite-diagonal` — The norm of an ideal is the product of its Hermite pivots

*theorem.*

**Statement.** For a nonzero ideal I of 𝓞_K and any ℤ-basis ω of 𝓞_K, N(I) = ∏_i H_ω(I)_{i,i}.

**Hypotheses.**

- I ≠ 0.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. The columns of H = H_ω(I) are the ω-coordinates of a ℤ-basis of I, so the change-of-basis determinant from ω to that basis is det H.
2. Mathlib Ideal.natAbs_det_basis_change: |det| = N(I).
3. det H = ∏ H_{i,i} > 0 (CA.3: a square Hermite matrix is upper triangular with positive diagonal).

**Acceptance.**

- N((1 + i)) = 2 · 1 = 2 in ℤ[i].
- N((m)) = mⁿ.
- N(𝓞_K) = 1.

**Prerequisites.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/ideal-hermite-matrix`
- `mathlib:Ideal.natAbs_det_basis_change`
- `mathlib:Ideal.absNorm`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3/index-eq-prod-hermite-pivots`

**Sources.**

- STEIN.ANT — William Stein, *Algebraic Number Theory, a Computational Approach*, Definition 6.3.2, Section 6.3, printed p. 75.

  > Definition 6.3.2 (Norm of Fractional Ideal). Suppose I is a fractional ideal of OK. The norm of I is the lattice index Norm(I) = [OK : I] ∈Q≥0, or 0 if I = 0. Note that if I is an integral ideal, then Norm(I) = #(OK/I).

  The norm as a lattice index, which the Hermite pivots compute.

- conrad-modulespid-2025 — Keith Conrad, *Modules over a PID*, Theorem 5.19, p. 18.

  > Theorem 5.19. Let M be a finite free Z-module with rank n and M′ be a submodule of M with rank n. Let x1, . . . , xn be a basis of M and y1, . . . , yn be a basis of M′. Writing yj = Pn i=1 cijxi with cij ∈Z, the index [M : M′] equals | det(cij)|.

  The index formula applied to the Hermite basis of I.

#### `hermite-matrix-of-an-ideal-criterion` — Which Hermite matrices are the matrices of ideals

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Let ω be a ℤ-basis of 𝓞_K and H an n × n integer matrix in Hermite normal form. Then H = H_ω(I) for some nonzero ideal I if and only if the column lattice L_ω(H) = Σ_j ℤ (Σ_i H_{i,j} ω_i) is stable under multiplication by every ω_k. Equivalently, for the multiplication matrices M_k of the ω_k (from the multiplication table of ω), each M_k H = H X_k for an integer matrix X_k.

**Hypotheses.**

- H square in Hermite normal form (so det H > 0 and L_ω(H) has full rank).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. If L_ω(H) is stable under each ω_k, it is stable under their ℤ-span 𝓞_K, so it is an ideal I; it is nonzero because it has full rank; H = H_ω(I) by IdealHermiteMatrix.eq_iff.
2. Conversely an ideal is stable under multiplication by 𝓞_K.
3. Matrix form: ω_k · (ω-coordinates x) = M_k x, and M_k H has columns in L_ω(H) iff M_k H = H X_k with X_k integral (H is invertible over ℚ, X_k = H⁻¹ M_k H).

**Acceptance.**

- In ℤ[i] with ω = (1, i), diag(2, 1) is in Hermite normal form but its lattice 2ℤ + ℤi is not an ideal (i · i = −1 ∉ it).
- The matrix with columns (2, 0), (1, 1) passes: it is H_ω((1 + i)).
- The identity always passes (I = 𝓞_K).

**Prerequisites.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/ideal-hermite-matrix`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3/hermite-normal-form-unique`

**Sources.**

- belabas-computational-2004 — Karim Belabas, *Topics in computational algebraic number theory*, Section 5.3.1, printed p. 36.

  > An integral ideal a is given by a matrix whose columns, viewed as elements of OK, generate a as a Z-module.

  The criterion is the check that a matrix handed over as an ideal is one: its column module must be an 𝓞_K-module. Transcribed from the page image.

#### `discriminant-of-a-sublattice-basis` — The discriminant of a basis of a sublattice of 𝓞_K

*theorem.* **Planet:** *Discriminant and index of a sublattice*.

**Statement.** Let b_1, …, b_n ∈ 𝓞_K be linearly independent over ℤ, n = [K : ℚ], and L = Σ ℤ b_i. Then disc(b) = [𝓞_K : L]² · d_K. Consequently b is an integral basis iff disc(b) = d_K; if disc(b) is squarefree then b is an integral basis; and for an order O ⊆ 𝓞_K, disc(O) = [𝓞_K : O]² d_K.

**Hypotheses.**

- n = [K : ℚ] and the b_i are ℤ-linearly independent (so L has finite index).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Let ω be an integral basis and C the integer matrix expressing b in ω (Mathlib Module.Basis.toMatrix), so b = ω·C.
2. Mathlib Algebra.discr_of_matrix_vecMul: disc(b) = det(C)² · disc(ω).
3. Tau Ceti NumberField.discr_eq_of_integralBasis: disc(ω) = d_K.
4. |det C| = [𝓞_K : L] (Mathlib Submodule.natAbs_det_basis_change).
5. If disc(b) is squarefree then [𝓞_K : L]² divides it, so the index is 1.

**Acceptance.**

- ℤ[√5] ⊆ 𝓞_{ℚ(√5)}: disc(1, √5) = 20 = 2² · 5, index 2.
- ℤ[ζ_n] = 𝓞_{ℚ(ζ_n)}: disc(power basis) = d_K (Mathlib IsCyclotomicExtension.Rat.discr is the computation of this discriminant).
- Dedekind's cubic x³ + x² − 2x + 8: every order ℤ[α] has even index, so 4 | disc(α) for every α (Stein Example 6.2.7).

**Prerequisites.**

- `mathlib:Algebra.discr_of_matrix_vecMul`
- `mathlib:Algebra.discr`
- `tauceti:NumberField.discr_eq_of_integralBasis`
- `mathlib:NumberField.RingOfIntegers.basis`
- `mathlib:Submodule.natAbs_det_basis_change`
- `mathlib:Module.Basis.toMatrix`
- `mathlib:NumberField.discr`
- `mathlib:IsCyclotomicExtension.Rat.discr`

**Sources.**

- STEIN.ANT — William Stein, *Algebraic Number Theory, a Computational Approach*, Proposition 6.2.6, Section 6.2, printed p. 73.

  > Proposition 6.2.6. Suppose O is an order in OK. Then Disc(O) = Disc(OK) · [OK : O]2.

  The node is the proposition for any full sublattice, with the source's proof (change-of-basis determinant).

#### `index-of-the-order-z-sqrt-d` — The index of ℤ[√d] in the ring of integers of ℚ(√d)

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Let d ≠ 0, 1 be squarefree, K = ℚ(θ) with θ² = d (minpoly ℤ θ = X² − d). Then [𝓞_K : ℤ[θ]] = 2 if d ≡ 1 (mod 4) and 1 otherwise.

**Hypotheses.**

- d squarefree, d ≠ 0, 1; θ generates K.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. θ is an integral primitive element; Tau Ceti TauCeti.NumberField.IntegralPrimitiveElement.discr_minpoly_eq_index_sq_mul_discr gives disc(X² − d) = 4d = index(θ)² · d_K.
2. Tau Ceti NumberField.discr_eq_of_squarefree_of_mod_four_eq_one and NumberField.discr_eq_four_mul_of_mod_four_ne_one give d_K = d or 4d.
3. Hence index(θ)² = 4 or 1.

**Acceptance.**

- d = 5: ℤ[√5] has index 2 in ℤ[(1 + √5)/2].
- d = −1: ℤ[i] = 𝓞_{ℚ(i)}.
- d = −3: ℤ[√−3] has index 2 in ℤ[(1 + √−3)/2], the ring containing the primitive cube roots of unity.

**Prerequisites.**

- `tauceti:TauCeti.NumberField.IntegralPrimitiveElement.discr_minpoly_eq_index_sq_mul_discr`
- `tauceti:TauCeti.NumberField.IntegralPrimitiveElement.index`
- `tauceti:NumberField.discr_eq_of_squarefree_of_mod_four_eq_one`
- `tauceti:NumberField.discr_eq_four_mul_of_mod_four_ne_one`
- `mathlib:Polynomial.discr`

**Sources.**

- conrad-quadraticfields-2021 — Keith Conrad, *Factoring in quadratic fields*, Section 3, Theorem 3.4, p. 2.

  > Theorem 3.4. The integers of K are {a + b √ d : a, b ∈Z} if d̸ ≡1 mod 4 and ( a + b 1 + √ d 2 ! : a, b ∈Z ) if d ≡1 mod 4.

  The two rings of integers whose comparison with ℤ[√d] the node records as an index.

- STEIN.ANT — William Stein, *Algebraic Number Theory, a Computational Approach*, Proposition 6.2.6, Section 6.2, printed p. 73.

  > Proposition 6.2.6. Suppose O is an order in OK. Then Disc(O) = Disc(OK) · [OK : O]2.

  Applied to O = ℤ[√d].

#### `kummer-dedekind-for-a-monogenic-presentation` — Dedekind–Kummer at every prime for a monogenic presentation

*construction.*

**Statement.** Let θ ∈ 𝓞_K with ℤ[θ] = 𝓞_K and f = minpoly ℤ θ. For every rational prime p, the primes of 𝓞_K over p correspond bijectively to the monic irreducible factors Q of f mod p; the prime attached to Q is (p, Q(θ)), its residue degree is deg Q and its ramification index is the multiplicity of Q in f mod p. No prime is excluded.

**Hypotheses.**

- ℤ[θ] = 𝓞_K (a monogenic presentation: quadratic fields with θ = √d or (1 + √d)/2, cyclotomic fields with θ = ζ).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. ℤ[θ] = 𝓞_K is equivalent to exponent θ = 1 (Mathlib RingOfIntegers.exponent_eq_one_iff), so no prime divides the exponent.
2. Apply Mathlib NumberField.Ideal.primesOverSpanEquivMonicFactorsMod with that hypothesis, and its three companion lemmas for the span, the residue degree and the ramification index.

**Acceptance.**

- ℤ[i], p = 3: X² + 1 is irreducible mod 3, one prime, inert.
- ℤ[i], p = 5: X² + 1 ≡ (X − 2)(X + 2), two primes of degree 1.
- ℤ[i], p = 2: X² + 1 ≡ (X + 1)², one prime with e = 2; the monogenic presentation covers the prime dividing the discriminant, which the general theorem with p ∤ exponent also covers but the hypothesis p ∤ disc f does not.

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `primesOverEquivMonicFactorsModOfAdjoinEqTop` | constructor | The bijection primesOver(p) ≃ monic irreducible factors of f mod p, for every prime p. |
| `inertiaDeg_primesOverEquivMonicFactorsModOfAdjoinEqTop_symm` | characterisation | The residue degree of the prime attached to Q is deg Q. |
| `ramificationIdx_primesOverEquivMonicFactorsModOfAdjoinEqTop_symm` | characterisation | Its ramification index is the multiplicity of Q in f mod p. |
| `primesOverEquivMonicFactorsModOfAdjoinEqTop_symm_apply_eq_span` | characterisation | The prime attached to (the reduction of) Q is (p, Q(θ)). |

**Uses.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-group-of-q-sqrt-minus-14`: the factorisations of 2 and 3 in ℤ[√−14]
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-group-of-q-sqrt-minus-30`: the factorisations of 2, 3 and 5 in ℤ[√−30]
- Stein, Algebraic Number Theory, Section 4.2: factoring primes from a generator's minimal polynomial
- Conrad, Class group calculations, Section 2: the factorisation tables of the worked examples

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `kummerDedekind_gaussian_three` (computation): In a field with ℤ[θ] = 𝓞_K and θ² = −1, there is exactly one prime over 3.
- `kummerDedekind_gaussian_five` (computation): In the same field there are exactly two primes over 5.
- `kummerDedekind_gaussian_two` (degenerate): In the same field there is one prime over 2 and its ramification index is 2 (the prime dividing the discriminant is covered).

**Prerequisites.**

- `mathlib:NumberField.Ideal.primesOverSpanEquivMonicFactorsMod`
- `mathlib:RingOfIntegers.exponent_eq_one_iff`
- `mathlib:RingOfIntegers.monicFactorsMod`
- `mathlib:NumberField.Ideal.inertiaDeg_primesOverSpanEquivMonicFactorsMod_symm_apply`
- `mathlib:NumberField.Ideal.ramificationIdx_primesOverSpanEquivMonicFactorsMod_symm_apply`
- `mathlib:NumberField.Ideal.primesOverSpanEquivMonicFactorsMod_symm_apply_eq_span`

**Sources.**

- STEIN.ANT — William Stein, *Algebraic Number Theory, a Computational Approach*, Theorem 4.2.3, Section 4.2, printed pp. 53–54.

  > Theorem 4.2.3. Let f ∈Z[x] be the minimal polynomial of a over Z. Suppose that p ∤[OK : Z[a]] is a prime.

  For a monogenic presentation the index is 1, so the hypothesis holds at every prime; the node records this specialisation with the residue degrees and ramification indices.

#### `zsqrtd-into-the-ring-of-integers` — The ring ℤ√d inside the ring of integers of ℚ(√d)

*construction.*

**Statement.** For d ∈ ℤ and θ ∈ 𝓞_K with θ² = d, the ring homomorphism φ : ℤ√d → 𝓞_K, x + y√d ↦ x + yθ (Mathlib Zsqrtd.lift). Its image is ℤ[θ]; it is injective when d is not a square; for squarefree d with K = ℚ(θ) it is an isomorphism iff d ≢ 1 (mod 4); and it carries the norm form x² − dy² of ℤ√d to the field norm N_{K/ℚ}.

**Hypotheses.**

- θ² = d; for the bijectivity and norm statements, d squarefree and θ generating K.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. φ is Zsqrtd.lift applied to (θ, θ² = d).
2. Image: generated by 1 and θ, i.e. ℤ[θ] (θ² = d).
3. Injective: x + yθ = 0 with y ≠ 0 would make θ rational, so d a square.
4. Bijective iff ℤ[θ] = 𝓞_K iff d ≢ 1 (mod 4) (Tau Ceti NumberField.adjoin_gen_eq_top_of_mod_four_ne_one; for d ≡ 1 the index is 2 by CA.5/index-of-the-order-z-sqrt-d).
5. Norm: N(x + yθ) = (x + yθ)(x − yθ) = x² − dy² (the conjugate of θ is −θ).

**Acceptance.**

- d = −1: an isomorphism ℤ[i] ≅ 𝓞_{ℚ(i)}.
- d = 5: not surjective, (1 + √5)/2 is missed.
- N(3 + 2√d) = 9 − 4d.

**API.** What a user of this object needs in order to use it without unfolding the definition.

| name | role | statement |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `zsqrtdToRingOfIntegers` | constructor | φ : ℤ√d →+* 𝓞_K, x + y√d ↦ x + yθ. |
| `ZsqrtdToRingOfIntegers.sqrtd` | simp | φ(√d) = θ. |
| `ZsqrtdToRingOfIntegers.range_eq` | characterisation | The image of φ is ℤ[θ]. |
| `ZsqrtdToRingOfIntegers.injective` | structure | φ is injective when d is not a square. |
| `ZsqrtdToRingOfIntegers.bijective_iff` | equivalence | For squarefree d and θ generating K: φ is bijective iff d ≢ 1 (mod 4). |
| `ZsqrtdToRingOfIntegers.norm` | compatibility | N_{K/ℚ}(φ(x)) = Zsqrtd.norm x. |

**Uses.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/pell-solutions-are-norm-one-units`: Pell solutions live in ℤ√d and are carried to units of 𝓞_K
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/negative-pell-iff-unit-of-norm-minus-one`: norm −1 solutions become units of norm −1
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.4`: Pell equations are stated in Mathlib's ℤ√d (Pell.Solution₁)
- Stein, Algebraic Number Theory, Section 8.2.1: Pell solutions as units of the ring of integers

**Unit tests.** A plausible wrong definition fails one of these.

- `zsqrtdToRingOfIntegers_gaussian` (computation): For d = −1 and θ² = −1 generating K, φ is bijective.
- `zsqrtdToRingOfIntegers_five_not_surjective` (non-example): For d = 5, φ is not surjective.
- `zsqrtdToRingOfIntegers_norm_example` (compatibility): N_{K/ℚ}(φ(3 + 2√d)) = 9 − 4d.

**Prerequisites.**

- `mathlib:Zsqrtd`
- `mathlib:Zsqrtd.lift`
- `mathlib:Zsqrtd.norm`
- `tauceti:NumberField.adjoin_gen_eq_top_of_mod_four_ne_one`
- `tauceti:NumberField.adjoin_halfGen_eq_top_of_mod_four_eq_one`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/index-of-the-order-z-sqrt-d`

**Sources.**

- conrad-quadraticfields-2021 — Keith Conrad, *Factoring in quadratic fields*, Section 3, Theorem 3.4, p. 2.

  > Theorem 3.4. The integers of K are {a + b √ d : a, b ∈Z} if d̸ ≡1 mod 4 and ( a + b 1 + √ d 2 ! : a, b ∈Z ) if d ≡1 mod 4.

  The node identifies Mathlib's ℤ√d with the first ring and embeds it in the second.

- conrad-quadraticintegers-2025 — Keith Conrad, *Quadratic integers*, Section 2, Theorem 2.3, p. 2.

  > Theorem 2.3. The norm is multiplicative: for α and β in Z[ √ d], N(αβ) = N(α) N(β).

  The norm form of ℤ√d, which φ carries to the field norm.

#### `pell-solutions-are-norm-one-units` — Pell solutions are the units of norm one

*theorem.* **Planet:** *Pell solutions as norm-one units*.

**Statement.** Let d > 1 be squarefree with d ≢ 1 (mod 4) and K = ℚ(√d), so that 𝓞_K = ℤ[√d]. The map (x, y) ↦ x + y√d is an isomorphism from Mathlib's group Pell.Solution₁ d of integer solutions of x² − dy² = 1 onto the kernel of the norm 𝓞_K^× → {±1}. This subgroup has index 1 or 2 in 𝓞_K^×, and the fundamental Pell solution generates it modulo ±1.

**Hypotheses.**

- d > 1 squarefree, d ≢ 1 (mod 4); K = ℚ(θ) with θ² = d.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Pell.Solution₁ d is the unitary group of ℤ√d, the elements of norm 1 (Mathlib Pell.is_pell_solution_iff_mem_unitary); they are units (Zsqrtd.norm_eq_one_iff).
2. CA.5/zsqrtd-into-the-ring-of-integers: φ is a ring isomorphism ℤ√d ≅ 𝓞_K compatible with norms, so it restricts to an isomorphism of the norm-one units.
3. The norm is a homomorphism 𝓞_K^× → {±1} with kernel the norm-one units, so the index is 1 or 2.
4. Generation modulo ±1 by the fundamental solution: Mathlib Pell.IsFundamental.eq_zpow_or_neg_zpow.

**Acceptance.**

- d = 2: the fundamental Pell solution 3 + 2√2 = (1 + √2)², where 1 + √2 has norm −1; index 2.
- d = 3: 2 + √3 has norm 1 and generates the units modulo ±1; index 1.
- For d ≡ 1 (mod 4) the solutions of x² − dy² = 1 are the norm-one units of the order ℤ[√d], of finite index but possibly not all norm-one units of 𝓞_K (d = 5: (1 + √5)/2 squared is not in ℤ[√5]).

**Prerequisites.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/zsqrtd-into-the-ring-of-integers`
- `mathlib:Pell.Solution₁`
- `mathlib:Pell.is_pell_solution_iff_mem_unitary`
- `mathlib:Zsqrtd.norm_eq_one_iff`
- `mathlib:Pell.IsFundamental.eq_zpow_or_neg_zpow`

**Sources.**

- STEIN.ANT — William Stein, *Algebraic Number Theory, a Computational Approach*, Section 8.2.1, printed p. 93.

  > Thus if (x, y) are integers such that x2 −dy2 = 1, then α = x + √ dy ∈OK has norm 1, so by Proposition 8.1.4 we have α ∈UK. The integer solutions to Pell’s equation thus form a finite-index subgroup of the group of units in the ring of integers of Q( √ d).

  The node makes the subgroup precise (the norm-one units, of index ≤ 2 when d ≢ 1 mod 4). The sentence that follows in the source misstates the cyclic group (ClassicalArithmeticCompletion/E601).

- conrad-pell-one-2025 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, I*, Theorem 5.3, p. 6.

  > Theorem 5.3. Assume x2 −dy2 = 1 has a solution in positive integers and let (x1, y1) be such a solution where y1 is minimal. Then all solutions to x2 −dy2 = 1 in integers are, up to sign, generated from (x1, y1) by taking integral powers of x1 + y1 √ d:

  The generation statement on the Pell side, which Mathlib proves as Pell.IsFundamental.eq_zpow_or_neg_zpow.

#### `negative-pell-iff-unit-of-norm-minus-one` — The negative Pell equation and units of norm −1

*theorem.*

**Statement.** Let d > 1 be squarefree with d ≢ 1 (mod 4) and K = ℚ(√d). The equation x² − dy² = −1 has an integer solution if and only if 𝓞_K has a unit of norm −1; in that case the norm-one units have index 2, and the least positive solution x_1 + y_1√d generates 𝓞_K^× modulo ±1.

**Hypotheses.**

- d > 1 squarefree, d ≢ 1 (mod 4).

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Under the isomorphism ℤ√d ≅ 𝓞_K (CA.5/zsqrtd-into-the-ring-of-integers), x + y√d has norm x² − dy²; an element of norm −1 is a unit (Zsqrtd.norm_eq_one_iff).
2. Conversely a unit of norm −1 of 𝓞_K = ℤ[√d] is x + y√d with x² − dy² = −1.
3. The index statement is CA.5/pell-solutions-are-norm-one-units with the norm surjective onto {±1}.

**Acceptance.**

- d = 2: 1² − 2·1² = −1, the unit 1 + √2 of norm −1.
- d = 3: x² ≡ −1 (mod 3) has no solution, so no unit of norm −1; the fundamental unit 2 + √3 has norm 1.
- d = 10: 3² − 10·1² = −1.

**Prerequisites.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/zsqrtd-into-the-ring-of-integers`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/pell-solutions-are-norm-one-units`
- `mathlib:Zsqrtd.norm_eq_one_iff`

**Sources.**

- conrad-pell-one-2025 — Keith Conrad, *Pell's equation, I*, Theorem 7.5, p. 12.

  > Theorem 7.5. Assume x2 −dy2 = −1 has a solution in positive integers and let (x1, y1) be that solution where y1 is minimal. The integral solutions to both equations x2 −dy2 = ±1 are, up to sign, generated from (x1, y1) by taking integral powers of x1 + y1 √ d:

  The Pell-side statement; the node transports it to the units of 𝓞_K.

#### `class-group-of-q-sqrt-minus-14` — The class group of ℚ(√−14) is cyclic of order 4

*application.*

**Statement.** For K = ℚ(√−14), Cl(K) ≅ ℤ/4ℤ.

**Hypotheses.**

- K = ℚ(θ), θ² = −14.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. M_K = (2/π)√56 ≈ 4.76, so Cl(K) is generated by the primes of norm ≤ 4, which lie over 2 and 3 (CA.5/class-group-generated-by-small-primes).
2. 𝓞_K = ℤ[θ] since −14 ≡ 2 (mod 4); by CA.5/kummer-dedekind-for-a-monogenic-presentation, X² + 14 ≡ X² (mod 2) and ≡ (X − 1)(X + 1) (mod 3), so (2) = p_2² and (3) = p_3 p_3′, and p_3′ ∼ p_3^{−1}.
3. Relations: p_2² = (2); (2 + θ) has norm 18 = 2·3² and is not divisible by 3, so (2 + θ) = p_2 p_3² for the suitably named p_3. The relation lattice of (p_2, p_3) contains the columns (2, 0) and (1, 2).
4. Completeness: p_2 is not principal (a² + 14b² = 2 has no solution), so [p_2] has order 2; [p_3]² = [p_2]^{−1} ≠ 1, so [p_3] has order 4 and generates. Hence Λ is spanned by (2, 0), (1, 2), of index 4 = h.
5. The Smith form of the matrix with columns (2, 0), (1, 2) is diag(1, 4) (CA.3/smith-normal-form-of-an-integer-matrix); CA.5/class-group-of-a-relation-matrix gives Cl(K) ≅ ℤ/4.

**Acceptance.**

- h = 4 = |det| of the relation matrix.
- The class group is cyclic, unlike ℚ(√−30) of the same class number.
- The generator is the class of a prime of norm 3.

**Prerequisites.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-group-generated-by-small-primes`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-group-of-a-relation-matrix`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/kummer-dedekind-for-a-monogenic-presentation`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3/smith-normal-form-of-an-integer-matrix`
- `tauceti:NumberField.adjoin_gen_eq_top_of_mod_four_ne_one`
- `mathlib:Ideal.absNorm_span_singleton`

**Sources.**

- conrad-classgroups-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Class group calculations by Minkowski bound*, Example 2.3, p. 2.

  > Example 2.3. Let K = Q(√−14). We will show the class group is cyclic of order 4. Here n = 2, r2 = 1, and disc(K) = −56. The Minkowski bound is ≈4.764, so the class group is generated by primes dividing (2) and (3).

  The node is Example 2.3, rewritten as a relation-matrix computation.

#### `class-group-of-q-sqrt-minus-30` — The class group of ℚ(√−30) is ℤ/2 × ℤ/2

*application.*

**Statement.** For K = ℚ(√−30), Cl(K) ≅ ℤ/2ℤ × ℤ/2ℤ.

**Hypotheses.**

- K = ℚ(θ), θ² = −30.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. M_K = (2/π)√120 ≈ 6.97, so the primes over 2, 3 and 5 generate.
2. 𝓞_K = ℤ[θ] (−30 ≡ 2 mod 4); X² + 30 ≡ X² modulo 2, 3 and 5, so (2) = p_2², (3) = p_3², (5) = p_5².
3. Relations: 2e_1, 2e_2, 2e_3 and (θ) = p_2 p_3 p_5 (norm 30), i.e. the columns 2e_1, 2e_2, 2e_3, (1, 1, 1).
4. Completeness: a² + 30b² takes none of the values 2, 3, 5, 6, 10, 15, so no nontrivial product p_2^{a} p_3^{b} p_5^{c} with exponents in {0, 1} other than p_2p_3p_5 is principal; the lattice spanned by the four columns has index 4 in ℤ³ and is the whole relation lattice.
5. The Smith form of the 3 × 4 matrix is diag(1, 2, 2) padded with a zero column; Cl(K) ≅ (ℤ/2)².

**Acceptance.**

- h = 4 as for ℚ(√−14), but the invariant factors are (2, 2), not (4).
- Every class has order dividing 2.
- The relation (1, 1, 1) is what cuts (ℤ/2)³ down to (ℤ/2)².

**Prerequisites.**

- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-group-generated-by-small-primes`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-group-of-a-relation-matrix`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/kummer-dedekind-for-a-monogenic-presentation`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3/smith-normal-form-of-an-integer-matrix`
- `tauceti:NumberField.adjoin_gen_eq_top_of_mod_four_ne_one`
- `mathlib:Ideal.absNorm_span_singleton`

**Sources.**

- conrad-classgroups-2026 — Keith Conrad, *Class group calculations by Minkowski bound*, Example 2.4, pp. 2–3.

  > Therefore the class group of K is ⟨[p2], [p3]⟩∼= ⟨[p2]⟩× ⟨[p3]⟩∼= Z/2Z × Z/2Z.

  The conclusion of Example 2.4; the node derives it from the relation matrix.

#### `certified-number-field-output-interpretation` — Interpreting certified number-field outputs as intrinsic invariants

*application.*

**Statement.** For a number field K, the certified outputs of ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.2 are interpreted by the comparison theorems of this layer as follows. (1) A family b of n elements of 𝓞_K with a certificate disc(b) = d_K (or disc(b) squarefree) is an integral basis (CA.5/discriminant-of-a-sublattice-basis). (2) A matrix certified to be in Hermite normal form with an 𝓞_K-stable column lattice is the Hermite matrix of a unique ideal, of norm the product of its pivots (CA.5/hermite-matrix-of-an-ideal-criterion, CA.5/ideal-norm-eq-prod-hermite-diagonal). (3) A family of prime ideals containing all primes of norm ≤ M_K with a relation matrix and a completeness certificate determines Cl(K) ≅ ⊕ ℤ/d_i by the Smith form (CA.5/class-group-of-a-relation-matrix); without the completeness certificate it determines only a multiple of h_K (CA.5/class-number-divides-relation-determinant). (4) A unit family with IsMaxRank and a certified bound h* ≤ h_K R_K with |det A| R(u) < 2h* is a fundamental system (CA.5/class-number-regulator-certificate).

**Hypotheses.**

- The certificates are those CN.2 produces; termination of a search alone is not a certificate of completeness.

**Construction, or proof, in steps.**

1. Each clause is the application of the named comparison theorem to the output, whose hypotheses are exactly the certified properties.
2. The analytic bound h* and its error control are CN.2/CN.4 outputs and enter only as the hypothesis h* ≤ h_K R_K.

**Acceptance.**

- The worked examples ℚ(√−14) and ℚ(√−30) are instances of clause (3) with hand-checked completeness.
- A relation matrix of determinant 8 for ℚ(√−14) (h = 4) is an output without completeness certificate: clause (3) yields only 4 | 8.
- For ℚ(√5) the unit (1 + √5)/2 with any certified L > R_K/2 is an instance of clause (4) with the class group trivial.

**Prerequisites.**

- `ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.2`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/discriminant-of-a-sublattice-basis`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/hermite-matrix-of-an-ideal-criterion`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/ideal-norm-eq-prod-hermite-diagonal`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-group-of-a-relation-matrix`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-number-divides-relation-determinant`
- `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5/class-number-regulator-certificate`

**Sources.**

- biasse-fieker-classgroup-2014 — Jean-François Biasse and Claus Fieker, *Subexponential class group and unit group computation in large degree number fields*, Section 4.3, printed p. 400.

  > then certifying the result boils down to verifying that h∗⩽h′R′ < 2h∗.

  The certification step of a class group and unit computation; the node lists which intrinsic invariant each certified output determines.

### Mistakes found in the sources of this layer

- **ClassicalArithmeticCompletion/E601** (error, affects a stated result) — STEIN.ANT, Section 8.2.1, Pell's equation, printed p. 93 (the author's PDF read): printed “Dirichlet’s unit theorem implies that for any d the solutions to Pell’s equation with x, y not both negative forms an infinite cyclic group,”. Correction: The solutions with x > 0 (equivalently x + y√d > 0) form an infinite cyclic group; the set with x, y not both negative is not closed under multiplication. Reason: (−1, 0) has x, y not both negative, but (−1, 0)·(x_1, y_1) = (−x_1, −y_1) for the fundamental solution with x_1, y_1 > 0 has both negative. The solutions with x > 0 are the positive elements of the group ±ε^ℤ, a cyclic group generated by the fundamental solution.

- **ClassicalArithmeticCompletion/E602** (misprint, affects nothing) — STEIN.ANT, Example 7.3.2, Section 7.3, printed p. 84: printed “BK = √4 · (4/π)^1 · (2!/2^2) = 8/π < 3.”. Correction: B_K = √4 · (4/π) · (2!/2²) = 4/π < 2. Reason: √4 · (4/π) · (2/4) = 2 · (4/π) · (1/2) = 4/π ≈ 1.27. The conclusion that Cl(ℚ(i)) is trivial is unaffected (with the correct bound no prime is needed at all).

---

## CA.6 Special algebraic numbers and Mahler measure

This layer builds the classical theory of the algebraic numbers that sit closest to the unit circle: the
**Pisot** and **Salem numbers**, with their classification by the moduli of their conjugates, and the three
proven lower bounds that frame **Lehmer's problem**: **Smyth's theorem** for nonreciprocal algebraic integers,
**Dobrowolski's theorem** for all algebraic numbers, and **Dimitrov's theorem** settling the
**Schinzel–Zassenhaus conjecture** for the house. It closes with the comparison between the canonical height of
the power map `x ↦ x^d` on `P¹` and the absolute Weil height. By the accepted restructuring RS-03 the layer keeps
exactly this; the Mahler measure of polynomials, the absolute height, Kronecker's theorem and Northcott's theorem
are built already and are imported, and the Mahler measure of an algebraic number, the house and the identity
`log M(α) = deg α · h(α)` are the nodes of `DiophantineApproximationAndTranscendence:DT.0`.

**Coverage: partial.** 45 nodes (5 definitions, 24 lemmas, 15 theorems, 1 comparison), 42 API items, 24 unit
tests, 6 planets. Two analytic inputs have no public proof or no owner (see *Gaps*); three inputs are requested
from other roadmaps (see *Dependencies*).

The sources are C. Smyth, *The Mahler measure of algebraic numbers: a survey* (arXiv:math/0701397v2, the text of
LMS Lecture Note Series 352, pp. 322–349; printed pages cited), C. Smyth, *Seventy years of Salem numbers*
(arXiv:1408.0195v3, Bull. London Math. Soc. 47, 2015), V. Dimitrov, *A proof of the Schinzel–Zassenhaus conjecture
on polynomials* (arXiv:1912.12545v1), O. B. Berrevoets, *On Lehmer's problem* (Leiden bachelor thesis, 2016,
supervised by J.-H. Evertse), which proves Dobrowolski's house bound and Dobrowolski's theorem in full, and the
Euler Circle note of Srivastava and Gollapudi, *Pisot numbers* (2018), for the elementary Pisot facts.

### What is already built (consume; never restate)

At Mathlib 082e2d3:

- **Mahler measure of polynomials.** `Polynomial.mahlerMeasure` (the circle average; `M(0) = 0`),
  `Polynomial.mahlerMeasure_mul`, Jensen's formula `Polynomial.mahlerMeasure_eq_leadingCoeff_mul_prod_roots`
  (`M(p) = |lc p| ∏ max(1, |root|)`).
- **Kronecker.** `Polynomial.pow_eq_one_of_mahlerMeasure_eq_one` and `Polynomial.cyclotomic_mahlerMeasure_eq_one`
  (an integer polynomial of Mahler measure 1 has roots of unity as its nonzero roots, and conversely);
  `NumberField.Embeddings.pow_eq_one_of_norm_le_one` and `NumberField.Embeddings.pow_eq_one_of_norm_eq_one`
  (algebraic integers with all conjugates in the closed disc, or on the circle); `NumberField.Units.mem_torsion`.
- **Northcott.** `Polynomial.finite_mahlerMeasure_le` with the count `Polynomial.card_mahlerMeasure_le_prod`;
  `NumberField.Embeddings.finite_of_norm_le` (bounded house in a fixed field);
  `NumberField.finite_setOfPred_mulHeight₁_le` (bounded height in a fixed field).
- **House and heights.** `NumberField.house`, `NumberField.house_eq_sup'`, `NumberField.house_mul_le`,
  `NumberField.one_le_house_of_isIntegral`; the absolute heights `NumberField.absMulHeight₁` and
  `NumberField.absLogHeight₁` (junk values 1 and 0 at transcendental elements) and `Height.mulHeight₁_pow`.
- **Algebra.** `IsConjRoot` (equal minimal polynomials), `IsConjRoot.exists_algEquiv`, `Polynomial.reverse`
  with `Polynomial.eval₂_reverse_mul_pow`, `Polynomial.eval₂_reverse_eq_zero_iff`, `Polynomial.coeff_zero_reverse`;
  Gauss's lemma `minpoly.isIntegrallyClosed_eq_field_fractions'`; Newton's identities
  `MvPolynomial.psum_eq_mul_esymm_sub_sum`, `MvPolynomial.mul_esymm_eq_sum`; the Frobenius identity
  `MvPolynomial.expand_zmod`; Bertrand's postulate `Nat.exists_prime_lt_and_le_two_mul`; Minkowski's theorem in
  the Minkowski space `NumberField.mixedEmbedding.exists_ne_zero_mem_ringOfIntegers_lt`; `trace_eq_sum_roots`,
  `Algebra.isIntegral_trace`, `Algebra.norm_eq_prod_roots`, `Algebra.isIntegral_norm`; Catalan numbers `catalan`,
  `catalan_succ`; `charpoly_leftMulMatrix` and `AdjoinRoot.powerBasis'`.

From `DiophantineApproximationAndTranscendence:DT.0` (cited by node id, never re-planned): the primitive minimal
polynomial `F_α` (`DT.0/primitive-minimal-polynomial`), the **Mahler measure of an algebraic number**
`M(α) := M(F_α)` (`DT.0/mahler-measure-of-algebraic-number`, with its Kronecker and Northcott API items), the
field-independent **house** `⌈α⌉` (`DT.0/house-of-algebraic-number`, `DT.0/house-eq-numberField-house`), the
comparison **`M(α) = absMulHeight₁(α)^{deg α}`**, i.e. `log M(α) = deg α · h(α)` (`DT.0/height-comparisons`), the
field-independence of the absolute height (`DT.0/abs-mul-height-eq-rpow`, `DT.0/abs-mul-height-eq-of-minpoly-eq`)
and Northcott for the house over `Q̄` (`DT.0/finite-algebraic-integers-house-le`).

### Conventions

- **Conjugates.** The conjugates of an algebraic number `α` are the complex roots of its minimal polynomial; for an
  algebraic integer this is `minpoly ℤ α`, whose image in `ℚ[X]` is `minpoly ℚ α` (Gauss). They are simple roots.
- **Mahler measure.** `M(α)` is the Mahler measure of the *primitive* minimal polynomial and contains its leading
  coefficient: `M(1/2) = 2`. For an algebraic integer, `M(α) = ∏ max(1, |αᵢ|)`, Mathlib's `Polynomial.mahlerMeasure`
  of `minpoly ℤ α` mapped to `ℂ[X]`. For arbitrary algebraic numbers the statements below are made through
  `absMulHeight₁(α)^{deg α}`, which equals `M(α)`.
- **House.** `⌈α⌉ = max |αᵢ|` over the conjugates; for `α` in a number field `K` it is `NumberField.house α`,
  independently of `K`. The house is not `|α|`: `|(1 − √5)/2| < 1 < ⌈(1 − √5)/2⌉`.
- **Weil height.** `h(α) = NumberField.absLogHeight₁ α = log M(α)/deg α` (Smyth's (5)), the absolute logarithmic
  height; `h(∞) = 0` on `P¹`.
- **Reciprocal.** An algebraic number `α ≠ 0` is *reciprocal* when it is conjugate to `α⁻¹`, written with Mathlib's
  `IsConjRoot ℚ α α⁻¹`; a polynomial `P` of degree `d` is reciprocal when `X^d P(1/X) = ±P` (Mathlib's
  `Polynomial.reverse`). For `α ≠ 0` the two agree (`CA.6/reciprocal-iff-reverse`). With Mathlib's `0⁻¹ = 0` the
  value `0` counts as conjugate to its inverse; every statement below that concerns nonreciprocal numbers assumes
  `α ≠ 0` explicitly.
- **Pisot and Salem.** Both are predicates on real numbers. A Pisot number may have degree one (the integers
  `n ≥ 2`); the reciprocal quadratic units `(a + √(a² − 4))/2`, `a ≥ 3`, sometimes called quadratic Salem numbers,
  are Pisot numbers and are **not** Salem numbers.
- **Distance to the nearest integer.** `‖x‖ = |x − round x|`.
- **Canonical heights.** `ĥ_f` is the canonical height of `ArithmeticDynamics:DY.1`, normalised against
  `absLogHeight₁`; for the power map it is compared with `h` exactly, with no factor (unlike the elliptic
  convention with its factor `1/2`).

### Boundaries

This layer owns the special algebraic numbers and the three selected lower bounds. It does **not** own: the height
machine and the Mahler measure of algebraic numbers (`DT.0`); canonical heights of general polarised morphisms and
the zero-height criterion (`ArithmeticDynamics:DY.1`); the dynamical Lehmer problem (`ArithmeticDynamics:DY.6`,
where it stays conjectural); explicit prime-counting estimates (`AnalyticNumberTheory:AN.2`); algorithms that
isolate complex roots and so certify conjugate moduli for arbitrary input (`ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.4`, which
consumes the classification below). The other results of Smyth's survey are not targets of this roadmap: the lower
bounds of Blanksby–Montgomery, Stewart, Schinzel (totally real), Langevin, Amoroso–David, Amoroso–Dvornicich,
Bombieri–Zannier and Zhang–Zagier; Dobrowolski's constant `1/1200` and Voutier's `1/4` (the layer proves the
constant `1/11700` of the proof it follows); the equality case and the isolation of `θ₀` in Smyth's theorem;
Salem's theorem that the Pisot numbers form a closed set, Pisot's converse theorem, and Salem's construction of
Salem numbers from Pisot numbers; counting and discriminant results.

### Objects

**Pisot numbers** (`IsPisot`, node `CA.6/pisot-number`, planet *Pisot number*). A real `θ` is a Pisot number if it
is an algebraic integer, `θ > 1`, and every complex root `z ≠ θ` of `minpoly ℤ θ` has `|z| < 1`.
API: `IsPisot.isIntegral`, `IsPisot.one_lt`, `IsPisot.norm_lt_one` (projections);
`IsPisot.eq_of_one_le_norm` (`θ` is the only conjugate outside the open disc); `isPisot_iff_minpoly_rat` (the same
condition with Mathlib's `minpoly ℚ θ`); `isPisot_natCast_iff` (`IsPisot n ⟺ n ≥ 2`).
Unit tests: `IsPisot.test_goldenRatio` (`φ` is Pisot), `IsPisot.test_two` (degree one),
`IsPisot.test_not_one` (strictness of `θ > 1`), `IsPisot.test_not_threeHalves` (integrality is required),
`IsPisot.test_not_sqrtTwo` (the conjugate `−√2`), `IsPisot.test_mahlerMeasure_goldenRatio` (`M(X² − X − 1) = φ`
with Mathlib's Mahler measure).

**Salem numbers** (`IsSalem`, node `CA.6/salem-number`, planet *Salem number*). A real `τ` is a Salem number if it
is an algebraic integer, `τ > 1`, every complex root `z ≠ τ` of `minpoly ℤ τ` has `|z| ≤ 1`, and some root has
`|z| = 1`. This is Salem's form; the form "degree at least 4, conjugate to `τ⁻¹`, all other conjugates on the
circle" is the theorem `CA.6/salem-number-conjugates`.
API: `IsSalem.isIntegral`, `IsSalem.one_lt`, `IsSalem.norm_le_one`, `IsSalem.exists_norm_eq_one` (projections);
`IsSalem.not_isPisot` (the classes are disjoint); `IsSalem.isConjRoot_inv`; `IsSalem.four_le_natDegree`;
`IsSalem.even_natDegree`.
Unit tests: `IsSalem.test_quartic` (the largest real root `2.1537…` of `X⁴ − 3X³ + 3X² − 3X + 1` is Salem),
`IsSalem.test_not_two`, `IsSalem.test_not_quadraticUnit` (`(3 + √5)/2` is not Salem),
`IsSalem.test_not_goldenRatio`, `IsSalem.test_mahlerMeasure_quartic` (the Mahler measure of the quartic is its
largest real root).

**The smallest Pisot number** (`plasticNumber`, node `CA.6/plastic-number`). `θ₀ = 1.3247179…` is the unique real
root of `X³ − X − 1` (discriminant `−23`), defined as the supremum of the real roots.
API: `plasticNumber_cube` (`θ₀³ = θ₀ + 1`), `eq_plasticNumber_of_cube` (uniqueness), `plasticNumber_mem_Ioo`
(`1.3247 < θ₀ < 1.3248`), `minpoly_plasticNumber` (`X³ − X − 1`), `isPisot_plasticNumber`,
`plasticNumber_not_isConjRoot_inv` (nonreciprocal), `mahlerMeasure_plasticNumber` (`M(X³ − X − 1) = θ₀`).
Unit tests: `plasticNumber.test_lt_goldenRatio` (`θ₀ < φ`), `plasticNumber.test_goldenRatio_lt_sq` (`φ < θ₀²`),
`plasticNumber.test_neg_isRoot` (`−θ₀` is the root of `X³ − X + 1`, so that polynomial does not define `θ₀`),
`plasticNumber.test_irrational`.

**Lehmer's number** (`lehmerNumber`, node `CA.6/lehmer-number`, planet *Lehmer's number*). `τ₁₀ = 1.1762808…` is
the largest real root of Lehmer's polynomial `L = X¹⁰ + X⁹ − X⁷ − X⁶ − X⁵ − X⁴ − X³ + X + 1`. `L` is reciprocal,
`L(x) = x⁵ Q(x + 1/x)` with `Q(y) = y⁵ + y⁴ − 5y³ − 5y² + 4y + 3`; `Q` changes sign at the rationals
`−19/10, −3/2, −1, −1/2, 1/2, 1, 2, 21/10`, so its roots are four reals in `(−2, 2)` and `α₁₀ = 2.0264…`, and the
roots of `L` are `τ₁₀^{±1}` and eight points of the unit circle.
API: `lehmerNumber_isRoot`, `one_lt_lehmerNumber`, `lehmerNumber_mem_Ioo` (`1.17628 < τ₁₀ < 1.17629`),
`eq_lehmerNumber_of_isRoot` (the only root `> 1`), `inv_lehmerNumber_isRoot`, `lehmerNumber_add_inv_isRoot`
(`τ₁₀ + τ₁₀⁻¹` is a root of `Q`), `mahlerMeasure_lehmerPolynomial` (`M(L) = τ₁₀`).
Unit tests: `lehmerNumber.test_lt_plasticNumber` (a Salem number below the smallest Pisot number),
`lehmerNumber.test_add_inv_bounds` (`2 < τ₁₀ + τ₁₀⁻¹ < 2.03`), `lehmerNumber.test_not_isPisot`,
`lehmerNumber.test_inv_lt_one`.

**The root-power polynomials** (`rootPowPoly`, node `CA.6/root-power-polynomial`). For a commutative ring `R`, a
monic `P ∈ R[X]` and `m ∈ ℕ`, `P_m` is the characteristic polynomial of multiplication by `x^m` on the free module
`R[x]/(P)`; over a field in which `P = ∏(X − αᵢ)` splits, `P_m = ∏(X − αᵢ^m)`. Defining `P_m` intrinsically makes
`P_m ∈ ℤ[X]` for monic `P ∈ ℤ[X]` part of the definition.
API: `rootPowPoly_monic`, `natDegree_rootPowPoly`, `rootPowPoly_one` (`P₁ = P`), `rootPowPoly_zero`
(`P₀ = (X − 1)^n`), `map_rootPowPoly` (base change), `rootPowPoly_eq_prod_roots`, `rootPowPoly_rootPowPoly`
(`(P_m)_k = P_{mk}`), `rootPowPoly_eq_minpoly_pow` (`(minpoly K α)_m = (minpoly K α^m)^{[K(α):K(α^m)]}`),
`rootPowPoly_two_comp_X_sq` (Graeffe: `P₂(X²) = (−1)^n P(X)P(−X)`).
Unit tests: `rootPowPoly.test_X_sub_C` (`(X − a)_m = X − a^m`), `rootPowPoly.test_X_sq_sub_two`
(`(X² − 2)₂ = (X − 2)²`), `rootPowPoly.test_cyclotomic_three` (`(Φ₃)₂ = Φ₃`), `rootPowPoly.test_one`,
`rootPowPoly.test_ne_comp` (`(X² + 1)₂ = (X + 1)² ≠ X⁴ + 1`: `P_m` is not substitution).

### Pisot and Salem numbers: classification and structure

- **Classification by conjugate moduli** (`pisot_salem_classification`, `CA.6/pisot-and-salem-numbers`). For a real
  algebraic integer `τ > 1`, with `r_>` and `r_=` the numbers of roots of `minpoly ℤ τ` of modulus `> 1` and `= 1`:
  `τ` is Pisot iff `r_> = 1` and `r_= = 0`; `τ` is Salem iff `r_> = 1` and `r_= ≥ 1`; otherwise `r_> ≥ 2`.
- **Certificate** (`isPisot_of_aeval_eq_zero`, `CA.6/pisot-certificate`). A real root `θ > 1` of a monic
  `P ∈ ℤ[X]` whose other roots (one copy of `θ` removed) lie in the open unit disc is Pisot; `P` need not be
  irreducible. This is how the named examples are certified: by root products and sign changes, with no numerical
  root isolation.
- **Reciprocity** (`isConjRoot_inv_iff_reverse`, `CA.6/reciprocal-iff-reverse`). For an algebraic integer `α ≠ 0`,
  `α` is conjugate to `α⁻¹` iff `X^d P(1/X) = ±P` for `P = minpoly ℤ α`; then `P(0) = ±1`, and the minus sign
  occurs only for `P = X − 1`.
- **Salem's lemma** (`isSalem_iff_conjugates`, `CA.6/salem-number-conjugates`). `τ` is Salem iff it is an algebraic
  integer `> 1` of degree `≥ 4`, conjugate to `τ⁻¹`, all of whose conjugates other than `τ^{±1}` lie on the unit
  circle. **The minimal polynomial of a Salem number is reciprocal** with the plus sign
  (`IsSalem.reverse_minpoly`, `CA.6/salem-minpoly-reciprocal`).
- **Powers** (`IsSalem.pow`, `CA.6/salem-number-pow`; `IsPisot.pow`, `CA.6/pisot-number-pow`). `τⁿ` (`n ≥ 1`) is a
  Salem number of the same degree; `θⁿ` is a Pisot number.
- **Traces** (`isSalem_iff_add_inv`, `CA.6/salem-number-iff-trace`). For real `τ > 1`: `τ` is Salem iff
  `α = τ + τ⁻¹` is an irrational algebraic integer all of whose other conjugates are real and lie in `(−2, 2)`;
  then `ℚ(α)` is totally real of index 2 in `ℚ(τ)`. Irrationality is essential (`α = 3` gives `(3 + √5)/2`).
- **Salem numbers in a field** (`IsSalem.div_of_mem_adjoin`, `CA.6/salem-number-in-field`;
  `IsSalem.exists_pow_generator`, `CA.6/salem-numbers-in-field-powers`). A Salem number `τ′ ∈ ℚ(τ)` generates
  `ℚ(τ)`, and `τ′/τ` is Salem when `τ′ > τ`; the Salem numbers of `ℚ(τ)` are the powers of the smallest one.
- **Near-integers** (`IsSalem.exists_near_integers`, `CA.6/salem-number-near-integers`;
  `IsPisot.tendsto_dist_round`, `CA.6/pisot-number-pow-near-integer`). For a Salem `τ` and `ε > 0` some `λ > 0`
  (an algebraic integer of `ℚ(τ)`, from Minkowski's theorem) has `‖λτⁿ‖ < ε` for all `n`; for a Pisot `θ`,
  `‖θⁿ‖ ≤ (deg θ − 1)ρⁿ → 0` with `ρ < 1` the largest modulus of another conjugate, since `Tr(θⁿ) ∈ ℤ`.
- **Mahler measure** (`mahlerMeasure_eq_self_of_norm_le_one`, `CA.6/mahler-measure-of-pisot-or-salem`). For Pisot
  and Salem numbers `M(τ) = ⌈τ⌉ = τ`.
- **Reciprocal Pisot numbers** (`IsPisot.minpoly_of_isConjRoot_inv`, `CA.6/reciprocal-pisot-numbers`). They are the
  quadratic units with minimal polynomial `X² − aX + 1`, `a ≥ 3`.
- **Siegel's theorem** (`plasticNumber_le_of_isPisot`, `CA.6/plastic-number-least-pisot`). Every Pisot number is
  at least `θ₀`: reciprocal ones are `≥ (3 + √5)/2`, and for the others `θ = M(θ) ≥ θ₀` by Smyth's theorem.
- **Lehmer's number is Salem** (`isSalem_lehmerNumber`, `CA.6/lehmer-number-is-salem`), with minimal polynomial
  `L`: the trace `α₁₀` has the conjugate pattern of the trace criterion by the sign changes of `Q`, `Q` is
  irreducible because its reduction `y⁵ + y⁴ + y³ + y² + 1` is irreducible over `𝔽₂`, so `deg τ₁₀ = 10`.

### Mahler measure, house and Weil height

- **Smyth's (6)** (`house_mahlerMeasure_comparison`, `CA.6/house-mahler-measure-comparison`). For an algebraic
  integer with `r > 0` conjugates of modulus `> 1`: `M(α)^{1/d} ≤ M(α)^{1/r} ≤ ⌈α⌉ ≤ M(α)`.
- **Units** (`mahlerMeasure_le_max_house_pow`, `CA.6/mahler-measure-of-unit-le-house`). For a unit `α ≠ 0`,
  `M(α) = M(α⁻¹) ≤ max(⌈α⌉, ⌈α⁻¹⌉)^{d/2}`.
- **Smyth's (9)** (`one_add_absLogHeight₁_le_house`, `CA.6/one-add-weil-height-le-house`). For a nonzero algebraic
  integer, `⌈α⌉ ≥ exp h(α) ≥ 1 + h(α)`; so a bound `M(α) ≥ c₀ > 1` gives `⌈α⌉ ≥ 1 + log(c₀)/d`, the bridge from
  Lehmer-type to Schinzel–Zassenhaus-type bounds.
- **`M(α) < 2` forces a unit** (`isIntegral_inv_of_mahlerMeasure_lt_two`, `CA.6/unit-of-mahler-measure-lt-two`).

### Smyth's theorem

- **The integer series** (`smyth_quotient_series`, `CA.6/smyth-integer-power-series`). For monic `P ∈ ℤ[X]` with
  `P(0) = ±1` and `P* = X^d P(1/X)`, the quotient `F = P(0)P/P*` lies in `1 + Xℤ⟦X⟧` and is constant iff `P` is
  reciprocal.
- **Smyth's theorem** (`smyth_nonreciprocal`, `CA.6/smyth-nonreciprocal-lower-bound`, planet *Smyth's theorem on
  nonreciprocal algebraic integers*). *A nonzero algebraic integer `α` that is not conjugate to `α⁻¹` has
  `M(α) ≥ θ₀ = M(X³ − X − 1)`*, and the constant is attained. Proof route: a non-unit has `M(α) ≥ |P(0)| ≥ 2`;
  for a unit, `F = 1 + a_kX^k + …` with `a_k ≠ 0` is the quotient `f/g` of two functions bounded by 1 on the unit
  disc with `f(0) = g(0) = M(P)⁻¹` (`g` a Blaschke product over the roots outside the circle), and Schur's
  conditions on the coefficients of such functions, together with the integrality of the coefficients of `F`,
  give `M(P) ≥ θ₀`. The last step is the gap *The analytic core of Smyth's nonreciprocal bound has no public
  source*. Lehmer's number (`M = 1.176 < θ₀`, reciprocal) shows the hypothesis cannot be dropped.

### Dobrowolski's lower bounds

The arithmetic input is that `α` and `α^p` are close modulo `p`; the analytic input is an auxiliary polynomial from
Siegel's lemma.

- `mvPolynomial_pow_sub_expand_dvd` (`CA.6/frobenius-congruence-integer-polynomial`): `p ∣ f^p − f(X₁^p, …, Xₙ^p)`
  in `ℤ[X₁, …, Xₙ]`.
- `power_sum_modEq` (`CA.6/power-sum-frobenius-congruence`): the power sums `sₙ` of the roots of a monic integer
  polynomial are integers with `s_{np} ≡ sₙ (mod p)`.
- `multiset_eq_of_power_sums_eq` (`CA.6/multiset-eq-of-power-sums-eq`): in characteristic 0, two multisets of size
  `d` with the same power sums `p₁, …, p_d` are equal.
- `eq_zero_or_pow_eq_one_of_isConjRoot_pow` (`CA.6/conjugate-powers-root-of-unity`): if `α^k` and `α^l` are
  conjugate with `k ≠ l` positive, `α` is `0` or a root of unity.
- **Dobrowolski's house bound** (`dobrowolski_house`, `CA.6/dobrowolski-house-bound`). A nonzero algebraic integer
  of degree `d` that is not a root of unity has `⌈α⌉ > 1 + 1/(4ed²)`: with a prime `2ed < p ≤ 4ed`, the power sums
  satisfy `|s_{np} − sₙ| ≤ 2de < p`, so `α^p` is conjugate to `α`.
- `norm_aeval_minpoly_pow_prime_dvd` (`CA.6/norm-minpoly-pow-prime-dvd`): `N_{K/ℚ}(f_α(α^p))` is divisible by
  `p^{[K:ℚ]}`.
- `exists_prime_aeval_pow_ne_zero` (`CA.6/exists-prime-power-not-root`): if all powers of `α ≠ 0` have degree `d`,
  `α` is not a root of unity and `F ≠ 0` has degree `N ≥ 13d`, some prime `p ∈ (x, 2x]`, `x = 3(N/d)log(N/d)`, has
  `F(α^p) ≠ 0`; the count of primes is Rosser–Schoenfeld's `π(2x) − π(x) > 3x/(5 log x)`.
- `exists_lower_degree_mahlerMeasure_le` (`CA.6/degree-drop-mahler-measure`): if some power of the algebraic
  integer `α` has smaller degree and `M(α) > 1`, an algebraic integer `β` of smaller degree has `1 < M(β) ≤ M(α)`.
- `exists_dobrowolski_auxiliary_polynomial` (`CA.6/dobrowolski-auxiliary-polynomial`): for `M ≥ 3`, `N ≥ 10`,
  `dM < N/2`, a nonzero `F ∈ ℤ[X]` of degree `≤ N`, divisible by `f_α^M`, with coefficients at most
  `N^{2dM²/N} M(α)^{2M}`.
- **Dobrowolski's theorem** (`dobrowolski`, `CA.6/dobrowolski-lower-bound`, planet *Dobrowolski's lower bound*).
  *A nonzero algebraic number `α` of degree `d ≥ 2` that is not a root of unity has
  `M(α) ≥ 1 + (1/11700)(log log d / log d)³`.* Non-integers have `M(α) ≥ 2`; degrees `≤ 20` follow from the house
  bound; a degree drop is handled by strong induction (the bound is non-increasing for `d ≥ 21`); otherwise, with
  `M = ⌈7 log d/log log d⌉` and `N = dM²`, the norm `N(F(α^p))` is a nonzero integer divisible by `p^{dM}` and at
  most `‖F‖^d M(α)^{pN}`, which yields `log M(α) ≥ 4/(117M³)`.

### The Schinzel–Zassenhaus conjecture

- `C_four_dvd_rootPowPoly_four_sub_two` (`CA.6/root-power-polynomial-congruence`, Arnold and Smyth): `P₄ ≡ P₂ (mod 4)`
  for monic `P ∈ ℤ[X]`, from `s₄ ≡ s₂ (mod 4)` applied to all symmetric powers of `P`.
- `exists_sq_eq_one_add_four_mul_X` (`CA.6/sqrt-one-add-four-integral`): `√(1 + 4Y) = 1 + 2Σ(−1)^k C_k Y^{k+1}` has
  integer coefficients (Catalan).
- `exists_sq_eq_of_sq_add_four_mul` (`CA.6/integral-square-root-power-series`, Dimitrov's Proposition 2.2): if
  `Q(0) = 1` and `Q` is a square modulo 4 then `√Q ∈ 1 + Xℤ⟦X⟧`; in particular `√(P₂* P₄*)` has integer coefficients.
- `rootPowPoly_square_tfae` (`CA.6/root-power-polynomial-square-cyclotomic`, Dimitrov's Lemma 2.3): for `P` monic
  irreducible of degree `> 1` with `P₂` not a square, `P` is cyclotomic of odd level ⟺ `P₂ = P₄` ⟺ `P₂P₄` is a
  square in `ℤ[X]` ⟺ it is a square in `ℚ(X)`.
- **Dimitrov's theorem** (`schinzel_zassenhaus`, `CA.6/schinzel-zassenhaus-dimitrov`, planet
  *Schinzel–Zassenhaus conjecture (Dimitrov)*). *A monic irreducible non-cyclotomic `P ∈ ℤ[X]` of degree `n > 1`
  has a root of modulus at least `2^{1/(4n)}`*; so `⌈α⌉ ≥ 2^{1/(4 deg α)} = 1 + log 2/(4 deg α) + O(deg α⁻²)` for a
  nonzero algebraic integer that is not a root of unity. If every root were smaller, the integer series
  `√(P₂*(1/X)P₄*(1/X))` would continue analytically off a hedgehog of transfinite diameter `< 1` (Dubinin), hence be
  rational (Bertrandias), so `P₂P₄` would be a square: `P` is cyclotomic, or `P₂ = Q²` and induction on the degree
  applies (with the base case `deg Q = 1`, `|root| = √|b| ≥ 2^{1/8}`). The potential-theoretic inputs are the gap
  *Transfinite diameter, Dubinin's hedgehog theorem and Bertrandias's rationality theorem are planned by no
  roadmap*.

### Heights and the power map

- `absLogHeight₁_pow` (`CA.6/abs-log-height-pow`): `h(αⁿ) = n h(α)` for the absolute height, from
  `Height.mulHeight₁_pow` in `ℚ(α)` and the field-independence of `DT.0`.
- `absLogHeight₁_eq_zero_iff` (`CA.6/weil-height-eq-zero-iff`): an algebraic number has `h(α) = 0` iff it is `0` or
  a root of unity; `(3 + 4i)/5` has both conjugates on the circle and `h = (log 5)/2`.
- **The canonical height of the power map** (`tendsto_absLogHeight₁_iterate_pow`, comparison
  `CA.6/canonical-height-power-map`). For `f(x) = x^d`, `d ≥ 2`, Tate's sequence `d^{−n} h(fⁿ(α))` is constant, so
  `ĥ_f = h = log M/deg` and `ĥ_f(∞) = 0`; the points of canonical height zero are `0`, `∞` and the roots of unity
  (the preperiodic points), and the dynamical Lehmer problem for `x^d` is Lehmer's problem.

### Lehmer's problem

Lehmer asked whether `M(α)` can be arbitrarily close to 1 for algebraic `α` that are neither zero nor roots of
unity; the **Lehmer conjecture** asserts `inf {M(α) : M(α) > 1} > 1`, with the smallest known value
`M(L) = τ₁₀ = 1.17628…`. It is a conjecture. This layer states it only here: it proves the unconditional bounds
that bracket it (Smyth for nonreciprocal numbers, Dobrowolski in general, Dimitrov for the house) and no theorem
of the atlas may consume the conjecture except through an explicit hypothesis.

### Dependencies

- Inside this roadmap: none. The stage lists `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.3` and `ClassicalArithmeticCompletion:CA.5`
  as inputs; the polynomial facts used (reverses, irreducibility by reduction modulo 2, characteristic polynomials)
  are Mathlib's, and no number-field invariant of CA.5 is needed.
- `DiophantineApproximationAndTranscendence:DT.0`: the nodes named above, and a **request** for Siegel's lemma over a
  number field in product form, `max|x_j| ≤ ((2√2N)^{dM} ∏_i ∏_k max_j |σ_k(a_ij)|)^{1/(N−dM)}` (Berrevoets, Lemma
  3.2), which Dobrowolski's auxiliary polynomial needs (DT.0's house-bounded form loses the product over
  embeddings).
- `AnalyticNumberTheory:AN.2`: a **request** for Rosser–Schoenfeld's `π(2x) − π(x) > 3x/(5 log x)` for `x ≥ 41/2`
  (Illinois J. Math. 6 (1962), Corollary 3), consumed by `CA.6/exists-prime-power-not-root`.
- `ArithmeticDynamics:DY.1`: a **request** for the canonical height `ĥ_f` of a polarised endomorphism of `P¹` of
  degree `d ≥ 2`, normalised by `absLogHeight₁` with `h(∞) = 0`, with existence of Tate's limit, `ĥ_f − h`
  bounded, `ĥ_f ∘ f = d ĥ_f` and uniqueness; consumed by `CA.6/canonical-height-power-map`.
- `ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.2` (RS-03 link): no CA.6 node needs a CN.2 output, since the named classifications
  are certified by explicit root products and sign changes. General certified root isolation belongs to
  `ComputationalNumberTheory:CN.4`, which consumes `CA.6/pisot-and-salem-numbers`.

### Gaps

- **The analytic core of Smyth's nonreciprocal bound has no public source.** The survey gives the outline (the
  series `F`, its factorisation `f/g`, Schur's coefficient conditions); the argument that produces `θ₀` is in
  Smyth, Bull. London Math. Soc. 3 (1971), 169–175, which is not publicly served, and Schur's theorem on bounded
  analytic functions is in neither library. Needed by `CA.6/smyth-nonreciprocal-lower-bound` and
  `CA.6/plastic-number-least-pisot`.
- **Transfinite diameter, Dubinin's hedgehog theorem and Bertrandias's rationality theorem are planned by no
  roadmap.** Needed by `CA.6/schinzel-zassenhaus-dimitrov`. The packet's restructure list proposes giving them to
  `DiophantineApproximationAndTranscendence:DT.5` or to a small roadmap of their own.

### Acceptance tests

- **Distinguish roots of unity, algebraic integers and algebraic numbers.** `M(α) = 1` exactly for `0` and the roots
  of unity (Kronecker, imported); `(3 + 4i)/5` has house 1, is not an algebraic integer and has `h = (log 5)/2 > 0`;
  `M(1/2) = 2` includes the leading coefficient; a nonzero algebraic integer has `⌈α⌉ ≥ 1`, with equality only at
  roots of unity.
- **Certify conjugate moduli before assigning a class.** `φ`: `X² − X − 1` with the root `−1/φ`; `θ₀`: `X³ − X − 1`
  with complex roots of modulus `θ₀^{−1/2}`; Lehmer's number: sign changes of `Q` at eight rationals and irreducibility
  of `Q` modulo 2; the quartic `X⁴ − 3X³ + 3X² − 3X + 1`: its trace `(3 + √5)/2` with conjugate `(3 − √5)/2`. Each
  class assignment goes through `CA.6/pisot-certificate` or `CA.6/salem-number-iff-trace`.
- **Numerical sanity.** `θ₀ ∈ (1.3247, 1.3248)`, `τ₁₀ ∈ (1.17628, 1.17629)`, `τ₁₀ < θ₀ < φ < θ₀²`; Dobrowolski's bound
  at `d = 10` is `1.0000041 < τ₁₀`; Dimitrov's bound at `d = 10` is `2^{1/40} = 1.0175 < τ₁₀`; Dobrowolski's house
  bound at `d = 2` is `1.0230 < φ`.
- **Sharpness and necessity.** `M(X³ − X − 1) = θ₀` (Smyth's constant attained); Lehmer's reciprocal `τ₁₀` has
  `M < θ₀` (the nonreciprocal hypothesis is needed); cyclotomic polynomials have all roots on the circle (Dimitrov's
  hypothesis is needed); `3 + √2` is neither Pisot nor Salem (`r_> = 2`).
- **Power map.** `ĥ_{x²}(2) = log 2`, `ĥ_{x²}(φ) = (log φ)/2`, `ĥ_{x²}(i) = 0`, `ĥ_{x²}(τ₁₀) = (log τ₁₀)/10`.

### Source notes

Mistakes found in the sources are recorded in the packet (`ClassicalArithmeticCompletion/E701`–`E716`) and the
nodes use the corrected statements. The ones that change a hypothesis: Berrevoets's Lemma 4.6 needs `α ≠ 0` (E716)
and its justification "because `y ≥ 10`" must be "because `y ≥ 13`" (E703); his Lemma 4.7 holds as proved for
